\chapter{Safety without identification: transient amplification and a maximal pre-trade guard}\label{ch:p2}

This chapter, the second paper of the thesis, reproduces the paper \emph{From Human Markets to Machine Markets: Transient Amplification, an Exact Depth Condition, and a Maximal Pre-Trade Guard}, joint with Suboh Alkhushayni, Yuhao Chen, Mansi Bhavsar and Prashant Niroula. It establishes the safety half of the thesis: what certifies a safe trading decision when the agents cannot be identified, and what that certificate costs.

\begin{quote}\small\textbf{Summary.} Eigenvalues and volatility do not bound the losses a market produces before it settles. In a limit order book with adaptive traders, cumulative transient risk equals $S=\tilde\kappa/(m-q\tilde\kappa)$, a book overshoots if and only if $\varphi\varepsilon>(1-a)\kappa$, and a pre-trade guard on the summed impulse response is the largest safe rule and does not depend on how traders split into cohorts. $S$ is certified with $\Theta(\sigma^2\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\epsilon))$ experiments whatever the cohort spacing, and the logarithm cannot be removed; and the certified guard is minimax-optimal in lost capacity up to a logarithmic factor. In 25 pre-registered tests, 15 pass: the statistic forecasts next-hour adverse excursions in thin Binance perpetuals beyond volatility and VPIN ($+2.79$ points of $R^2$, CI $[+1.43,+4.94]$), more weakly in thin US stocks, and not on Coinbase; tests of a liquidation mechanism fail.\end{quote}

\paragraph{Reproducibility.}Every number in the paper is produced by a script or notebook cell that writes a result file from public data or from a closed form; Appendix~\ref{p2:app:repro} maps each table, figure and number to its source. Pre-registered specifications, their SHA-256 hashes and test-set unlock counters are in the replication package. Every simulation number was re-run for this version. The specifications of Parts 22--25 were committed to the repository before their test data were downloaded; because each reached the public repository in the same push as its results, that order is documented by the commit history rather than by an independent timestamp.

\section{Introduction}

On October 10, 2025, crypto derivatives markets liquidated an estimated \$19 billion of leveraged positions in a single day, the largest such event on record \citep{p2:shares2025}; on May 6, 2010, US equity prices fell and recovered by several percent within minutes \citep{p2:kirilenko2017}. In both episodes the losses that mattered were transient: positions were closed out on the path, before prices settled. This chapter is about that path risk in markets where algorithms trade with each other.

Algorithmic markets are closed-loop systems: trading moves prices, prices move algorithms, and algorithms trade. A common shortcut equates the stability of such a system with its eigenvalues lying inside the unit circle. That is the wrong object for a leveraged intermediary, whose survival depends on the path of losses before the system settles. A market can be spectrally stable and still push a dealer through its maintenance margin within seconds.

This chapter asks three questions. When does a limit order book amplify a flow shock, so that prices keep moving after the shock has passed? How can a dealer or a venue control transient loss without identifying who is trading, which Chapter~\ref{ch:p1} shows is expensive or impossible from public data? And when the traders cannot be identified, what information certifies a safe trading decision, what does that information cost, and how much safe trading must be given up when it is missing? We give exact answers in a linear book model and then confront them with data, reporting the results as they came out.

\paragraph{1. Spectral stability does not bound transient loss.} The exact safe shock radius of a stable linear market is the loss budget divided by its peak transient gain, and that gain can be arbitrarily large at any fixed spectral radius (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:radius}). Markets with identical eigenvalues, one-step volatility and stationary price variance can have arbitrarily large worst-case transient gain, as Chapter~\ref{ch:p1} shows. Supervision through eigenvalues or volatility alone is therefore insufficient in principle.

\paragraph{2. When a book amplifies.} We distinguish peak overshoot, a response that rises above its direct impact, from cumulative amplification, a summed response far above it. When order flow depletes one side of the book and the book refills at rate $r$, the price response overshoots its direct impact if and only if $\varphi\varepsilon>(1-a)\kappa$ (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak}\ref{p2:thm:t6}). With momentum traders the one-period margin $C_3=\varphi\varepsilon+\kappa^2\eta q-(1-a)\kappa$ is sufficient (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak}\ref{p2:prop:t6m}) but no one-period condition is exact (Remark~\ref{p2:rem:counter}); momentum alone cannot make a stable book overshoot (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak}\ref{p2:thm:nomom}), and an explicit condition rules overshoot out (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak}\ref{p2:thm:noover}). Cumulative risk has a closed form, $S=\tilde\kappa/(m-q\tilde\kappa)$: long-run impact divided by reversion net of momentum (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum}). It needs no depletion, and it is the form our evidence supports: crypto books show no peak overshoot, but thin books revert slowly, so their summed response is large.

\paragraph{3. The maximal safe guard.} For bounded shocks, worst-case loss over a horizon is inventory times the shock bound times the summed absolute impulse response, the classical $\ell_1$ gain of control theory. Under a declared risk contract (fixed inventory, per-period shock bound, mark-to-market loss), a pre-trade guard on this summed gain is necessary and sufficient for staying within the loss budget, so it admits the largest safe set of actions (Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard}). A guard on the peak response, the quantity closest to the eigenvalue intuition, admits unsafe actions. A Gr\"onwall bound carries the guard to an estimated operator (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robust}), a contraction-block certificate makes it computable on a finite horizon (Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:tail}), and its inputs are identified from prices without identifying the algorithms (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:invariance} and Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}). We embed the guard in a dealer's balance sheet with margin, hedging and fill accounting (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:forward}) and show that strategic re-optimization by filtering algorithms reinforces it (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:lucas}).

\paragraph{4. Information and safety.} The information that certifies a safe decision is not the information that identifies the traders. Two markets can agree exactly on their early price responses and differ threefold in risk (Example~\ref{p2:ex:witness}), and any supervisor who must rarely admit an unsafe level then gives up a computable amount of safe capacity (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:twoworld}). A finite window of responses certifies the summed response to an interval computed exactly from the Markov--Krein extremal measures, which shrinks geometrically with the window under a bound on memory length and is unbounded without one (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}). With noisy reset experiments the summed response is certified with $N=\Theta(\sigma^2\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\epsilon))$ experiments whatever the number and spacing of the traders' memories, and no procedure removes the logarithm, while identifying them costs $(\Delta\eta)^{-(4K-2)}$ or more (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}); Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}'s hardest pairs have identical summed responses. A guard built on the certificate has a closed form, admits an interval of levels, flags infeasible states, and is minimax-optimal in lost capacity up to a logarithm (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robustguard}). When admission also changes the book, the safe levels need not form an interval, so certifying the largest one no longer certifies those below it; we certify the whole safe set by interval branch and bound (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:venue}, Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:venuebb}). The window bounds are proved rather than computed (Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:certnum}), and certificates that allow correlated noise keep their level where the simple one fails (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:hotelling}).

\paragraph{5. The value of disclosure.} Disclosure that rules out the extremal compatible economy raises safe capacity, and its value grows as memory lengthens, approaching the full oracle capacity; disclosure of a noisy proxy can be worth less than nothing (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:disclosure}, Table~\ref{p2:tab:discsim}).

\paragraph{6. Who controls the risk.} Reversion is a public good. In equilibrium dealers under-provide it, momentum traders over-provide feedback, fewer dealers mean more transient risk, and the rebate that restores the first best is $(1-\theta/N)\Lambda S^2/\tilde\kappa$, a function of the guard's own statistic (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:equil}). When dealers also choose depth, fewer dealers raise risk through larger impact while the unit-free multiple $S/\tilde\kappa$ stays fixed, the pattern we see across Binance perpetuals.

\paragraph{7. Evidence.} We test the theory's empirical content in 25 pre-registered primary tests, each with a hashed specification and a test set opened once; 15 pass (Table~\ref{p2:tab:alltests}). Where the theory predicts nothing, we find nothing: BTCUSDT, the deepest book, sits on the stable side of the amplification condition on 93.5\% of days, shows no overshoot and fails a pre-registered forecasting test (Section~\ref{p2:sec:depth}); on a fixed price grid flow depletes the side it hits, and the opposite finding in Binance's depth bands is an artefact of their re-centring (Section~\ref{p2:sec:fixedgrid}). In thin books, where the amplifying regime is more frequent, six-hour estimates of the amplification margin forecast the size of the next hour's adverse excursion out of sample in every pre-registered forecasting test on thin Binance perpetuals, on new symbols and on untouched days ($+0.85$ to $+4.2$ points of $R^2$), beyond a full volatility-forecasting baseline and VPIN, and mostly through impact-weighted reversion, as Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum} implies. Within a volatility quintile, hours in the top quintile of the statistic have excursions about 26 basis points larger than hours in the bottom quintile, enough to roughly double the hourly liquidation probability of a $20\times$ position. The effect is about a fifth as large in 23 thin US stocks (Section~\ref{p2:sec:us}), negligible on Bybit and absent on Coinbase spot (Section~\ref{p2:sec:p17}).

We have not identified why. The effect does not rise with open-interest leverage (Part 19, specified in advance but not hash-registered) and does not run through open-interest liquidations (Part 22, both primaries fail). A follow-up registered after Part 22 (Part 25) finds that the statistic forecasts price persistence over the next 15 to 60 minutes, which accounts for nearly all of its forecasting gain, but its placebo criterion fails, much of that mediation is mechanical, and the statistic is itself built from slow reversion. We therefore read the evidence as showing that thin books are risky because their prices revert slowly relative to impact, which is what the statistic measures, not as a test of a deeper mechanism.

As a risk control, the certified guard lowers tail loss against a static limit by 8\% on untouched days and 23\% on untouched symbols (Sections~\ref{p2:sec:p15} and~\ref{p2:sec:p16}), but it does not beat a volatility cap at matched exposure. Combined with a volatility cap it lowers the 18-hour forced-liquidation rate of a $20\times$ position by 7.4 percentage points (Part 24, registered after a capacity test failed), mostly because it admits 11\% less exposure; scaled to the same mean exposure the two rules are indistinguishable (Section~\ref{p2:sec:p24}). Simulations with a known truth confirm the coverage, attainment and optimality claims and show where the certificate fails: when memory is longer than declared, and when feedback strengthens after certification (Section~\ref{p2:sec:simtests}).

\paragraph{What we do not claim.} The information-safety theorems concern declared observation and risk contracts (controlled reset experiments, exact windows, tagged or audited data); they are not finite-sample guarantees for observational order-book records, for which we report a bootstrap diagnostic and its out-of-sample performance. Our cohorts are any traders whose demand follows a linear filter of past prices, human or automated; nothing in the evidence attributes an effect to algorithms rather than to people. We do not claim that crypto books amplify shocks through depth: flow depletes the side it hits on a fixed price grid, but we observe no peak overshoot, and the depth term carries no forecasting content. The thin-book forecasting result is reduced-form, and its size depends on the venue: largest in thin Binance books, about a fifth as large in thin US stocks, negligible on Bybit and absent on Coinbase; it improves tail classification only against the full volatility baseline. We do not claim that the guard replaces volatility in practical position sizing: its theorems are statements about worst-case loss under a certified operator, an estimated six-hour response adds nothing to volatility, and a three-day certified estimate improves a volatility limit only modestly (2.4\%, a narrow pass) while the guard alone does worse. The October 10, 2025 episode is reported descriptively and supports no inference (Section~\ref{p2:sec:oct10}). One test cell of the first thin-book test was executed twice with unchanged code and specification (Section~\ref{p2:sec:x6}). The equilibrium is a stylized model; thinner books do carry more cumulative risk per dollar of flow, as it predicts, but through larger impact rather than through the weaker reversion it names.

\subsection{Related literature}

\paragraph{Closest work.} Three strands are closest. Models of transient impact and resilience \citep{p2:obizhaeva2013,p2:bouchaud2004,p2:bouchaud2018} take the decay of impact as given; we derive when depletion, refill and momentum make a book overshoot, give cumulative impact in closed form, and ask who supplies the reversion that bounds it. Models of liquidity spirals \citep{p2:brunnermeier2009,p2:shleifer1992} explain how funding constraints amplify shocks; our amplification is a property of book dynamics and does not need funding constraints, and our guard is the pre-trade control that bounds the resulting loss. \citet{p2:sornette2023} show that non-normal interactions create transient bubbles; we give a necessary and sufficient pre-trade control for such transients and test the conditions for them in order-book data.

\paragraph{Market microstructure and resilience.} Transient impact and resilience follow \citet{p2:obizhaeva2013}, \citet{p2:bouchaud2004} and \citet{p2:bouchaud2018}; optimal execution with impact is in \citet{p2:almgren2001} and \citet{p2:cartea2015}. Depth imbalance as a short-horizon price predictor is documented by \citet{p2:cont2014}. \citet{p2:biais2005} survey the field; price impact as a measure of information and illiquidity goes back to \citet{p2:kyle1985} and \citet{p2:amihud2002}. Inventory-based dealer models go back to \citet{p2:ho1981}, and \citet{p2:avellaneda2008} give the standard high-frequency market-making model. Empirically, resiliency, the speed at which a book refills after a liquidity shock, is measured by \citet{p2:biais1995}, \citet{p2:degryse2005} and \citet{p2:large2007}, who estimates it with a self-exciting point process for order events; \citet{p2:foucault2005} show that it is set by the mix of patient and impatient traders and the tick size. Our reversion term $m$ is the reduced-form counterpart of resiliency, and $S=\tilde\kappa/(m-q\tilde\kappa)$ shows how it combines with impact and momentum: a book that is resilient in this sense can still carry a large cumulative response when momentum feedback absorbs most of its reversion. Our book model is a minimal two-state version of resilience dynamics, chosen so that the amplification condition is exact.

\paragraph{Feedback, fire sales and automated markets.} Liquidity spirals and fire sales are in \citet{p2:shleifer1992} and \citet{p2:brunnermeier2009}; automated-market crashes in \citet{p2:kirilenko2017}; and the market-design case for discrete auctions in \citet{p2:budish2015}. \citet{p2:menkveld2013} documents high-frequency market makers as the new intermediaries, and algorithmic trading has been found to improve liquidity and price discovery in normal times \citep{p2:hendershott2011,p2:brogaard2014}; our question is what happens on the path after a shock. \citet{p2:sornette2023} show that non-normal interactions can generate transient bubbles in unleveraged systems. We add an exact condition for when a book amplifies, a control that does not need the identity of the traders, and evidence on whether the condition holds in a large market.

\paragraph{Early warning and liquidity crashes.} The best-known early-warning metric for flow-driven crashes is VPIN \citep{p2:easley2012}, whose forecasting content beyond volume and volatility is disputed \citep{p2:andersen2014}. Equilibrium models explain crashes through endogenous liquidity withdrawal \citep{p2:huang2009} and illiquidity contagion across assets \citep{p2:cespa2014}. Our statistic differs in kind: it is a structural quantity, the summed response of the book to a unit of flow, with a closed form and a control-theoretic meaning, and we evaluate it out of sample against a full volatility-forecasting baseline. In a post-hoc comparison on the same test hours, $\hat C_3$ keeps about 73\% of its gain once VPIN is added to the baseline (Section~\ref{p2:sec:z9}). Crypto markets are studied as a laboratory for microstructure and arbitrage by \citet{p2:makarov2020}.

\paragraph{Non-normal dynamics and robust control.} Transient growth of stable non-normal systems and the Kreiss matrix theorem are classical \citep{p2:trefethen2005,p2:kreiss1962}; the sharp $en$ constant is due to \citet{p2:spijker1991}. Standard $H_\infty$ control bounds frequency-domain gain under known coordinates. The time-domain worst case under bounded disturbances, the $\ell_1$ norm of the impulse response, is the object of $\ell_1$-optimal control \citep{p2:vidyasagar1986,p2:dahleh1987,p2:dahleh1995}, and our guard's worst-case identity is that classical fact. What we add is its use as an admission rule on a dealer's balance sheet, its invariance to the unobservable cohort coordinates and identification from prices, and its link to who supplies reversion in equilibrium. Cross-impact under no dynamic arbitrage is studied by \citet{p2:schneider2019}.

\paragraph{Regulation.} SEC Rule 15c3-5 requires broker-dealers with market access to enforce pre-trade controls on credit and capital \citep{p2:sec2010}. Our guard is a candidate pre-trade control with a provable maximality property.

\paragraph{Forecast evaluation.} Our predictive tests use \citet{p2:clark2007} and \citet{p2:diebold1995} with \citet{p2:newey1987} standard errors and block resampling in the spirit of \citet{p2:kunsch1989}, the volatility baseline follows \citet{p2:corsi2009} and \citet{p2:riskmetrics1996}, local projections follow \citet{p2:jorda2005}, and we correct for multiple testing as in \citet{p2:holm1979}. Following the case for pre-registration in science \citep{p2:nosek2018} and the concerns about multiple testing in financial economics \citep{p2:harvey2016,p2:harvey2017}, we pre-register specifications, count every evaluation of a held-out period, and report the failures with the successes.

\subsection{Outline}
Section~\ref{p2:sec:model} sets out the closed-loop market. Section~\ref{p2:sec:radius} shows why spectral stability is not solvency. Section~\ref{p2:sec:t6} derives the amplification condition. Section~\ref{p2:sec:guard} constructs and analyzes the guard, and Section~\ref{p2:sec:equil} asks who supplies reversion in equilibrium. Sections~\ref{p2:sec:depth} and~\ref{p2:sec:spot} report the BTCUSDT evidence, and Section~\ref{p2:sec:thin} the thin-book program, its replications and its failures. Section~\ref{p2:sec:policy} discusses policy, and Section~\ref{p2:sec:conclusion} concludes.
\section{The closed-loop market}\label{p2:sec:model}

\subsection{Price formation}
Time is discrete with bars of length $\Delta$. Let $p_t$ be the price, $p^*_t=p^*_{t-1}+\xi_t$ the unobserved fundamental and $x_t=p_t-p^*_t$ the pricing error. Competitive dealers post aggregate depth $D>0$, so price impact is $\kappa=1/D$, and fundamental arbitrage removes a fraction $\kappa\varphi_f$ of the error each period. With algorithmic demand $u_t$ and liquidity noise, the pricing error follows
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:price}
x_{t+1}=ax_t+\kappa u_t+w_{t+1},\qquad a=1-\kappa\varphi_f\in[0,1).
\end{equation}
Algorithmic cohorts trade $u_t=\sum_iq_iy_{i,t}$ with exponential memory $y_{i,t+1}=(1-\eta_i)y_{i,t}+\eta_ix_{t+1}$, a reduced form that arises when an adaptive desk updates its belief by steady-state Kalman filtering or regularized online gradient descent and maximizes expected continuation return $\beta_i y_{i,t}u_{i,t}$ minus quadratic execution cost $\tfrac12\lambda_{{\rm tc},i}u_{i,t}^2$ and inventory penalty $\tfrac12\gamma_{{\rm inv},i}I_{i,t}^2$ ($I_{i,t}=(1-\delta_{\rm inv})I_{i,t-1}+u_{i,t}$, $|I_{i,t}|\le\bar I_i$), yielding $u_{i,t}^*=q_iy_{i,t}-\psi_{{\rm inv},i}I_{i,t-1}$ with $q_i=\beta_i/(\lambda_{{\rm tc},i}+\gamma_{{\rm inv},i})$ and $\psi_{{\rm inv},i}=\gamma_{{\rm inv},i}(1-\delta_{\rm inv})/(\lambda_{{\rm tc},i}+\gamma_{{\rm inv},i})$ (Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}; this single-agent filtering-and-control rule is distinct from the joint dealer--trader equilibrium of Section~\ref{p2:sec:equil}). The baseline equations set $\psi_{{\rm inv},i}=0$, the limit in which inventory holding penalties are small relative to execution costs ($\gamma_{{\rm inv},i}\ll\lambda_{{\rm tc},i}$) or inventory is reset at the bar horizon; Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:inventory_S} below relaxes this and gives the closed-form summed response when cohorts carry inventory across bars with $\psi_{{\rm inv},i}>0$. Stacking $z_t=(x_t,y_{1,t},\ldots,y_{K,t})^\top$ gives $z_{t+1}=Jz_t+bw_{t+1}$, where $b=(1,\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_K)^\top$ because cohorts update on the same period's price (a unit of flow enters through $\kappa b$), and
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:J}
J=\begin{pmatrix} a & \kappa q_1 & \cdots & \kappa q_K\\ \eta_1 a & 1-\eta_1+\eta_1\kappa q_1 & \cdots & \eta_1\kappa q_K\\ \vdots & & \ddots & \vdots\\ \eta_K a & \eta_K\kappa q_1 & \cdots & 1-\eta_K+\eta_K\kappa q_K\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Stability and the role of depth}

\begin{proposition}[Stability with one cohort]\label{p2:prop:jury}
With one cohort $(q,\eta)$ and $a=1-\kappa\varphi_f$, the market is Schur stable if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:jury}
q<\varphi_f,\qquad \kappa<\frac{4-2\eta}{(2-\eta)\varphi_f-\eta q}\ \ \text{when}\ (2-\eta)\varphi_f>\eta q,\qquad |1-\kappa\varphi_f|(1-\eta)<1 .
\end{equation}
Higher impact $\kappa$ (thinner depth) lowers price persistence $a$; feedback is destabilizing only when cohort aggressiveness $q$ reaches the arbitrage strength $\varphi_f$.
\end{proposition}

The condition $q<\varphi_f$ is the familiar statement that momentum demand must be weaker than value arbitrage. The upper bound on $\kappa$ rules out oscillatory instability when the book is very thin.

\subsection{What an observer can use}

Chapter~\ref{ch:p1} shows that prices identify only the merged rate--weight measure $M=\sum_iq_i\eta_i\delta_{1-\eta_i}$ of the cohorts. A pre-trade control must therefore depend on the market only through objects that are the same for every cohort representation of $M$.

\begin{proposition}[Input--output invariance]\label{p2:prop:invariance}
Two populations with the same merged measure generate the same transfer function $H(z)=\sum_kg_kz^{-k}=\kappa z/[z-a-\kappa G(z)]$, $G(z)=\sum_iq_i\eta_iz/(z-1+\eta_i)$, and the same impulse coefficients $g_k=\kappa\,c_{\rm price}^\top J^kb$ for every $k\ge0$. Under any invertible change of state coordinates $z\mapsto Tz$, the Euclidean gain $\|CT^{-1}(TJT^{-1})^k\|_2$ depends on $T$, whereas the price path
\[
\Delta p_{t+k}=\sum_{j\ge1}g_{k-1+j}u_{t-j}+\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}g_{k-1-m}u_{t+m}
\]
and its worst case over $\|u\|_\infty\le\bar u$ depend only on $\{g_k\}$ and are invariant to both $T$ and cohort splitting.
\end{proposition}

Numerically, splitting a cohort of 1{,}000 random economies into two with the same memory rate leaves every impulse coefficient unchanged up to rounding ($\max_k|g_k(\mathcal E^A)-g_k(\mathcal E^B)|\le2.5\times10^{-12}$ over 200 lags; \texttt{recompute\_oct20.py}). Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:invariance} is the reason we build the guard on impulse coefficients and avoid state norms in latent coordinates. When a state norm is needed, we fix units by observable calm-market innovation standard deviations, which is what makes the spot statistic of Section~\ref{p2:sec:spot} unit-free.

\begin{proposition}[Summed response with carried cohort inventory]\label{p2:prop:inventory_S}
Suppose each cohort $i$ carries inventory $I_{i,t}=(1-\delta_{\rm inv})I_{i,t-1}+u_{i,t}$ with $\delta_{\rm inv}\in(0,1]$ and submits order flow $u_{i,t}=q_i y_{i,t}-\psi_i I_{i,t-1}$ with $q_i\ge0$ and $\psi_i\ge0$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The closed-loop transfer function from a flow impulse to price is $H_{\rm inv}(z)=\kappa z/\bigl[z-a-\kappa G_{\rm inv}(z)\bigr]$ with
\[
G_{\rm inv}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^K \frac{q_i\eta_i z(z-1+\delta_{\rm inv})}{(z-1+\eta_i)(z-1+\delta_{\rm inv}+\psi_i)},
\]
which is invariant to splitting any cohort that shares $(\eta_i,\psi_i)$. Whenever the augmented state matrix is Schur stable, the signed cumulative price response is
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:Sinv}
S(\psi)=\sum_{t=0}^\infty h_t(\psi)=H_{\rm inv}(1)=\frac{\kappa}{1-a-\kappa\Phi_{\rm eff}(\psi)},\qquad \Phi_{\rm eff}(\psi)\equiv\sum_{i=1}^K\frac{\delta_{\rm inv}}{\delta_{\rm inv}+\psi_i}\,q_i\le\Phi.
\end{equation}
\item If $0\le\psi_i\le 1-\delta_{\rm inv}$ and $\delta_{\rm inv}\ge\eta_i$ for each $i$ and $a+\kappa\Phi_{\rm eff}(\psi)<1$, every open-loop feedback tap $k_m(\psi)$ and hence every closed-loop impulse coefficient $h_t(\psi)$ is nonnegative, so the worst-case $\ell_1$ gain equals the signed sum, $\|h(\psi)\|_1=\sum_{t=0}^\infty |h_t(\psi)|=S(\psi)$. When $\psi_i$ is large enough that inventory payback reverses the sign of late taps, $\|h(\psi)\|_1>S(\psi)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

Inventory mean-reversion $\psi_i>0$ acts as an automatic stabilizer that attenuates each cohort's long-run feedback weight from $q_i$ to $\frac{\delta_{\rm inv}}{\delta_{\rm inv}+\psi_i}q_i$ (across 500 random stable economies \eqref{p2:eq:Sinv} matches the simulated state-space impulse sum to $1.78\times10^{-15}$, and in the monotone regime $\|h(\psi)\|_1=S(\psi)$ to $1.78\times10^{-15}$, reducing $S$ from $2.7778$ at $\psi=0$ to $2.5000$ at the calibrated worked example; in the sign-reversal regime $\|h(\psi)\|_1=2.0960>S(\psi)=1.7241$; \texttt{recompute\_p2\_step2\_theory.py}).

\begin{proposition}[Quote cost of ambiguity]\label{p2:prop:ambig}
For a one-unit ticket with adverse mark $Z_\theta\sim N(\mu_\theta,\sigma_\theta^2)$ and a CARA dealer with risk aversion $\gamma$, let $c_\theta=\mu_\theta+\gamma\sigma_\theta^2/2$. Under competition with zero robust certainty equivalent, the informed quote is $c_{\theta_0}$ and the ambiguous quote $\sup_\theta c_\theta$; with inverse demand $(V_0-V)/b$ and marginal cost $c$, raising an interior quote from $s_0$ by $\Delta$ costs $b(s_0-c)\Delta+\tfrac b2\Delta^2$ in surplus.
\end{proposition}

Ambiguity about the algorithms is costly only when it changes the worst relevant cost. A guard that bounds worst-case transient exposure can therefore narrow the ambiguity premium; whether it does in a real market is an empirical question we do not answer here.

\subsection{Discussion of the assumptions}

\paragraph{Linearity and small shocks.} All dynamics are linear. This is the right approximation for the transient response to flow shocks of ordinary size, which is what a pre-trade control must bound one step ahead; it is not a model of regime changes such as exchange outages, where nonlinear liquidity withdrawal dominates.

\paragraph{One book, one asset.} Sections~\ref{p2:sec:t6}--\ref{p2:sec:guard} treat a single instrument; Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:multi} shows how directed cross-impact enlarges transient gains in a network. Cross-margined portfolios need the multi-asset operator, which we leave to future work.

\paragraph{Bounded shocks.} The guard bounds losses for shocks in a declared envelope. Choosing the envelope is a risk-management decision; a larger envelope trades admitted flow for safety. The theorems hold for any envelope, and the empirical work does not depend on it.

\paragraph{Observables.} Every input to the guard (impulse coefficients, an operator estimate and its confidence radius, inventory and margin) is observable to a dealer or venue. Nothing requires the identities or memory parameters of the algorithms.

\section{Spectral stability is not solvency}\label{p2:sec:radius}

\begin{theorem}[Safe radius, Kreiss bounds and the failure of the spectral radius]\label{p2:thm:radius}
Let $z_t=J^tz_0$ with $\rho(J)<1$, loss budget $B>0$ and loss output $C$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The largest initial-state radius with $\sup_t\|CJ^tz_0\|_2\le B$ for every $\|z_0\|_2\le r$ is
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:rexact}
r^*_{\rm exact}=\frac{B}{M_C},\qquad M_C\equiv\sup_{t\ge0}\|CJ^t\|_2 .
\end{equation}
\item With $C=I$ and the Kreiss constant $\mathcal K(J)=\sup_{|z|>1}(|z|-1)\|(zI-J)^{-1}\|_2$, the Kreiss matrix theorem gives $\mathcal K(J)\le\sup_t\|J^t\|_2\le e\,n\,\mathcal K(J)$ for $J\in\R^{n\times n}$, hence
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:kreiss}
\frac{B}{e\,n\,\mathcal K(J)}\;\le\;r^*_{\rm exact}\;\le\;\frac{B}{\mathcal K(J)} .
\end{equation}
\item For any $\rho\in(0,1)$ and any $B>0$ there is a family $J_\theta$ with $\rho(J_\theta)=\rho$ for all $\theta$ and an output $C=(1,0)$ such that $r^*_{\rm exact}(J_\theta)\to0$ as $\theta\to\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

Table~\ref{p2:tab:kreiss} illustrates the theorem for $J_\theta=\begin{psmallmatrix}0.8&\theta\\0&0.4\end{psmallmatrix}$: the spectral radius is $0.8$ throughout, while the price-row gain rises from 1 to 120 and the safe radius falls by the same factor. The Kreiss constant tracks the gain within the factor $en$.

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Safe radius and Kreiss bounds at a fixed spectral radius}\label{p2:tab:kreiss}
\begin{tabular}{rccccc}
\toprule
$\theta$ & $\rho(J_\theta)$ & $M_C$ (price row) & $\sup_t\|J_\theta^t\|$ & $\mathcal K(J_\theta)$ & $r^*_{\rm exact}$ ($B=1$)\\
\midrule
0 & 0.8 & 1.00 & 1.00 & 1.00 & 1.000\\
1 & 0.8 & 1.36 & 1.37 & 1.06 & 0.735\\
5 & 0.8 & 6.03 & 6.04 & 3.43 & 0.166\\
20 & 0.8 & 24.01 & 24.01 & 13.42 & 0.042\\
100 & 0.8 & 120.00 & 120.00 & 66.99 & 0.008\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item $J_\theta=\begin{psmallmatrix}0.8&\theta\\0&0.4\end{psmallmatrix}$; Kreiss constant by grid search over $|z|>1$. \end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The theorem concerns a specified initial-state ball and output norm. Changing state coordinates changes the disturbance set, so latent-state units cannot be read as monetary losses; this is why Section~\ref{p2:sec:guard} works with input--output gains instead.

\paragraph{Spectral blindness.} Chapter~\ref{ch:p1} shows more: for any eigenvalues $0<\lambda_2<\lambda_1<1$, one-step price variance and sufficiently large stationary price variance, there are two-state markets sharing all three quantities whose worst-case transient gain is arbitrarily large (Chapter~ef{ch:p1}, spectral-blindness theorem), so neither eigenvalue nor volatility monitors bound transient loss.

At $\lambda=(0.8,0.7)$ with one-step variance 1 and stationary variance 5, the gain is $1.77$, $5.10$, $16.95$, $50.85$ and $169.50$ as the off-diagonal coupling rises from 1 to 100. Figure~\ref{p2:fig:pseudo} shows the geometry for a constructed matrix with spectral radius $0.5$: its pseudospectra cross the unit circle, and the price-row gain grows with the coupling before decaying.

\begin{figure}[t]\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{p2figs/fig_pseudo.pdf}
\caption{Left: $\varepsilon$-pseudospectra of $J=\begin{psmallmatrix}0.5&5\\0&0.4\end{psmallmatrix}$; the eigenvalues (dots) are well inside the unit circle (dotted), but the $\varepsilon=10^{-1}$ contour reaches beyond it. Right: price-row gain $\|e_1^\top J^k\|$ for couplings 1, 5 and 20.}\label{p2:fig:pseudo}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{No amplification without depth.} In the cohort economy \eqref{p2:eq:J} with nonnegative weights and $\rho(J)<1$, the price impulse response is a nonnegative mixture of decaying exponentials with total weight $\kappa$; it is completely monotone and peaks at the direct impact (Chapter~ef{ch:p1}, positive-geometry lemma).

This is a negative result with a positive use. The cohort matrix \eqref{p2:eq:J} is non-normal, yet its price output never amplifies. Non-normality is necessary for price amplification but not sufficient, and adaptive momentum feedback alone does not supply it. Section~\ref{p2:sec:t6} shows that depth depletion does.

\subsection{Multi-asset coupling}

Market makers trade many correlated instruments. Let $x_t\in\R^d$ be pricing errors and $u_t\in\R^d$ flows, with $x_{t+1}=Ax_t+Ku_t+w_{t+1}$, $A={\rm diag}(a_1,\ldots,a_d)$, a cross-impact matrix $K$, and momentum flow $y_{t+1}=(I-H)y_t+Hx_{t+1}$, $u_t=Qy_t$. The stacked Jacobian is
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:Jmulti}
J_{\rm multi}=\begin{pmatrix}A&KQ\\ HA&I-H+HKQ\end{pmatrix}\in\R^{2d\times2d}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Directed cross-impact shrinks the safe radius]\label{p2:prop:multi}
Suppose a leader--satellite structure ($K_{12}=0$, $K_{21}\ge0$) with $A,Q,K\ge0$ entrywise, $H\in(0,1)^d$, stable diagonal blocks and a fixed output $C\ge0$. Let $J_{\rm diag}$ be the decoupled Jacobian with $K_{21}=0$. Then every power $J_{\rm multi}^t$ is entrywise non-decreasing in $K_{21}$, so $M_C(J_{\rm multi})\ge M_C(J_{\rm diag})$ and $r^*_{\rm exact}(J_{\rm multi})\le r^*_{\rm exact}(J_{\rm diag})$; likewise $\mathcal K(J_{\rm multi})\ge\mathcal K(J_{\rm diag})$.
\end{proposition}

The proposition concerns nonnegative downstream coupling under a fixed output and shock set. Hedged or signed monetary outputs need separate treatment, and the size of the effect requires an empirical calibration we do not provide. Persistence and transient amplification are nevertheless different things: in a network of identical cohort economies coupled by symmetric nonnegative cross-impact, which stays symmetric in suitable coordinates, cross-impact of $0.05$ raises the spectral radius from $0.822$ ($d=1$) to $0.973$ ($d=10$) while the Kreiss constant stays at one (\texttt{recompute\_oct20.py}). Directed coupling, as in Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:multi}, is what turns persistence into amplification.
\section{When does a book amplify? An exact condition}\label{p2:sec:t6}

Transient path risk takes two forms, and the distinction organizes both the theory and the evidence. The first is \emph{peak overshoot}, a response that rises above its direct impact; Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak} shows that it requires depth depletion to outweigh impact-weighted reversion. The second is \emph{cumulative amplification}, a summed response $S$ much larger than direct impact; Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum} shows that it arises without any depletion whenever reversion is slow relative to impact and momentum. The guard of Section~\ref{p2:sec:guard} bounds the second. The data of Sections~\ref{p2:sec:depth}--\ref{p2:sec:thin} show no peak overshoot in crypto books, even though flow depletes the side it hits on a fixed price grid, and they support the second form in thin books.

\subsection{A book with depletion and refill}
Let $x_t$ be the price displacement, $d_t$ the signed depth imbalance (bid minus ask depth near the mid, relative to total) and $u_t$ signed order flow. Flow moves the price directly and depletes one side of the book, which refills at rate $r$; depth imbalance moves the price:
\begin{align}
x_t &= a\,x_{t-1} + \varphi\,d_{t-1} + \kappa\,u_t, \label{p2:eq:x}\\
d_t &= (1-r)\,d_{t-1} + \varepsilon\,u_t, \label{p2:eq:d}
\end{align}
with $\kappa>0$, $a\in[0,1)$ and $r\in(0,1]$. Write $b=1-r$. With the sign convention $d=(B-A)/(B+A)$, a buy that consumes ask depth raises $d$, so $\varepsilon>0$ means \emph{depletion} of the side that is hit, and $\varphi>0$ means that a bid-heavy book pushes the price up. The impulse response to a unit flow shock at $t=0$ is $h_0=\kappa$ and
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:rec}
h_k=a\,h_{k-1}+\varphi\varepsilon\,b^{k-1},\qquad k\ge1.
\end{equation}

\begin{definition}
The book \emph{amplifies} if $\max_kh_k>h_0$: the price keeps moving in the direction of the shock after the shock has passed.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Peak overshoot}

Add momentum traders whose flow $f_t=(1-\eta)f_{t-1}+\eta q\,x_{t-1}$ enters both \eqref{p2:eq:x} and \eqref{p2:eq:d} alongside $u_t$; the two-state model is the case $q=0$. Write $C_2\equiv\varphi\varepsilon-(1-a)\kappa$ and $C_3\equiv\varphi\varepsilon+\kappa^2\eta q-(1-a)\kappa$.

\begin{theorem}[Peak overshoot: depletion versus momentum]\label{p2:thm:peak}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item\label{p2:thm:t6} \emph{Two-state condition.} Without momentum ($q=0$) the book amplifies if and only if $C_2>0$; when $C_2\le0$ the response peaks at impact, $\max_kh_k=\kappa$.
\item\label{p2:prop:shape} \emph{Shape.} Let $q=0$, $0<a<1$ and $0<b<1$. If $a\neq b$, $h_k=Aa^k+Bb^k$ with $B=\varphi\varepsilon/(b-a)$ and $A=\kappa-B$; if $a=b$, $h_k=a^{k-1}(a\kappa+\varphi\varepsilon k)$ for $k\ge1$. When the book amplifies, $h$ viewed as a function of real $k\ge0$ has a single maximum, at
\[
k^*=\frac{\log\bigl(-A\log a/(B\log b)\bigr)}{\log(b/a)}\quad(a\neq b),\qquad k^*=\frac1{\log(1/a)}-\frac{a\kappa}{\varphi\varepsilon}\quad(a=b),
\]
the second being the limit of the first as $b\to a$; the peak lag is $\lfloor k^*\rfloor$ or $\lceil k^*\rceil$ (and is $1$ when $k^*<1$, because $h_1>h_0$). The cumulative response is $\sum_kh_k=\kappa/(1-a)+\varphi\varepsilon/[(1-a)r]$ in all cases, and faster refill lowers both the peak and the cumulative response. When $a=0$ ($h_k=\varphi\varepsilon b^{k-1}$) or $r=1$ ($h_k=a^{k-1}(a\kappa+\varphi\varepsilon)$) the response peaks at lag 1.
\item\label{p2:prop:t6m} \emph{One-period margin with momentum.} $h_1-h_0=C_3$, so $C_3>0$ implies that the book amplifies.
\item\label{p2:thm:nomom} \emph{Momentum alone cannot amplify.} If $\varphi\varepsilon=0$, $q\ge0$ and the system is stable, then $h_k=c_1\lambda_1^k+c_2\lambda_2^k$ with residues $c_1,c_2\ge0$, $c_1+c_2=\kappa$ and $|\lambda_2|\le\lambda_1=\rho<1$, so $h_k\le\kappa\rho^k<\kappa$ for every $k\ge1$; in particular $C_3<0$.
\item\label{p2:thm:noover} \emph{A sufficient condition for no overshoot.} For any signs of $\varphi$, $\varepsilon$ and $q$, if
\[
\bar C_3\equiv|\varphi\varepsilon|\Bigl(1+\frac{\kappa|q|}{r}\Bigr)+\kappa^2|q|-(1-a)\kappa\;\le\;0,
\]
then $\sup_k|h_k|=\kappa$. When $\varphi\varepsilon\ge0$ and $q\ge0$, $C_3>0\Rightarrow$ amplification $\Rightarrow\bar C_3>0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i) \emph{If.} $h_1-h_0=a\kappa+\varphi\varepsilon-\kappa=C_2>0$. \emph{Only if.} Suppose $C_2\le0$. If $\varphi\varepsilon<0$, then by \eqref{p2:eq:rec} $h_k=a^k\kappa+\varphi\varepsilon\sum_{j<k}a^{k-1-j}b^j\le a^k\kappa\le\kappa$. If $\varphi\varepsilon\ge0$, let $\Delta_k=h_k-h_{k-1}$. Then $\Delta_1=C_2\le0$ and, from \eqref{p2:eq:rec}, $\Delta_{k+1}=a\Delta_k+\varphi\varepsilon(b^{k}-b^{k-1})=a\Delta_k-\varphi\varepsilon\,r\,b^{k-1}\le a\Delta_k$, so by induction $\Delta_k\le0$ for all $k$ and $h$ peaks at $h_0=\kappa$.

(ii) Solving \eqref{p2:eq:rec} gives the two forms. For $a\ne b$, $\frac{d}{dk}h=Aa^k\log a+Bb^k\log b$ has at most one zero because $(b/a)^k$ is monotone, which gives $k^*$; for $a=b$, $\frac{d}{dk}a^{k-1}(a\kappa+\varphi\varepsilon k)=a^{k-1}[(a\kappa+\varphi\varepsilon k)\log a+\varphi\varepsilon]$ vanishes once, at the stated $k^*$, and expanding the first formula in $b-a$ gives the same limit. A unimodal function of real $k$ attains its integer maximum at $\lfloor k^*\rfloor$ or $\lceil k^*\rceil$. Summing the geometric series gives the cumulative response, which falls in $r$; the peak falls in $r$ because $h_k$ is decreasing in $r$ term by term for $\varphi\varepsilon>0$.

(iii) With a unit shock at $t=0$, $x_0=\kappa$, $d_0=\varepsilon$ and $f_1=\eta q\kappa$, so $h_1=a\kappa+\varphi\varepsilon+\kappa^2\eta q$.

(iv) Without the depth channel the state $(x_t,f_t)$ evolves by $M=\begin{psmallmatrix}a+\kappa\eta q&\kappa(1-\eta)\\ \eta q&1-\eta\end{psmallmatrix}$, which is entrywise nonnegative, and $h_k=\kappa(M^k)_{11}$. The off-diagonal product is nonnegative, so the eigenvalues are real, and by the Perron--Frobenius theorem $\lambda_2\le m_{11}\le\lambda_1=\rho$. Writing $(M^k)_{11}=c_1'\lambda_1^k+c_2'\lambda_2^k$ with $c_1'=(m_{11}-\lambda_2)/(\lambda_1-\lambda_2)\in[0,1]$ and $c_2'=1-c_1'$ gives the residues $c_j=\kappa c_j'\ge0$ and $(M^k)_{11}\le\rho^k$. Since $m_{11}\le\rho<1$, $C_3=\kappa(m_{11}-1)<0$.

(v) Suppose $|x_j|\le\kappa$ for $j<k$. Then $|f_j|\le|q|\kappa$ for $j\le k$ and $|d_{k-1}|\le|\varepsilon|\bigl(b^{k-1}+\sum_{j\ge1}b^{k-1-j}|q|\kappa\bigr)\le|\varepsilon|(1+|q|\kappa/r)$, so $|x_k|\le a\kappa+|\varphi\varepsilon|(1+\kappa|q|/r)+\kappa^2|q|\le\kappa$. Induction from $x_0=\kappa$ completes the proof.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Interpretation.} The left side $\varphi\varepsilon$ of $C_2$ is the extra price pressure that a unit of flow creates one period later through the book it leaves behind. The right side $(1-a)\kappa$ is the part of the direct impact that reverts in one period. The book amplifies exactly when the depth channel adds more than mean reversion removes. Refill $r$ does not enter the condition; it governs how long an overshoot lasts.

We verify part~\ref{p2:thm:t6} in 1,995 random parameter draws and the refill comparative static in 565 amplifying draws, with no exceptions. The peak lag lies in the stated bracket in all of 150{,}039 amplifying draws with $a\ne b$ and all of 20{,}000 confluent draws; it is not always the integer nearest to $k^*$, which an earlier version claimed and which fails in 15\% of draws (\texttt{recompute\_audit.py}, \texttt{hardening/p2\_sec4\_checks.py}). Figure~\ref{p2:fig:t6} illustrates the four cases.

\begin{figure}[t]\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.6\textwidth]{p2figs/fig_t6.pdf}
\caption{Illustrative impulse responses of model \eqref{p2:eq:x}--\eqref{p2:eq:d} ($a=0.9$, $\kappa=1$, $\varepsilon=0.1$). The response overshoots only when $\varphi\varepsilon>(1-a)\kappa$; faster refill $r$ lowers the overshoot. Parameters are illustrative, not estimates.}\label{p2:fig:t6}
\end{figure}

With two slow modes the converse of part~\ref{p2:prop:t6m} fails, and no condition on the one-period margin is exact.

\begin{remark}[Counterexamples to an exact one-period condition]\label{p2:rem:counter}
Between $C_3$ and $\bar C_3$ the sign of $C_3$ does not decide. With $\kappa=1$, $a=0.98526$, $\varphi=0.7409$, $\varepsilon=0.01183$, $r=0.03436$, $\eta=0.5092$ and $q=0.01127$ (all channels reinforcing, $\rho=0.9995$), $C_3=-0.00024$ but the response peaks at lag 60 at $1.092\kappa$. With replenishment ($\varphi=2.614$, $\varepsilon=-0.405$, $a=0.9426$, $r=0.033$, $\eta=0.0016$, $q=0.0785$, $\rho=0.998$), $C_3=-1.11$ yet the response oscillates to $9.2\kappa$ at lag 90. In a search over 400{,}000 parameter draws such cases occur only near the stability boundary or with slow refill and slow momentum.
\end{remark}

The spectral reason is visible in the first example. Without depth, part~\ref{p2:thm:nomom} makes every residue nonnegative, so the response is a nonnegative mixture of decaying modes and cannot rise above $\kappa$; we found no negative residue in 10{,}694 stable momentum-only draws. The depth channel breaks this: the three-state response is $h_k=1.145\,(0.9995)^k-0.155\,(0.9626)^k+0.011\,(0.4853)^k$, with a negative residue on the intermediate mode. That mode subtracts from the response early and dies out faster than the slowest one, so the response first dips ($h_1<h_0$, hence $C_3<0$) and then rises toward the slow mode until lag 60.

\subsection{Cumulative transient risk}

\begin{theorem}[Summed response in closed form]\label{p2:thm:sum}
Let $\tilde\kappa=\kappa+\varphi\varepsilon/r$ (long-run impact: direct plus depth) and $m=1-a$ (reversion). In the stable three-state model,
\[
S\equiv\sum_{k\ge0}h_k=\frac{\tilde\kappa}{m-q\tilde\kappa},
\]
and when $\varphi\varepsilon\ge0$ and $q\ge0$ the system is stable only if $m>q\tilde\kappa$. When all $h_k\ge0$, $S$ is the summed absolute response that enters the guard of Section~\ref{p2:sec:guard}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$S=e_1^\top(I-M_3)^{-1}(\kappa,\varepsilon,0)^\top$ with $M_3$ the transition matrix of $(x,d,f)$. Direct computation gives $\det(I-M_3)=\eta\,[\,r(m-\kappa q)-\varphi\varepsilon q\,]$ and the stated ratio; the formula matches the simulated sums to a relative error of $7\times10^{-14}$ in 3{,}000 stable draws. If $\varphi\varepsilon\ge0$ and $q\ge0$, $M_3$ is entrywise nonnegative and stability requires $\det(I-M_3)>0$, i.e.\ $m>q\tilde\kappa$.
\end{proof}

Cumulative transient risk is long-run impact divided by reversion net of momentum. Two practical consequences follow. First, the statistic that matters for losses is the summed response, which grows without bound as reversion falls to $q\tilde\kappa$, even when no single lag overshoots (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak}\ref{p2:thm:nomom}). Second, $C_3$ is best read as a one-period amplification margin: it moves with $m$, $q$ and $\tilde\kappa$ in the same direction as $S$ and is what we estimate in Sections~\ref{p2:sec:depth} and~\ref{p2:sec:thin}. Both statistics are invariant in sign to rescaling flow.
\section{Identification and financial safety}\label{p2:sec:infosafety}

Chapter~\ref{ch:p1} shows that identifying the cohorts of algorithms from prices is expensive: separating $K$ cohorts whose memories differ by $\Delta\eta$ takes a number of experiments of order $(\Delta\eta)^{-(4K-2)}$, and with general memory accuracy improves only as $1/\log N$ (Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}). A pre-trade control does not need the cohorts; it needs a bound on the risk they create. This section asks what information certifies a safe trading decision, what that information costs, and how much safe trading must be given up when it is missing.

\subsection{Observation and risk contracts}\label{p2:sec:contracts}
We work in the positive cohort economy \eqref{p2:eq:J} with $q_i\ge0$ and known $a$ and $\kappa$. Its merged measure $M=\sum_iq_i\eta_i\delta_{r_i}$, $r_i=1-\eta_i$, has kernel $k_m=\int r^m\,dM$, and the price response to a unit of flow obeys $h_0=\kappa$, $h_{t+1}=ah_t+\kappa\sum_{m\le t}k_mh_{t-m}$. All $h_t\ge0$ (Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}, positive-geometry lemma), and the summed response is
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:SPhi}
S=\sum_th_t=\frac{\kappa}{1-a-\kappa\Phi},\qquad \Phi=\int\frac{dM(r)}{1-r}=\sum_iq_i ,
\end{equation}
with stability exactly when $a+\kappa\Phi<1$. A \emph{memory bound} $\bar r<1$ states that every memory rate satisfies $r_i\le\bar r$.

\emph{Risk contract.} A position $I$ is held fixed, each period's flow shock is bounded by $\bar u$, losses are marked to market at every date, and the horizon is unbounded. By Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} the worst-case loss is $|I|\bar uS$, so the safe capacity, the largest shock bound with loss at most the budget $\mathcal B$, is $\mathcal B/(|I|S)$. Section~\ref{p2:sec:guard} adds admission, margin and fills. All statements below are deterministic worst cases under this contract; none is a probability-of-ruin statement.

\emph{Observation contracts.} We consider four: (O1) $N$ independent reset experiments, each returning $h_0,\ldots,h_{T-1}$ with independent Gaussian errors of standard deviation $\sigma$ per tap, the controlled-probe contract of Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}; (O2) an exact window $h_0,\ldots,h_n$; (O3) class-tagged flows; and (O4) audited exposure, which reveals $\Phi$. The theorems concern these contracts. Observational public records of a live book are a different contract, for which we offer evidence (Section~\ref{p2:sec:p15}) but no finite-sample guarantee.

Given data $D$, let $\Theta(D)$ be the set of economies compatible with it. A decision is \emph{certifiably safe} if its worst-case loss is within budget for every economy in $\Theta(D)$, \emph{certifiably unsafe} if it exceeds the budget for every one, and \emph{undecidable} otherwise. This classification is a definition, not a result; it is the finance version of the data-informativity view of control without identification \citep{p2:vanwaarde2020} and of set-membership estimation \citep{p2:milanese1991}. The results below make it explicit for this economy.

\subsection{Two markets that look the same}

\begin{example}[An exact ambiguity witness]\label{p2:ex:witness}
Let $a=0.4$ and $\kappa=1$. Market A has one cohort ($q=0.08$, $\eta=0.5$); market B has two ($q=0.02/0.95$, $\eta=0.95$; $q=0.4$, $\eta=0.05$). Their merged measures have the same mass and first moment, so in rational arithmetic their price responses coincide at lags 0, 1 and 2 ($1$, $11/25$, $267/1250$) and first differ at lag 3. Their summed responses are $1.92$ and $5.59$, so for a unit position and budget the safe shock bound is $0.52$ in A and $0.18$ in B.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}[Unavoidable loss of safe capacity]\label{p2:prop:twoworld}
Let two economies have safe capacities $v_0<v_1$ and data laws $P_0,P_1$. If a data-driven rule $A(D)\in[0,v_1]$ admits an unsafe level in economy 0 with probability at most $\beta$, $P_0(A>v_0)\le\beta$, then
\[
\E_1[v_1-A(D)]\;\ge\;(v_1-v_0)\,\bigl[1-\beta-{\rm TV}(P_0,P_1)\bigr]_+ .
\]
The bound is attained when $P_0=P_1$ by the rule that admits $v_1$ with probability $\beta$ and $v_0$ otherwise.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $E=\{A\le v_0\}$. Then $P_0(E)\ge1-\beta$ and $P_1(E)\ge P_0(E)-{\rm TV}$. On $E$ the capacity given up in economy 1 is at least $v_1-v_0$, and it is nonnegative elsewhere.
\end{proof}

The argument is the two-point method of statistical decision theory \citep{p2:lecam1986}; its use here is to price missing information in units of safe trading. In Example~\ref{p2:ex:witness}, a supervisor who sees the first three coefficients and must admit an unsafe level in B with probability at most 5\% gives up at least $0.95\times0.34=0.32$ units of safe shock capacity in A.

\subsection{What a finite window certifies}

Lags $1$ to $n$ of the response determine the first $n$ moments of $M$ exactly, through $k_t=(h_{t+1}-ah_t-\kappa\sum_{m<t}k_mh_{t-m})/\kappa^2$. The summed response depends on $M$ only through $\Phi=\int f\,dM$ with $f(r)=(1-r)^{-1}$. Certifying it from a window is therefore an extremal moment problem.

\begin{theorem}[Finite-window certification]\label{p2:thm:window}
Observe $h_0,\ldots,h_n$ exactly under a memory bound $\bar r<1$, and suppose the implied moment vector $(k_0,\ldots,k_{n-1})$ lies in the interior of the moment space $\mathcal M_n([0,\bar r])$ (equivalently, the Hankel matrices of the moment sequence and of its localization by $r(\bar r-r)$ are positive definite). Let $\mu_-$ and $\mu_+$ be its lower and upper principal representations on $[0,\bar r]$ (the extremal measures of the Markov--Krein theorem, unique in the interior; $\mu_+$ has an atom at $\bar r$), and $\Phi_\pm=\int f\,d\mu_\pm$. On the boundary of the moment space the compatible measure is unique and $\Phi_-=\Phi_+$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{Sharp interval.} The summed responses of stable economies compatible with the data form the interval $[S(\Phi_-),S(\Phi_+)]$ if $a+\kappa\Phi_+<1$, and $[S(\Phi_-),\infty)$ otherwise. Both endpoints are attained by economies with at most $\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1$ cohorts.
\item \emph{Geometric convergence.} $\Phi_+-\Phi_-\le2k_0E_{n-1}$, where $E_{n-1}=(2/\bar r)\,\varrho^{\,n-1}/(x_0^2-1)$ is the error of the best uniform approximation of $f$ on $[0,\bar r]$ by polynomials of degree $n-1$, $x_0=2/\bar r-1$ and $\varrho=x_0-\sqrt{x_0^2-1}<1$. A window of length $n=O\bigl(\log(1/\epsilon)/\log(1/\varrho)\bigr)$ certifies $\Phi$ to precision $\epsilon$, and $\log(1/\varrho)\sim2\sqrt{(1-\bar r)/\bar r}$ as $\bar r\to1$.
\item \emph{No certificate without a memory bound.} If memory rates may lie anywhere in $[0,1)$, then for every $n$ and every window whose moment vector is interior to the moment space, compatible stable economies have arbitrarily large summed responses: no finite window certifies any finite risk bound.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

Part (i) is an application of the Markov--Krein theorem \citep{p2:krein1977,p2:karlin1966}: since $f^{(n)}(r)=n!/(1-r)^{n+1}>0$, the extremes of $\int f\,d\mu$ over measures with given moments are attained at the principal representations. Part (ii) uses Chebyshev's formula for the best approximation of $1/(x_0-x)$ \citep{p2:achieser1956}. The proofs are in Appendix~\ref{p2:app:proofs}. Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window} turns the three-way classification into a computation: a decision is certifiably safe from the window when $|I|\bar uS(\Phi_+)\le\mathcal B$, certifiably unsafe when $|I|\bar uS(\Phi_-)>\mathcal B$, and undecidable in between. In an eight-cohort economy with memory bound $0.95$ and true summed response $5.56$, the window of lags $0$--$3$ certifies nothing ($a+\kappa\Phi_+\ge1$), lags $0$--$4$ certify $S\le92.8$, lags $0$--$6$ give $[5.01,7.20]$ and lags $0$--$8$ give $[5.30,6.00]$; the widths stay inside the bound of part (ii) at every window. For fixed data the upper bound grows without limit as the memory bound approaches one ($\Phi_+=2.42$, $9.45$, $79.7$ and $782$ for $\bar r=0.9$, $0.99$, $0.999$ and $0.9999$, from the first four moments of a three-cohort economy), as part (iii) says (\texttt{research\_extension/information\_safety}).

The endpoints in these examples are computed, not proved, in floating point. The next result makes them proved.

\begin{lemma}[Certified endpoints]\label{p2:lem:certnum}
Let $p_+$ and $p_-$ be polynomials of degree at most $n-1$, $p_\pm(r)=\sum_jc^\pm_jr^j$, with $p_-\le f\le p_+$ on $[0,\bar r]$. Then every economy compatible with the window has $\sum_jc^-_jk_j\le\Phi\le\sum_jc^+_jk_j$. The Hermite interpolants of $f$ at the atoms of $\mu_+$ and $\mu_-$, with derivative conditions at interior atoms, satisfy the sign conditions and give $\Phi_+$ and $\Phi_-$ exactly.
\end{lemma}

Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:certnum} turns any computed pair of polynomials into a proof, whatever the rounding errors that produced them: if the sign conditions are verified, the bounds hold. We compute the interpolants in 60-digit arithmetic, round them to double precision, add a slack of $10^{-11}$ of $\Phi$, verify the sign conditions on $[0,\bar r]$ in interval arithmetic with outward rounding (a mean-value form on an adaptive subdivision; \citealp{p2:rump2010}), and evaluate the bounds with outward rounding. In the eight-cohort example the certified intervals for $S$ are $[5.01,7.20]$ and $[5.30,6.00]$ for lags $0$--$6$ and $0$--$8$, and every certified endpoint lies outside the 60-digit one by at most $1.5\times10^{-8}$ of its value, using about 62{,}000 subintervals (test T10; the exact count depends on the platform's floating-point library, the certified bounds do not). The interval endpoints quoted in this chapter are therefore results, not approximations.

\subsection{What certification costs, and what identification costs}

\begin{theorem}[Certification cost of the summed response]\label{p2:thm:certcost}
Consider the class $\mathcal K(\bar r,\bar s)$ of positive economies with memory bound $\bar r$ and $a+\kappa\Phi\le\bar s<1$, observed under contract (O1). Let $\bar\lambda=1-(1-\bar r)(1-\bar s)$ and $\bar h_t$ the average of the $N$ responses.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{A valid certificate.} For every economy in the class, $h_t\le\kappa\bar\lambda^t$, and
\[
\hat S^+_T=\sum_{t<T}\bar h_t+z_{1-\gamma}\,\sigma\sqrt{T/N}+\frac{\kappa\bar\lambda^T}{1-\bar\lambda}
\]
satisfies $P(S\le\hat S^+_T)\ge1-\gamma$. With $T_\epsilon=\lceil\log(2\kappa/((1-\bar\lambda)\epsilon))/\log(1/\bar\lambda)\rceil$ and $N\ge16z_{1-\gamma}^2\sigma^2T_\epsilon/\epsilon^2$, the excess $\hat S^+-S$ is at most $\epsilon$ with probability at least $1-2\gamma$. Certification to precision $\epsilon$ therefore costs $N=O(\sigma^2\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\epsilon))$.
\item \emph{A matching lower bound.} Fix an interior single-cohort economy $\theta_0$ and let $C_0=\|\partial h/\partial q\|_2/(\partial S/\partial q)$ at $\theta_0$. Any procedure that is valid at level $1-\gamma$ on the class and overshoots $S$ by less than $2\epsilon$ with probability at least $1/2$ at $\theta_0$ needs $N\ge\bigl((1-2\gamma)\sigma/(2C_0\epsilon)\bigr)^2(1+o(1))$ as $\epsilon\to0$.
\item \emph{The logarithm is necessary.} There is $c>0$ depending only on $(a,\kappa,\bar r,\bar s)$ such that any procedure that is valid at level $1-\gamma$, $\gamma<1/4$, on the class and overshoots $S$ by at most $\epsilon$ with probability at least $1-\gamma$ at every economy of the class needs $N\ge c\,\sigma^2\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\epsilon)$ for all small $\epsilon$, however many responses each experiment records. With part (i), the cost of certifying $S$ is $\Theta(\sigma^2\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\epsilon))$.
\item \emph{Independence of the cohort structure.} None of these bounds depends on the number of cohorts or on the spacing of their memories, whereas identifying them costs $N\asymp\sigma^2(\Delta\eta)^{-(4K-2)}$ with exponential memory and $\exp(\Theta(1/w))$ experiments for accuracy $w$ with general memory (Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

The certificate in part (i) is elementary: it is a sum of averaged taps, a Gaussian margin and a tail that the memory bound controls. Part (iv) shows that its logarithm is not an artefact of truncation: no procedure, however it weights the observed responses, certifies the summed response at the parametric rate, because $1/(1-r)$, the kernel of the total feedback, has a pole at infinite memory and any polynomial that approximates it on the memory range must carry large coefficients. Part (ii) is the parametric bound at a fixed economy; part (iv) is the worst case over the class. The content is in the comparison. In contrast to the $\mathcal H_\infty$ norm, which \citet{p2:tu2018} show is no easier to estimate than the model itself, the summed response of a positive economy is a linear functional of the response, so certifying it costs only one logarithm more than a parametric problem, while the cohort parameters cost polynomially more. Table~\ref{p2:tab:certvsid} makes this concrete on Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}'s hardest two-cohort pairs. Those pairs have \emph{identical} summed responses, because they share the total weight $\sum_iq_i$: the directions in which prices are least informative are directions in which the risk a guard must bound does not move. Identifying their weights to $\pm0.01$ needs from 7 to 1.5 million reset experiments as the spacing falls from $0.30$ to $0.04$ (log--log slope $-6.06$), while certifying the summed response to 1\% needs 11 to 18 at every spacing (slope $+0.23$).
