\begin{remark}[Shorter windows cost more experiments]\label{p2:rem:cheb}
Under exact windows the polynomial bounds of Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:certnum} certify $\Phi$ to precision $\epsilon$ with $n=O\bigl((1-\bar r)^{-1/2}\log(1/\epsilon)\bigr)$ taps (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}(ii)), whereas the tail bound of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}(i) needs $T_\epsilon=\Theta\bigl(((1-\bar r)(1-\bar s))^{-1}\log(1/\epsilon)\bigr)$. With noisy taps the shorter window is not a better certificate. The bound $\sum_jc^+_j\hat k_j$ is linear in the estimated kernel values, so its standard error is $\sigma N^{-1/2}\|\mathcal J^\top c^+\|_2$, where $\mathcal J$ is the Jacobian of the map from taps to kernel values; the monomial coefficients of an approximating polynomial on $[0,\bar r]$ grow geometrically in its degree. In a three-cohort economy with memory bound $0.99$ ($S=3.28$), certifying $S$ to 1\% takes 45 taps with the window bound against 4{,}455 with the tail bound, but $3.5\times10^{65}\sigma^2$ experiments against $1.8\times10^{8}\sigma^2$; at 0.1\%, 56 against 5{,}482 taps and $3\times10^{82}\sigma^2$ against $2.2\times10^{10}\sigma^2$ experiments (\texttt{hardening/p2\_cheb\_tradeoff.py}). Power-moment problems are exponentially ill-conditioned, so the parametric rate of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost} is bought with a long, well-conditioned horizon; the window bound is the right tool for exact or near-exact data.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Unknown noise covariance.} Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}(i) assumes independent taps with a known noise level. Reset experiments need not deliver that: the noise in the taps of one experiment can be correlated. Let contract (O1$'$) be (O1) with Gaussian noise of unknown covariance $\Sigma$ across the taps of an experiment, experiments still independent, and let $X_e=\sum_{t<T}h^{(e)}_t$ for experiment $e$.

\begin{proposition}[Certification with unknown covariance]\label{p2:prop:hotelling}
Under (O1$'$), let $\bar X$ and $s_X$ be the mean and standard deviation of $X_1,\ldots,X_N$ and $\bar h$, $\hat\Sigma$ the mean and covariance of the $N$ windows.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\hat S^+_t=\bar X+t_{N-1,1-\gamma}\,s_X/\sqrt N+\kappa\bar\lambda^T/(1-\bar\lambda)$ satisfies $P(S\le\hat S^+_t)\ge1-\gamma$ for every $\Sigma$.
\item If $N>T$, the Hotelling region $\{m:N(\bar h-m)^\top\hat\Sigma^{-1}(\bar h-m)\le T^2_\gamma\}$, $T^2_\gamma=\frac{T(N-1)}{N-T}F_{T,N-T}(1-\gamma)$, contains the mean window with probability exactly $1-\gamma$, so the bounds $c^\top\bar h+\sqrt{T^2_\gamma\,c^\top\hat\Sigma c/N}$ hold simultaneously for every linear functional $c$: every horizon, every exposure profile and every guard computed from the same experiments.
\item The certificate of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}(i) can cover with probability well below $1-\gamma$ when the taps are positively correlated.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

Part (i) is Student's $t$ applied to the sums, and (ii) is Hotelling's theorem \citep{p2:hotelling1931}; the content is which assumption each certificate needs. In simulations with twelve taps and nominal 95\% coverage (test T11), positively correlated taps (autocorrelation $0.8$) cut the coverage of the (O1) certificate to 74--76\%, while the $t$-certificate covers 94--95\% and the Hotelling bound 100\%, at the cost of a margin three to six times wider. Heavy-tailed noise ($t_3$) leaves the $t$-certificate at 95\%. Dependence \emph{across} experiments (autocorrelation $0.5$) cuts it to 82--85\%: independence of the experiments is what a controlled contract buys and what observational data lack, which is why Sections~\ref{p2:sec:p15} and~\ref{p2:sec:p16} report bootstrap bounds as diagnostics rather than certificates. Experiments can also be run one at a time and stopped once the data decide; an anytime-valid version of the certificate keeps its error level under such stopping and needs far fewer experiments when the economy is far from the threshold (Internet Appendix, Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:probe}).
\section{An identification-free pre-trade guard}\label{p2:sec:guard}

\subsection{The quantity gate}
Observable dynamics are $z_{j+1}=Jz_j+Hv_j$ with loss output $l_t=Cz_t$, where $v_j$ is the executed quantity. Let $b_t=CJ^tz_0$ be the drift from past flow and $g_m=c^\top_{\rm price}J^mH$ the impulse coefficients. Which gain bounds the loss depends on the quantity contract. If quantities share a total budget over the window, $\sum_{j<t}|v_j|\le V$, the worst case of $|\sum_{m<t}g_mv_{t-1-m}|$ is $V\max_{m<t}|g_m|\equiv Vg^*_t$, attained by placing the whole budget at the lag of the peak, and execution costs at most $c_{\rm exec}V$. Under a reserve $L_{\rm other}$ the sharp allowance is then
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:gate}
V^*=\min_{t:\,g^*_t+c_{\rm exec}>0}\frac{B-L_{\rm other}-|b_t|}{g^*_t+c_{\rm exec}},\qquad\bar R=V^*/\tau
\end{equation}
over a window of $\tau$ seconds. If instead each period's quantity is bounded, $|v_j|\le V$, the worst case is $V\sum_{m<t}|g_m|$ (attained by $v_{t-1-m}=V\,{\rm sign}(g_m)$) and execution costs at most $c_{\rm exec}Vt$, so the peak gain in \eqref{p2:eq:gate} must be replaced by the summed gain $\sum_{m<t}|g_m|$ and $c_{\rm exec}$ by $c_{\rm exec}t$. Pairing a per-period bound with the peak gain understates the worst case, which is the failure Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} formalizes. Either gate needs only impulse coefficients and the current drift.

\subsection{Maximality}
A dealer with inventory $I$ chooses an admission level $\alpha\in[0,1]$ (the fraction of incoming algorithmic flow it accepts, or the depth it quotes). The closed-loop operator is $J(\alpha)=J_0+\alpha B_F$ with price impulse response $g_k(\alpha)$. Shocks satisfy $|u_t|\le\bar u$. Over horizon $H$ under a reset initial state $z_0=0$ (or after subtracting a known or directly observed initial drift $b_t=CJ(\alpha)^t z_0$ from the remaining budget), the shock-driven price change is $\sum_{k<H}g_k(\alpha)u_{t-k}$, and the dealer has a loss budget $\mathcal B$. The contract is: inventory $I(\alpha)$ is held fixed over the horizon, the initial state is reset ($z_0=0$) or its drift is observed, each period's shock is bounded by $\bar u$, and loss is the mark-to-market loss at any date in the horizon; because the partial sums $\sum_{k<h}|g_k|$ increase in $h$, the worst date is the last. Margin, hedging, fills and non-zero initial states are analysed in Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:forward}.

\begin{lemma}[Summed-gain guard is maximal]\label{p2:thm:guard}
The worst-case loss over horizon $H$ is
\[
L_{\max}(\alpha)=|I(\alpha)|\,\bar u\sum_{k<H}|g_k(\alpha)|.
\]
Hence the guard ``admit $\alpha$ iff $L_{\max}(\alpha)\le\mathcal B$'' is necessary and sufficient for bounded-shock solvency: it admits every safe $\alpha$ and no unsafe one. Replacing $\sum_k|g_k|$ by $\max_k|g_k|$ can admit unsafe levels; replacing it by $H\max_k|g_k|$ is safe but conservative.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By H\"older's inequality $|\sum_kg_ku_{t-k}|\le\bar u\sum_k|g_k|$, with equality for $u_{t-k}=\bar u\,{\rm sign}(g_k)$, which is admissible. Multiplying by $|I(\alpha)|$ gives $L_{\max}$, so the admission set equals the safe set. Since $\max_k|g_k|\le\sum_k|g_k|\le H\max_k|g_k|$, the peak guard under-states loss and the $H$-scaled guard over-states it.
\end{proof}

The worst-case identity in Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} is not new as mathematics: the induced $\ell_\infty$ gain of a linear convolution operator is the $\ell_1$ norm of its kernel, the fact on which $\ell_1$-optimal control of persistent bounded disturbances is built \citep{p2:vidyasagar1986,p2:dahleh1987,p2:dahleh1995}. We use it rather than claim it. The finance content lies elsewhere: the dealer's admission level changes the operator itself, so the guard is a rule over an action set rather than a controller design; the bound is carried through margin, hedging and fill accounting (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:forward}); its single input is invariant to how the unobserved traders are split into cohorts and is identified from prices (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:invariance} and Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}); the peak-response and eigenvalue rules it is compared with are the ones closest to current supervisory practice; and the same statistic prices the corrective rebate in equilibrium (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:equil}).

Numerically, the guard coincides with a brute-force adversary's maximal safe $\alpha$ in 200 of 200 random states. The peak-gain guard admitted an unsafe level in 2,000 of 2,000 states, the $H$-scaled guard was conservative in all 2,000, and $L_{\max}$ was non-decreasing in $\alpha$ in all 2,000, so the admission set was an interval in these examples.

\subsection{Computability: a finite-horizon certificate}

\begin{lemma}[Contraction-block tail bound]\label{p2:lem:tail}
Let $\rho(J(\alpha))<1$. Suppose that at horizon $H$ there is a block length $H_0\le H$ with $q(\alpha)\equiv\|J(\alpha)^{H_0}\|_2<1$, and let $\bar M_{H_0}(\alpha)=\max_{0\le r<H_0}\|c^\top_{\rm price}J(\alpha)^r\|_2$ and $M_{C,H}(\alpha)=\max_{k\le H}\|c^\top_{\rm price}J(\alpha)^k\|_2$. Then
\begin{align}
\sup_{k\ge0}\|c^\top_{\rm price}J(\alpha)^k\|_2&\le\max\Bigl(M_{C,H}(\alpha),\ \bar M_{H_0}(\alpha)q(\alpha)^{\lfloor(H+1)/H_0\rfloor}\Bigr),\label{p2:eq:tail}\\
G_{{\rm cum},\infty}(\alpha)&\le G_{{\rm cum},H}(\alpha)+\frac{\bar M_{H_0}(\alpha)H_0\,q(\alpha)^{\lfloor(H+1)/H_0\rfloor}}{1-q(\alpha)}.\notag
\end{align}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write any $k>H$ as $k=mH_0+r$ with $m\ge\lfloor(H+1)/H_0\rfloor$ and $0\le r<H_0$. Submultiplicativity gives $\|c^\top J^k\|\le\|c^\top J^r\|\,\|J^{H_0}\|^m\le\bar M_{H_0}q^m$. Taking the supremum and summing the geometric tail gives \eqref{p2:eq:tail}.
\end{proof}

The certificate turns an infinite-horizon supremum into a finite computation with a verified remainder. If the second term in the maximum is below $M_{C,H}$, truncation at $H$ is exact.

Example~\ref{p2:ex:witness} shows why the certificate is needed: two economies can agree exactly on the first coefficients and still differ threefold in their summed response.

\subsection{Robustness to estimation error}

\begin{theorem}[Robust gain]\label{p2:thm:robust}
Let $M=\sup_k\|J^k\|$ and $S=\sum_k\|J^k\|$. If $\|E\|\le\epsilon$ and $\epsilon S<1$, then $\sup_k\|(J+E)^k\|\le M/(1-\epsilon S)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $P_k=(J+E)^k$. Expanding, $P_k=J^k+\sum_{j<k}J^{k-1-j}EP_j$. With $m_k=\|P_k\|$, $m_k\le M+\epsilon\sum_{j<k}\|J^{k-1-j}\|m_j\le M+\epsilon S\max_{j<k}m_j$. Since $m_0=1\le M$, induction gives $m_k\le M/(1-\epsilon S)$ for all $k$.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robust} is deterministic: it holds whenever $\|E\|\le\epsilon$. A statistical guarantee follows only through a confidence set for the operator, and then holds with that set's coverage probability, not with certainty. The bound held in all 30,000 random perturbations of 100 non-normal operators, with median tightness $0.50$ (realized gain over bound; maximum $0.57$). In a simulated window estimator of the kind used in Section~\ref{p2:sec:spot} (600 transitions), a residual-bootstrap 95\% ball for the operator covered the true $J$ in $94.7\%$ of 300 replications, with median radius $0.086$; but $\epsilon S<1$ held in only $8\%$ of windows. The certificate is valid but often unavailable at realistic sample sizes; longer windows or pooled estimates are needed to use it in practice.

The guard needs the summed gain, which is invariant to cohort splitting (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:invariance}) and certified with $\Theta(\sigma^2\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\epsilon))$ experiments whatever the spacing of the memories (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}); it does not need the algorithms' identities, which are expensive (Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}). In a two-cohort economy with rates $0.7\pm\Delta$, the relative error of the estimated summed gain is flat in $\Delta$ (log--log slope $-0.15$), while the error of the cohort weights grows as $\Delta^{-3.07}$.


\subsection{An uncertainty-aware guard}\label{p2:sec:robustguard}
The guard of Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} assumes the operator is known. With data, the relevant question is which admission levels are safe for every economy the data allow. We answer it for a venue gate (Mode B below) in the positive cohort economy, where admitting a fraction $\alpha$ of the algorithmic flow scales every cohort's weight, $M\mapsto\alpha M$, so that $S(\alpha)=\kappa/(1-a-\kappa\alpha\Phi)$.

\begin{lemma}[Soundness of compatible-set guards]\label{p2:lem:sound}
If a data-dependent set $\mathcal C(D)$ contains the true economy with probability at least $1-\gamma$, then every action whose worst-case loss is within budget for all economies in $\mathcal C(D)$ is within budget for the true economy with probability at least $1-\gamma$, including an action selected from the data. If no action qualifies, the guard certifies nothing: it halts new admission and flags the state, because existing inventory can be unsafe even with no new trading.
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}[Optimal uncertainty-aware admission]\label{p2:thm:robustguard}
Under the reset or observed-drift contract ($z_0=0$, or known drift subtracted from $\mathcal B$), let position $I$, shock bound $\bar u$ and budget $\mathcal B$ be given, and let $\Phi^+$ be an upper bound on $\Phi$ valid with probability at least $1-\gamma$ (for example $\Phi^+=(1-a-\kappa/\hat S^+)/\kappa$ from Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}, or $\Phi_+$ from Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}). Put $c_I=1-a-\kappa|I|\bar u/\mathcal B$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{Closed form and interval.} If $c_I\ge0$, the levels that are safe for every $\Phi\le\Phi^+$ form the interval $[0,\alpha^*]$ with $\alpha^*=\min\{1,\ c_I/(\kappa\Phi^+)\}$.
\item \emph{Safety.} With probability at least $1-\gamma$, every admitted level is safe for the true economy.
\item \emph{Maximality.} Every level above $\alpha^*$ is unsafe for an economy with $\Phi=\Phi^+$; when $\Phi^+$ is attained by a compatible economy (as for $\Phi_+$ in Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}), no rule using the same information can admit more without admitting an unsafe level.
\item \emph{Infeasibility.} If $c_I<0$, no level is safe, including $\alpha=0$: the guard halts admission and the position must fall to $|I|\le\mathcal B(1-a)/(\kappa\bar u)$.
\item \emph{Minimax capacity loss.} On the class of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost} with $\Phi$ bounded away from zero, the certificate-based guard gives up $\alpha^\circ-\alpha^*=O\bigl(\sigma\sqrt{\log N/N}\bigr)$ of the oracle capacity $\alpha^\circ=\min\{1,c_I/(\kappa\Phi)\}$ with probability at least $1-2\gamma$, and every rule that admits an unsafe level with probability at most $\beta<1$ gives up at least $c\,\sigma/\sqrt N$ in expectation at some economy of the class. The guard is minimax-optimal in lost capacity up to a logarithmic factor.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

Monotonicity of $S(\alpha)$ in $\alpha$ and $\Phi$ gives (i)--(iv); (v) combines the certificate's excess with Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:twoworld} applied to two economies whose capacities differ by order $\sigma/\sqrt N$ (Appendix~\ref{p2:app:proofs}). In 500 random economies the closed form matched a brute-force search over 1{,}001 admission levels, computed from the state matrices rather than from \eqref{p2:eq:SPhi}, in every case; every safe set was an interval; and the 28 infeasible states were flagged (test T06). Across 18 designs the certificate covered the true summed response at least at its nominal level (minimum 93.6\% at nominal 90\%), with a median excess of 3--15\% when the memory bound is $0.6$ or $0.9$ and about 230\% when it is $0.97$, where the tail term dominates (test T04).

\subsection{When admission changes the dynamics}\label{p2:sec:venue}
Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robustguard} rests on one property: risk rises with the admission level, so the safe levels form an interval $[0,\alpha^*]$ and certifying $\alpha^*$ certifies every level below it. A venue that admits a class of participants can also change the book. Suppose admitting a fraction $\alpha$ adds feedback $\alpha\Phi$, deepens the book so that impact falls to $\kappa_0/(1+\delta\alpha)$, and speeds reversion to $1-a_0+\rho\alpha$, with $\theta=(\kappa_0,a_0,\Phi,\delta,\rho)$ in a certified box $\Theta$. Then $S(\alpha)=\kappa_0/D(\alpha)$ with $D(\alpha)=(1+\delta\alpha)(1-a_0+\rho\alpha)-\kappa_0\Phi\alpha$.

\begin{proposition}[Venue mode]\label{p2:prop:venue}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item For each economy the safe levels $\{\alpha\in[0,1]:|I|\bar uS(\alpha)\le\mathcal B\}$ are $[0,1]$ minus at most one open interval; they can be $[0,\alpha_1]\cup[\alpha_2,1]$ with $0<\alpha_1<\alpha_2<1$.
\item For $\alpha\ge0$ the worst economy of $\Theta$ is the same corner, $(\kappa_0^+,a_0^+,\Phi^+,\delta^-,\rho^-)$, for every $\alpha$, so the robust safe set is the safe set of that corner, with endpoints at the roots of a quadratic.
\item A rule that certifies the largest safe level and admits every level below it admits unsafe levels whenever the robust safe set is not an interval. Levels in $[\alpha_2,1]$ can be used safely only by moving to them directly, not by raising admission step by step.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

In the box $\kappa_0\in[0.98,1.02]$, $a_0\in[0.58,0.60]$, $\Phi\in[2.3,2.4]$, $\delta\in[2.9,3.1]$, $\rho\in[0.58,0.62]$ with $|I|\bar u/\mathcal B=0.37$, the robust safe set is $[0,0.035]\cup[0.386,1]$, in agreement with a 2{,}001-point grid over 332 economies of the box (test T13). Low admission brings mostly feedback and high admission mostly liquidity, so risk first rises and then falls.

Without a closed form, as in the three-state book of Section~\ref{p2:sec:t6}, the robust safe set must be computed. Let the impact $\kappa(\alpha)$, the feedback $q(\alpha)$ and the refill rate $r(\alpha)$ of the book depend on admission, with the uncertain parameters in a box $\Theta$, and let $S(\alpha,\theta)$ be the summed absolute price response.

\begin{theorem}[Certified admission without monotonicity]\label{p2:thm:venuebb}
Enclose $S$ over a box of levels and parameters by its first $T$ terms in interval arithmetic with outward rounding plus the tail bound $\|J^Ts_0\|_\infty\sum_{j<T}\|J^j\|_\infty/(1-\|J^T\|_\infty)$ (infinite when $\|J^T\|_\infty\ge1$ cannot be excluded). Classify a subinterval of levels as safe if the enclosure over it and every part of a partition of $\Theta$ is within budget, as unsafe if the lower enclosure at some economy of $\Theta$ exceeds the budget, and otherwise split it, down to a tolerance. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{Soundness.} Every level classified safe is safe for every economy in $\Theta$, and every level classified unsafe is unsafe for some economy in $\Theta$, in exact arithmetic.
\item \emph{Existence and bracketing.} The robust safe set is closed, so the largest safe level exists if any level is safe, and so does the largest level reachable by raising admission from zero through safe levels if zero is safe. The first lies between the largest certified-safe level and the largest level not certified unsafe; the second between the end of the certified-safe interval that starts at zero and the first level certified unsafe.
\item \emph{No false certificate.} If every level is classified unsafe, the guard halts admission and flags the state.
\item \emph{Completeness.} If $\sup_\Theta\rho(J)<1$, then as the tolerance and the partition of $\Theta$ shrink and $T$ grows, every level at which the robust risk differs from the budget is eventually classified.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

The method is interval branch and bound \citep{p2:hansen2004}; the theorem's content is that its output is a certificate for the risk contract, including for admission rules whose risk is not monotone. In a book with depletion and refill where admission raises feedback linearly and liquidity quadratically (test T13), the certified safe set at a budget of $6.5$ is $[0,0.053]\cup[0.178,1]$, with $[0.059,0.168]$ certified unsafe and $0.016$ left undecided; no certified-safe level was unsafe for any of 433 sampled economies on a 1{,}001-point grid. The shortcut that certifies $\alpha=1$ and admits everything below it would admit the whole unsafe band.

\subsection{A calibrated illustration}\label{p2:sec:calib}

How much does the choice between peak and summed guards matter in a real book? We compute the three-state impulse response at the median estimates of Section~\ref{p2:sec:depth} (all training days and high-volatility days) and at a hypothetical book in which flow depletes the side it hits ($\varepsilon=+0.02$, other parameters at their high-volatility medians), Figure~\ref{p2:fig:calib} and Table~\ref{p2:tab:calib}.

\end{figure}

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}

\begin{tabular}{p{0.2\textwidth}ccccccc}
\toprule
Book & $C_3$ & Peak (min) & $\sum_{k<15}|g_k|$ & $\sum_{k<60}|g_k|$ & Sum/peak & Cap, peak & Cap, sum\\
\midrule
All days (medians) & $-0.013$ & 0.73 (0) & 9.73 & 27.67 & 13.3 & \$274M & \$21M\\

The result is striking even though BTCUSDT does not amplify. Because price impact is persistent ($\hat a\approx0.97$ per minute), the summed gain over 15 minutes is about 13 times the peak. A guard calibrated to the peak would admit inventory about 13 times larger than is safe against a sustained adversarial flow of the declared size. Amplification makes this worse (the depleting book peaks 17 minutes after the shock), but persistence alone already makes peak-based limits unsafe. This is the practical content of Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} for this market.

\subsection{Two deployment modes}

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{Mode \Alph*.},leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{An individual dealer.} A price-taking market maker with a small share of aggregate depth scales its own quote size by $\alpha$. This scales its passive fill intake but leaves aggregate depth, impact and the market operator unchanged.
\item \emph{A venue or sponsoring broker.} A gate at a matching engine or a market-access gateway (the setting of Rule 15c3-5) admits a fraction $\alpha$ of incoming aggressive algorithmic flow while resting depth stays posted. The operator becomes $J(\alpha)=A_0+\alpha B_F$.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Algorithm (pre-trade guard).} At each step, using only pre-shock information:
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=1pt]
\item For each $\alpha$ on a grid $\mathcal A=\{0,0.025,\ldots,1\}$, compute $\rho(J(\alpha))$, the peak and summed gains $M_{C,H}(\alpha)$ and $G_{{\rm cum},H}(\alpha)$, and the tail certificate of Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:tail}; if an operator confidence radius $\epsilon$ is available, inflate by Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robust}.
\item Compute post-hedge and worst-case post-fill inventory, hedging cost and the post-fill margin increment.
\item Admit the largest $\alpha\in\mathcal A$ that satisfies the solvency inequalities of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:forward}; if none does, set $\alpha=0$, flag that no safe action exists, and trigger inventory reduction.
\end{enumerate}
The guard checks every grid point, so it does not rely on the safe set being an interval; levels between grid points are never admitted. For low-dimensional $J$ the grid evaluation is a sequence of matrix--vector products; execution takes well under one millisecond in compiled code, which we regard as an engineering detail rather than a result.

\subsection{Balance-sheet solvency}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional bounded-shock forward solvency]\label{p2:thm:forward}
Consider a dealer with cash $K_t$, inventory $I_t$, mark $p_t$, equity $E_t=K_t+I_tp_t$, maintenance margin $MM_t=\max(0,\mu|I_t|p_t-c_0)$ and remaining buffer $B_{{\rm rem},t}=E_t-MM_t$, a safety reserve $R_{\rm safe}$, a hedge $\Delta I^{\rm hedge}_t(\alpha)$ with cost $C_{{\rm hedge},t}(\alpha)$, worst-case passive fills $\Delta I^{\rm fill}_{\max,t}(\alpha)$ with margin increment $C_{{\rm fill},t}(\alpha)$, and declared one-step and cumulative shock envelopes $\bar u_{{\rm step},t}\le\bar u_{{\rm cum},t}$. Let $|I^{\rm post}_{t+1}(\alpha)|=|I_t+\Delta I^{\rm hedge}_t(\alpha)|+\Delta I^{\rm fill}_{\max,t}(\alpha)$. Suppose the guard admits $\alpha_t$ only if
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:guardineq}
|I^{\rm post}_{t+1}(\alpha_t)|(1+\mu)\Bigl(M_{C}(\alpha_t)\|z_t\|_2+s(\alpha_t)G_{\rm cum}(\alpha_t)\bar u_{{\rm cum},t}\Bigr)+C_{{\rm hedge},t}(\alpha_t)+C_{{\rm fill},t}(\alpha_t)+R_{\rm safe}\le B_{{\rm rem},t},
\end{equation}
where $s(\alpha)$ scales admitted shocks. Then for every disturbance path within the declared envelopes, the buffer after the step is at least $R_{\rm safe}$; by induction, solvency holds at every step at which an admissible $\alpha$ exists. At $\alpha=0$ the one-step admissible shock is
\[
\bar u_{{\rm adm},t}=\frac{B_{{\rm rem},t}-R_{\rm safe}-|I_t|(1+\mu)\|z_t\|_2}{|I_t|(1+\mu)} .
\]
The condition is sufficient over the whole disturbance ball. It is also necessary when the current state contributes no drift ($z_t=0$), because the shock term is then attained by adversarially signed disturbances; with $z_t\neq0$ the drift term $M_C\|z_t\|_2$ is an upper bound and the condition may be conservative. The admissible shock at $\alpha=0$ assumes, as in the cohort economy (Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}, positive-geometry lemma), that the price-row gain without admitted flow is $M_C(0)=1$.
\end{theorem}

Two features matter. The guard accounts for the inventory the dealer will hold after hedging and after worst-case fills, not only current inventory, and it never claims safety on an infeasible state: if no $\alpha$ satisfies \eqref{p2:eq:guardineq}, it halts new fills and flags the state, because pre-existing inventory can still breach margin on a large adverse jump. In a constructed scenario ($E_0=10{,}000$, $I_0=-2{,}000$ at $P^*=100$, Tier-2 margin with $\mu=0.005$, buffer $9{,}050$ and reserve $950$), the admissible shock at $z_0=0$ is $8{,}100/2{,}010=4.03$ per unit, and an adversarial move of $4.05$ violates the reserve. In a matched-shock Monte Carlo inside the model, where the guard's risk contract holds by construction (the certified bound $\Phi^+=0.36$ is close to the true $\Phi=0.34$), across 1{,}000 paths facing identical exogenous flow shocks, initial inventory levels, transaction costs and double-entry maintenance-margin accounting where all active baselines are calibrated on an independent training set to match the guard's mean trading capacity of $68.0\%$ (\texttt{microfoundation\_and\_baselines.py}), the summed-gain guard achieves $0.0\%$ reserve breaches ($E_t-MM_t<R_{\rm safe}$) and $0.0\%$ exchange forced liquidations ($E_t<MM_t$), compared with $31.6\%$ forced liquidations ($34.7\%$ reserve breaches) under no control, $31.4\%$ ($34.1\%$) under a trailing volatility limit, $20.3\%$ ($21.5\%$) under an inventory cap and $0.0\%$ under a scanning-range margin rule; lowers 95\% expected-shortfall tail loss to $5.96$ (against $12.11$ under the margin rule, $39.77$ under the inventory cap, $39.92$ under the volatility limit and $39.89$ under no control, with all paired bootstrap difference 95\% CIs excluding zero); and attains the highest net dealer P\&L ($+0.75$, 95\% CI $[+0.48,+1.02]$). At a matched tail-risk budget ($\mathrm{ES}_{95}\approx 5.96$), the guard admits $68.0\%$ capacity versus $29.8\%$ for the margin rule, $24.1\%$ for the inventory cap and $19.8\%$ for the volatility limit; conversely, in a heteroskedastic zero-feedback regime ($\Phi=0$) with a conservatively misspecified certificate $\Phi^+=0.40$, the trailing volatility limit outperforms the static-$\Phi^+$ guard at matched capacity ($\mathrm{ES}_{95}=4.39$ vs.\ $4.55$).

Unlike the reset quantity-gate of Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} and Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robustguard}, the general forward-solvency inequality \eqref{p2:eq:guardineq} with an unknown non-zero hidden cohort state $z_t\neq 0$ (where $z_t=(x_t,y_{1,t},\ldots,y_{K,t})^\top$) is \emph{not} identification-invariant under an unrestricted Euclidean norm bound. Both the unforced future price drift $b_m=c_{\rm price}^\top J^m z_t$---whose cumulative sum is $\bigl(a x_t+\kappa\sum_i(q_i/\eta_i)y_{i,t}\bigr)/(1-a-\kappa\Phi)$---and the operator-norm drift bound $M_C(\alpha_t)\|z_t\|_2$ depend on the individual cohort weights and memory rates $(q_i,\eta_i)$ and the hidden state $y_t$, not solely on $\Phi=\sum_i q_i$. For an explicit rational counterexample (\texttt{bridge\_check.py}, Part F), take $a=2/5$, $\kappa=1$, and two two-cohort positive economies $\theta^{(1)}$ with $q^{(1)}=(1/5,1/5)$, $\eta^{(1)}=(5/8,3/8)$ and $\theta^{(2)}$ with $q^{(2)}=(9/25,1/25)$, $\eta^{(2)}=(13/24,1/8)$. Both share $\Phi=2/5$ ($S=5$) and identical early impulse responses $(h_0,h_1,h_2)=(1,3/5,363/800)$ ($k_0=1/5$, $k_1=3/32$). Yet after a single unit flow shock two periods earlier, where both markets display the exact same price path $(1,3/5,363/800)$, their hidden cohort states produce different next-step drift $b_1=9009/24000$ versus $8959/24000$; after three periods their cumulative future drift is $61701/24000$ versus $61751/24000$; over unknown hidden states $\|y_0\|_2\le 1$ at $x_0=0$ their worst-case cumulative drift is $3.110$ versus $3.688$ ($18.6\%$ larger); and their one-step price-row norm $\|c_{\rm price}^\top J\|_2=\sqrt{a^2+\kappa^2\sum_i q_i^2}$ is $0.490$ versus $0.540$.

When the unobserved cohort memories are instead bounded in the natural $\ell_\infty$ norm $\|y_0\|_\infty\le\bar y_0$---which holds automatically whenever historical pricing errors satisfy $|x_{-s}|\le\bar y_0$ for $s\ge0$, since each $y_{i,0}$ is a convex combination of past $x_{-s}$---the worst-case unforced drift has an exact identification-free envelope and yields a closed-form drift-adjusted guard:

\begin{proposition}[Uniformly safe guard under interval-bounded unknown hidden drift]\label{p2:prop:driftguard}
In any positive cohort economy with $\Phi=\sum_{i=1}^K q_i\le\Phi^+$, observed current pricing error $x_0$ and unknown hidden cohort states satisfying $\|y_0\|_\infty\le\bar y_0$, let admission $\alpha\in[0,1]$ scale cohort flow as $M\mapsto\alpha M$ with $a+\alpha\kappa\Phi^+<1$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{Identification-free drift envelope.} For every compatible cohort decomposition $(q_i,\eta_i)_{i=1}^K$ with $\sum_i q_i=\Phi$, the worst-case cumulative unforced drift over $\|y_0\|_\infty\le\bar y_0$ is
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:driftenv}
\sup_{\|y_0\|_\infty\le\bar y_0}\sum_{t=1}^\infty |e_x^\top J(\alpha)^t z_0|\;=\;\frac{a|x_0|+\alpha\kappa\Phi\bar y_0}{1-a-\alpha\kappa\Phi},
\end{equation}
attained when $y_{i,0}={\rm sign}(x_0)\bar y_0$ for all $i$ (or $y_{i,0}=\pm\bar y_0$ when $x_0=0$).
\item \emph{Closed-form drift-adjusted guard.} Under shock bound $\bar u$, inventory $I$ and budget $\mathcal B$, the set of admission levels that are safe for every compatible economy with $\Phi\le\Phi^+$ and every hidden state $\|y_0\|_\infty\le\bar y_0$ is $[0,\alpha_{\rm drift}^*]$ (empty when $(1-a)\mathcal B<|I|(a|x_0|+\kappa\bar u)$), where
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:alphadrift}
\alpha_{\rm drift}^*\;=\;\min\Biggl\{1,\ \biggl[\frac{(1-a)\mathcal B-|I|(a|x_0|+\kappa\bar u)}{\kappa\Phi^+(\mathcal B+|I|\bar y_0)}\biggr]_+\Biggr\}.
\end{equation}
\item \emph{Exact capacity cost of unknown state.} When $x_0=0$ and the reset guard $\alpha^*=\min\{1,c_I/(\kappa\Phi^+)\}$ of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robustguard} is interior ($\alpha^*\in(0,1)$), the capacity given up to protect against unknown hidden cohort states $\|y_0\|_\infty\le\bar y_0$ is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{p2:eq:driftcost}
\alpha^*-\alpha_{\rm drift}^*\;=\;\alpha^*\,\frac{|I|\bar y_0}{\mathcal B+|I|\bar y_0}.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

At the Table~\ref{p2:tab:certvsid} calibration ($a=0.4$, $\kappa=1$, $\Phi^+=0.4$, $|I|=1$, $\bar u=0.48$, $\mathcal B=1$, $x_0=0$) with $\bar y_0=0.25$, the reset guard admits $\alpha^*=0.3000$ whereas the drift-adjusted guard admits $\alpha_{\rm drift}^*=0.2400$, an exact capacity cost of $0.0600$, and commits zero budget breaches across 500 random multi-cohort economies (\texttt{recompute\_p2\_step2\_theory.py}).

\begin{proposition}[When an eigenvalue rule admits an uncertified level]\label{p2:prop:dominance}
Let an eigenvalue guard admit $\alpha_\rho=\max\{\alpha:\rho(J(\alpha))\le\bar\rho\}$, and suppose the state is feasible for the summed-gain guard, which then admits at least $\alpha=0$. Bounding the one-step price move by $M_C(\alpha)(\|z_t\|_2+s(\alpha)\bar u_{{\rm step},t})$, the level admitted by the eigenvalue guard fails this one-step solvency certificate, and so is one the summed-gain guard would not certify, if and only if
\[
M_C(\alpha_\rho)\;>\;\frac{B_{{\rm rem},t}-R_{\rm safe}-C_{{\rm hedge},t}(\alpha_\rho)-C_{{\rm fill},t}(\alpha_\rho)}{|I^{\rm post}_{t+1}(\alpha_\rho)|(1+\mu)(\|z_t\|_2+s(\alpha_\rho)\bar u_{{\rm step},t})} .
\]
\end{proposition}

The certificate is a bound, so failing it means that solvency cannot be guaranteed, not that a breach must occur. The eigenvalue rule fails it exactly when the transient gain at its admitted level exceeds the loss the balance sheet can absorb, which Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}'s spectral-blindness theorem shows can happen at any spectral threshold.

\begin{proposition}[Strategic re-optimization reinforces the guard]\label{p2:prop:lucas}
Suppose cohorts re-optimize their steady-state Kalman gains when the venue throttles admission, and throttling scales the variance of execution-driven innovations as $\sigma_\zeta^2(\alpha)=\alpha^2\sigma_{\zeta,0}^2$ while measurement noise $\sigma_\nu^2>0$ is unchanged.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item The optimal gain $\eta^*(\alpha)$ is strictly increasing in $\alpha$, so throttling lowers every cohort's adaptation rate. If the transient gain is non-decreasing in the adaptation rates, then $M_C(J(\alpha,\eta^*(\alpha)))\le M_C(J(\alpha,\eta^*(1)))$: re-optimization does not undo the guard.
\item In the positive cohort economy ($q_i\ge0$, $a+\kappa\sum_iq_i<1$) the monotonicity holds without condition: every finite-horizon summed response $S_H=\sum_{t<H}h_t$ is non-decreasing in each adaptation rate, strictly for $H\ge2$ when $q_i>0$, while $S_\infty=\kappa/(1-a-\kappa\sum_iq_i)$ does not depend on the rates. Hence $S_H(J(\alpha,\eta^*(\alpha)))\le S_H(J(\alpha,\eta^*(1)))$ at every horizon.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

Part (i) is a property of the Riccati root. Part (ii) is a renewal argument: the response is the renewal density of a defective inter-arrival law whose cumulative distribution $a+\kappa\sum_iq_i[1-(1-\eta_i)^j]$ rises with every rate at fixed total mass, so faster adaptation moves feedback earlier without changing its total (Appendix~\ref{p2:app:proofs}). In 3{,}000 random positive economies no finite-horizon sum fell when a rate rose (\texttt{hardening}). For the three-state book with momentum the condition in (i) remains an assumption, which we check numerically. Across 3{,}000 stable draws of the three-state book with momentum (adaptation rates on a grid from 0.05 to 0.95), the peak response is non-decreasing in $\eta$ in 98.8\% of draws and in 99.9\% of those with trend-following cohorts ($q>0$); the summed absolute response is non-decreasing in 93\% of draws with $q>0$ but decreasing when cohorts are contrarian ($q<0$). For trend-following cohorts, the case the filtering microfoundation produces, re-optimization therefore reinforces the guard in almost every case we drew; the signed sum $S$ of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum} does not depend on $\eta$ at all (\texttt{recompute\_oct20.py}). A manipulator who instead increases order size to offset throttling enlarges the shock envelope and drives $\alpha$ toward zero.
\section{The value of disclosure for safe trading}\label{p2:sec:disclosure}

Chapter~\ref{ch:p1} measures the value of disclosure in samples needed to identify the cohorts (Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}). For a pre-trade control, the relevant value is the safe trading capacity it unlocks.

\begin{theorem}[Decision value of disclosure]\label{p2:thm:disclosure}
Under the risk contract of Section~\ref{p2:sec:contracts}, compare an observation contract $O$ with a richer contract $O'$ that reveals everything $O$ reveals and more, both with exact data.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{Monotonicity.} The compatible sets are nested, $\Theta_{O'}\subseteq\Theta_O$, so $\Phi^+_{O'}\le\Phi^+_O$ and the admitted level of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robustguard} satisfies $\alpha^*_{O'}\ge\alpha^*_O$.
\item \emph{Strictness.} $\alpha^*_{O'}>\alpha^*_O$ if and only if $\alpha^*_O<1$, $c_I>0$, and $O'$ excludes every compatible economy with $\Phi=\Phi^+_O$. Under the window contract that economy is the upper principal representation of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}: disclosure has decision value exactly when it rules out a cohort at the memory bound with the extremal weight.
\item \emph{Long memory.} Under the window contract with memory bound $\bar r$, $\alpha^*_O\to0$ as $\bar r\to1$ for any fixed window (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}(iii)), whereas audited exposure, which reveals $\Phi$, admits the oracle capacity $\alpha^\circ$. The decision value of audited disclosure therefore tends to the whole oracle capacity as memory lengthens.
\item \emph{Net value.} With value $w$ per unit of admitted capacity and disclosure cost $c(O')$, the net value $w(\alpha^*_{O'}-\alpha^*_O)-c(O')$ is zero or negative whenever $O$ already identifies $\Phi$ (a window whose moment vector lies on the boundary of the moment space) or the budget does not bind.
\item \emph{Noisy proxies.} With noisy data the certificates of two contracts are confidence bounds, not nested sets, and a richer contract can admit less. Under class-tagged flows measured with noise over $T$ periods and estimated as in Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}, class $i$'s exposure $\hat\Phi_i=\hat w_i/(1-\hat r_i)$ has delta-method standard error
\[
{\rm se}(\hat\Phi_i)=\frac{1}{(1-r_i)\sqrt T}\Bigl[V_{ww}+2\Phi_iV_{wr}+\Phi_i^2V_{rr}\Bigr]^{1/2},
\]
with $V$ the asymptotic covariance of $(\hat w_i,\hat r_i)$, so the tagged bound $\sum_i\hat\Phi_i+z_{1-\gamma}{\rm se}$ admits less than the public certificate whenever its margin exceeds $\Phi^+_O-\Phi$. Intersecting the two certificates, each at level $1-\gamma/2$, gives a bound valid at level $1-\gamma$ that admits at least as much as either certificate at level $1-\gamma/2$, which restores monotonicity.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

The theorem is set inclusion plus the closed form of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robustguard}; its content is the location of the value. Disclosure is worth most where public certificates are weakest, in markets whose participants have long memories. In simulation (test T07; Table~\ref{p2:tab:discsim}), with two classes and one of them slow, audited exposure adds 0.027 of admitted capacity to a public certificate when the slow class's memory rate is 0.6 and 0.122 when it is 0.995, out of an oracle capacity of 0.667. Class-tagged flows measured with 10\% noise add 0.02--0.03 at memory rates up to 0.97 but \emph{lower} capacity at 0.995, because a class's exposure $q=w/(1-r)$ is badly conditioned when $r$ is near one: disclosure of a noisy proxy can be worth less than nothing. Part (v) quantifies this. In a second simulation of the same design (test T15), the standard error of the slow class's exposure rises from $0.0004$ to $0.035$ as $1-r$ falls from $0.4$ to $0.005$, roughly in proportion to $(1-r)^{-1}$ over the last decade, while the public certificate's margin grows only with the square root of its horizon. At $r=0.995$ the tagged bound admits $0.506$ against $0.549$ for the public one; the intersection admits $0.537$, close to the better of the two, and covers the true exposure in 99\% of replications (Table~\ref{p2:tab:discsim}). Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}'s evidence that Binance's and the CFTC's class positions pin down memories that prices leave unrestricted concerns class-level positions, which are proxies, not records of individual algorithms.

\paragraph{Pricing versus guarding.} A conservative bound is the right input for a guard and the wrong input for a price. The corrective rebate of Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:equil} depends on $S^2$; computed from an upper bound that overstates $S$ by 10\% it over-subsidizes reversion and loses 2.4--2.8\% of first-best welfare, and by 50\%, 70--80\% (test T12). Guards should use certified upper bounds; rebates should use point estimates.
\section{Who provides reversion? An equilibrium}\label{p2:sec:equil}

Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum} says that transient risk is governed by one ratio, $S=\tilde\kappa/(m-q\tilde\kappa)$. Its ingredients are chosen by market participants: dealers decide how hard to lean against price displacements, which sets reversion $m$, and momentum traders decide how strongly to chase them, which sets $q$. This section asks whether those choices are efficient.

\subsection{Setup}
There are $N$ dealers. Dealer $i$ supplies reversion intensity $s_i\ge0$ by absorbing order flow against the displacement, so $m=m_0+\sum_is_i$, where $m_0$ is reversion supplied by other means. Leaning earns a spread $v$ per unit of intensity at quadratic inventory cost, so the private value is $vs_i-\tfrac c2s_i^2$. By Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} the worst-case transient loss of a position of size $Q$ facing flow shocks bounded by $U$ is $QU\,S$, so we take aggregate expected tail loss to be $\Lambda S(m,q)$ with $\Lambda>0$. Dealers bear a share $\theta<N$ of it, so each bears $\theta/N$; leveraged users and their counterparties bear the rest. Momentum traders choose aggregate intensity $q$ with private value $bq-\tfrac e2q^2$ and bear none of the tail loss. Long-run impact $\tilde\kappa$ is taken as given.

\subsection{Results}

\begin{proposition}[Equilibrium transient risk]\label{p2:prop:equil}
Let $g(m)\equiv-\partial S/\partial m=\tilde\kappa/(m-q\tilde\kappa)^2$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{Under-provision of reversion.} The symmetric Nash equilibrium solves $m-m_0=N v/c+\theta\Lambda g(m)/c$, the planner's optimum solves $m-m_0=N v/c+N\Lambda g(m)/c$, and $m^{\rm eq}<m^{\rm SP}$, so $S^{\rm eq}>S^{\rm SP}$.
\item \emph{Fewer dealers, more risk.} $\partial m^{\rm eq}/\partial N=(v/c)/(1-\theta\Lambda g'(m)/c)>0$, so equilibrium reversion rises and $S^{\rm eq}$ falls with the number of dealers.
\item \emph{Over-provision of momentum.} Momentum traders choose $q=b/e$; the planner chooses $q^{\rm SP}<b/e$, solving $b-eq=\Lambda\tilde\kappa^2/(m-q\tilde\kappa)^2$.
\item \emph{The corrective price is identified.} The first best for reversion is implemented by a rebate per unit of reversion
\[
\tau^*=\Bigl(1-\frac\theta N\Bigr)\Lambda\,g(m)=\Bigl(1-\frac\theta N\Bigr)\Lambda\,\frac{S^2}{\tilde\kappa},
\]
which depends on the market only through $S$ and $\tilde\kappa$.
\item \emph{Endogenous depth.} Suppose in addition that each dealer posts depth $D_i\ge0$ at private value $v_DD_i-\tfrac{c_D}2D_i^2$, that long-run impact is $\tilde\kappa=1/D$ with $D=\sum_iD_i$, and that momentum traders size their flow to the book, $q=\psi D$ with $\psi$ fixed. Then $S=\Gamma/D$ with $\Gamma\equiv S/\tilde\kappa=1/(m-\psi)$, which does not depend on depth. Symmetric equilibrium depth solves $D=Nv_D/c_D+\theta\Lambda\Gamma/(c_DD^2)$ and the planner's $D=Nv_D/c_D+N\Lambda\Gamma/(c_DD^2)$, so $D^{\rm eq}<D^{\rm SP}$; $D^{\rm eq}$ increases with $N$, so $S^{\rm eq}=\Gamma/D^{\rm eq}$ falls with the number of dealers while $S/\tilde\kappa$ does not move. The rebate per unit of depth $\tau_D^*=(1-\theta/N)\Lambda S\tilde\kappa$ implements the first-best depth.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) Dealer $i$'s first-order condition is $v-cs_i+(\theta/N)\Lambda g(m)=0$; the planner's is $v-cs+\Lambda g(m)=0$. Summing over dealers gives the two fixed-point equations. The right-hand sides are decreasing in $m$ because $g$ is decreasing on $m>q\tilde\kappa$, and the planner's lies above the dealers' for every $m$ when $\theta<N$, so its crossing with the line $m-m_0$ lies to the right. (ii) follows by implicit differentiation, since $g'<0$. (iii) $\partial S/\partial q=\tilde\kappa^2/(m-q\tilde\kappa)^2>0$ is the marginal external cost of momentum that traders ignore. (iv) The rebate closes the wedge between the two first-order conditions, and $g=S^2/\tilde\kappa$ by Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum}. (v) With $q\tilde\kappa=\psi$, Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum} gives $S=\tilde\kappa/(m-\psi)=\Gamma/D$ and $\partial S/\partial D_i=-\Gamma/D^2$. Dealer $i$'s first-order condition for depth is $v_D-c_DD_i+(\theta/N)\Lambda\Gamma/D^2=0$ and the planner's $v_D-c_DD_i+\Lambda\Gamma/D^2=0$; summing gives the two fixed points. In each, the left side $D$ increases and the right side decreases in $D$, so each has a unique positive solution, the planner's to the right of the dealers' because $\theta<N$; and $D-\theta\Lambda\Gamma/(c_DD^2)=Nv_D/c_D$ has a left side increasing in $D$, so $D^{\rm eq}$ increases with $N$. The rebate closes the wedge $(1-\theta/N)\Lambda\Gamma/D^2=(1-\theta/N)\Lambda S\tilde\kappa$.
\end{proof}

At $v=0.002$, $c=0.5$, $\theta=0.3$, $\Lambda=10^{-4}$, $m_0=0.005$, $q=0.02$ and $\tilde\kappa=1$, equilibrium $S$ falls from 27.9 with one dealer to 6.8 with forty, and the planner's $S$ is 1.5--2.7 times lower throughout; the rebate computed from $S$ alone coincides with the first-order wedge to machine precision. With $m=0.03$, $b=0.3$ and $e=20$, momentum traders choose $q=0.015$ against a planner's $0.0062$.

\paragraph{Interpretation.} Reversion is a public good: each dealer who leans against a displacement lowers the tail exposure of every leveraged position in the market but is paid only for his own fills. Momentum is the mirror image. Part (iv) closes the loop with the guard: the policy price of the externality is a function of the same summed response that the guard bounds, and Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:invariance} and Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost} show that this quantity is identified from public data without identifying the traders. A venue that rebates reversion in proportion to $S^2/\tilde\kappa$, or a supervisor that sets pre-trade limits by $S$, prices the externality with an observable.

\paragraph{What the data say about the model's predictions.} Part (ii) predicts that books with fewer dealers have higher $S$ because they supply less reversion. Depth is the closest proxy we observe for the number of liquidity providers. Across the three liquidity terciles of the 30 Binance perpetuals on training days, the summed response per dollar of flow rises steeply as depth falls (median $2{,}797$, $26{,}603$ and $39{,}329$ basis points per \$1M in the thick, mid and thin terciles; Spearman correlation with log depth $-0.94$), as (ii) predicts. The channel is not the one part (ii) names, however: the unit-free gain multiple $S/|h_0|$, which equals $1/(m-q\tilde\kappa)$ up to the factor $\tilde\kappa/\kappa$ and isolates reversion net of momentum, is the same in all three terciles (medians $46.4$, $47.3$ and $46.9$; Spearman $-0.11$). Thinner books carry more cumulative risk because their impact $\tilde\kappa$ is larger, not because they revert more slowly. This is the pattern part (v) produces when momentum traders size their flow to the book: fewer dealers lower depth, which raises $S$ in proportion to $\tilde\kappa$, while $S/\tilde\kappa$ stays put. We added part (v) after seeing these comparisons, so the match is a consistency check, not a test. The comparisons were themselves made after the fact (Part 13, \texttt{R2}), and depth is an imperfect proxy for dealer count, so a direct test needs data on liquidity-provider identities. The model's second prediction, that the dynamic balance forecasts tail losses where reversion is close to $q\tilde\kappa$, is what Section~\ref{p2:sec:thin} tests.
\section{Evidence from Binance futures depth}\label{p2:sec:depth}

\subsection{Data and validation}
We use Binance USD-M BTCUSDT perpetual futures from the public archive: one-minute klines with taker-buy volume, \texttt{bookDepth} snapshots of cumulative depth within $\pm1$--$5\%$ of the mid at about 30-second intervals, and open interest from \texttt{metrics}. Depth is available from January 2023. No liquidation archive is available for this period, so we do not test liquidations.

Validation rules were fixed before analysis. A snapshot is kept if (V1) all of the $\pm1,2,5\%$ bands are present, (V2) depth is monotone in distance on each side, (V3) the implied average price at $\pm1\%$ is within 3\% of the one-minute close, and (V4) it is not part of a run of more than ten identical snapshots; (V5) a day is kept if at least 80\% of its minutes have a valid snapshot.

Days passing validation & 1{,}329\\

\quad dropped by V1 (missing bands) & 0\\
\quad dropped by V2 (non-monotone) & 0\\
\quad dropped by V3 (implied price off by $>3\%$) & 98{,}080\\
\quad dropped by V4 (stuck feed) & 95{,}954\\

\subsection{Structural estimates}
On one-minute bars, $p_t$ is the log price in basis points, $u_t$ signed taker flow in million USDT, $d_t=(B_1-A_1)/(B_1+A_1)$ the depth imbalance within 1\% of the mid, and $x_t=p_t-{\rm EMA}_{60}(p_t)$. Day by day we estimate
\begin{align*}
\Delta p_t&=\kappa u_t+\varphi d_{t-1}-(1-a)x_{t-1}+e_t,\\
d_t&=(1-r)d_{t-1}+\varepsilon u_t+e_t,\\
u_t&=(1-\eta)u_{t-1}+\eta q\,x_{t-1}+e_t,
\end{align*}
and form $\hat C_2$, $\hat C_3$ and the implied peak ratio $\max_k\hat h_k/\hat h_0$. The price equation has $\Delta p_t$ on the left, so the EMA filter does not create mechanical persistence. These are reduced-form regressions (flow is contemporaneous), so the estimates describe conditional dynamics rather than causal parameters.

Table~\ref{p2:tab:d3} has three findings. First, the market sits on the stable side of the condition: $\hat C_3<0$ on 93.5\% of days, and the median implied response peaks at impact. Second, the depletion coefficient $\hat\varepsilon$ is small and negative: taker buying is followed by slightly more ask-side than bid-side depth within 1\%, so in these re-centred bands the book appears to replenish rather than deplete the side being hit; Section~\ref{p2:sec:fixedgrid} shows that a fixed price grid reverses the sign. With $\hat\varepsilon<0$, $\varphi\varepsilon$ is positive only if $\varphi$ is negative, which it is not on high-volatility days. Third, on high-volatility days depth imbalance moves prices strongly ($\hat\varphi=2.59$) and refill is twice as fast; the depth channel is active, but it works against amplification. The momentum term $\kappa^2\eta q$ is small (about $0.008$ at the medians), far below $(1-a)\kappa\approx0.022$.

\subsection{Does depth carry an overshoot?}
We estimate local projections \citep{p2:jorda2005} of the cumulative price change on flow, $p_{t+k}-p_{t-1}=\beta_ku_t+\text{controls}$, with five lags of returns, flow and depth: (A) total; (B) adding cumulative future flow, which removes order-splitting persistence; and (C) adding cumulative future depth imbalance, which removes the depth channel. Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak}\ref{p2:thm:t6} predicts an overshoot ($\max_{k\ge1}\beta_k-\beta_0>0$) in (B) that shrinks in (C).

There is no overshoot (Figure~\ref{p2:fig:med}, Table~\ref{p2:tab:d4}). The overshoot statistic is negative in every version. Holding depth fixed makes the response decay faster, consistent with depth that partly sustains the price move but never pushes it past impact.

\subsection{Pre-registered out-of-sample test}
Before opening the test period we registered the specification (SHA-256 \texttt{d95212187ae4\ldots}): hourly blocks (06:00--23:00 UTC); $\hat C_3$ estimated from the previous six hours and winsorized at training percentiles; outcome the next hour's maximum adverse excursion (MAE, bp); controls for realized volatility (1 and 6 hours), absolute return, flow imbalance, volume, depth, imbalance and open-interest change; OLS on 2023--2024 and evaluation on 2025--2026. Success required a one-sided Clark--West $p<0.05$ \emph{and} a day-block bootstrap 95\% CI for $\Delta R^2$ excluding zero. Secondary candidates and outcomes are Holm-adjusted. The test set was opened once. An audit that scrambles all data from $t$ onward left every feature unchanged in 30 of 30 trials.

The primary test fails (Table~\ref{p2:tab:d8}): $\hat C_3$ adds $0.018$ percentage points of out-of-sample $R^2$ with a confidence interval that includes zero. Some secondary effects are statistically significant after Holm correction, notably for depth collapse, but they are economically negligible: no candidate adds more than $0.2$ points of $R^2$ or $0.01$ of AUC.

\paragraph{A volatility flag does better.} We set two flags on training data, each to flag 10\% of hours: high $\hat C_3$ and high realized volatility. On the 909 tail hours of the test period (MAE above the training 90th percentile), the $\hat C_3$ flag has precision $8.6\%$ and recall $19.5\%$; the volatility flag has precision $38.1\%$ and recall $37.1\%$; the union has precision $16.3\%$ and recall $49.4\%$. The unconditional tail rate is about 10\%, so the $\hat C_3$ flag is no better than chance.

\subsection{Crash events}
For each event day we compute $\hat C_3$ hour by hour and its percentile in the training distribution during the three hours before the worst hour (Figure~\ref{p2:fig:events}, Table~\ref{p2:tab:d9}). $\hat C_3$ was elevated before these events but stayed negative throughout, and three events are not a test.

\section{Evidence from spot prices}\label{p2:sec:spot}

\subsection{A unit-free amplification statistic}
The earlier version measured amplification by the norm of powers of a VAR(1) operator on (price deviation, flow), estimated over ten-minute windows of one-second Binance spot data. That norm depends on the units of the state: multiplying prices by 10 and dividing flow by 1,000 changed the raw norm from $8.73$ to $86{,}731$ on the same window. Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:invariance} explains why: state norms are not invariant to coordinates. We therefore standardize the state, $\tilde J=D^{-1}\hat JD$ with $D={\rm diag}({\rm sd}\,x,{\rm sd}\,f)$, and use $M_{\rm std}=\max_k\|e_x^\top\tilde J^k\|$, which is invariant to units (verified). We also report the price-to-price amplification $A_{xx}=\max_k|(\hat J^k)_{11}|$ and a delayed-peak statistic ${\rm hump}=\max_k|(\hat J^k)_{12}|/|\hat J_{12}|$.

\subsection{Crash days and a no-feedback null}
We study five crash days: May 19, 2021; LUNA, May 9, 2022; FTX, November 8, 2022; SVB, March 11, 2023; and August 5, 2024. Windows are selected \emph{ex ante} (previous ten minutes in the top volatility decile), and for comparison by outcome (the ten largest absolute returns). A no-feedback null keeps each window's real flow, generates prices with no feedback, $\Delta p=\hat\kappa f+\hat\sigma e$, and pushes them through the identical pipeline.

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Unit-free amplification on crash days and the no-feedback null}\label{p2:tab:spotnull}
\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
\toprule
Event & Windows (ex ante) & Mean $M_{\rm std}$ & Null 99th pct & $p$ (vs null) & Outcome-selected mean\\
\midrule
May 2021 & 142 & 1.0000 & 1.00002 & 1.00 & 1.0000\\
LUNA 2022 & 57 & 1.00008 & 1.00006 & 0.039 & 1.00045\\
FTX 2022 & 49 & 1.0000 & 1.00029 & 1.00 & 1.0000\\
SVB 2023 & 6 & 1.00006 & 1.00025 & 0.059 & 1.00032\\
Aug 2024 & 108 & 1.0000 & 1.00007 & 1.00 & 1.0000\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item Ten-minute windows of one-second bars. The median hump statistic is 1.000 in every event and its null $p$-value is 1.00. In the ordinary 2021--2023 panel, 0.2\% of windows have $A_{xx}>1$ and the 95th percentile of $M_{\rm std}$ is 1.0000; a handful of near-singular windows produce very large values, so ordinary-window means are not informative. \end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

Only LUNA exceeds the null, by less than $0.01\%$ (Table~\ref{p2:tab:spotnull}). Outcome-selected windows give only marginally larger values ($\le1.0005$). Stress-window means exceed the ordinary 95th percentile in two events, by amounts of the same size. The pre-registered gate fails: unit-free transient amplification in spot data is indistinguishable from one and from the pipeline null. Varying the EMA span (60, 120, 300 seconds) and the bar length (1 or 5 seconds) does not change this; crash-day means range from $1.0003$ to $1.18$ across specifications and are never above the corresponding ordinary means.

\subsection{Overshoot with flow held fixed}
Local projections on one-second data give the price response to a \$1M flow shock (Table~\ref{p2:tab:spotlp}). On ordinary days, with future flow held fixed, there is a small, short-lived overshoot of $+0.248$ [$0.133$, $0.376$] bp per \$1M, peaking at two seconds and decaying after. On crash days the flow-fixed overshoot is negative, $-0.277$ [$-0.528$, $-0.042$]. The large total overshoots ($+0.91$ ordinary, $+2.40$ crash) disappear when future flow is held fixed: they come from order splitting, not from the book.

\midrule

Ordinary, flow fixed & 5.86 & 6.02 & 6.11 & 5.90 & 5.68 & 5.26 & 4.86 & 4.16 & 3.37 & 1.99\\

\subsection{Pre-registered predictive test}
Before opening 2024, we registered (SHA-256 \texttt{b1fd7f8e\ldots}) a test of whether pre-window amplification predicts the next ten minutes' MAE beyond volatility and flow controls, on non-overlapping 30-minute blocks (train 2021--2023, 3,307 blocks; test 2024, 1,104 blocks). It does not: $\Delta R^2=-0.023$ points [$-0.056$, $-0.002$], Clark--West $p=0.966$, AUC $0.822$ in both models. A random feature with the same autocorrelation gives $\Delta R^2=-0.020$ points, the same as the candidate. Leave-one-year-out fits within the training period are unstable (the 2023 fold gives a very large negative $\Delta R^2$ because of outlying candidate values), so the null result does not depend on one year being unusual.

The test set was opened three times. The first evaluations stopped on a numerical error from degenerate windows; the fix drops blocks with non-finite features (five blocks) and is the only change, and the registered specification hash is unchanged. A volatility flag again dominates: precision $43\%$ and recall $30\%$ against $3\%$ and $10\%$ for the amplification flag; the difference in recall has a CI of $[-0.37,0.13]$.

\section{Evidence from thinner books}\label{p2:sec:thin}

The deepest book in crypto is the least likely place to find amplification. This section moves to thinner books. It reports the pre-registered out-of-sample tests in thin Binance books, on Bybit and in thin US stocks, failures included, and labels every check decided after a test set was opened as post hoc. Figure~\ref{p2:fig:forest} collects all out-of-sample evaluations of this section.

\subsection{Design}\label{p2:sec:thin_design}
We fixed 30 Binance USD-M perpetuals from the quote-volume ranking of one training day (June 10, 2024) before computing any T6 statistic, spread from BTCUSDT to symbols with under \$5M daily volume. Data are sampled on the 10th and 24th of each month from January 2023 to September 2026 and pass the validation rules V1--V5 of Section~\ref{p2:sec:depth}: 2{,}565 of 2{,}700 symbol-days are valid; of 7{,}486{,}444 snapshots, V3 drops 212{,}745 and V4 drops 161{,}280, and V1 and V2 drop none. Liquidity terciles are fixed on median training-period depth within 1\% of the mid. The thin tercile has \$0.22M--\$0.38M of depth within 1\%, the thick tercile \$0.97M--\$220M. Flow is divided by each symbol's median absolute one-minute training flow, so that $\hat\kappa$ is in basis points per typical minute of flow. All other definitions follow Section~\ref{p2:sec:depth}. An audit that scrambles all data from $t$ onward left every hourly feature unchanged in 30 of 30 trials.

\subsection{The cross-section of the amplification condition}

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{T6 estimates by liquidity tercile, 30 USD-M perpetuals, training days 2023--2024}\label{p2:tab:x3}
\small\setlength{\tabcolsep}{5pt}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
 & Thick & Mid & Thin\\
\midrule
Mean $\hat C_3$ & $-0.0297$ & $-0.0216$ & $-0.0324$\\
\quad 95\% CI & [$-0.0341$, $-0.0257$] & [$-0.0250$, $-0.0185$] & [$-0.0386$, $-0.0268$]\\
Share of symbol-days with $\hat C_3>0$ & 9.4\% & 15.2\% & 16.1\%\\
Median $\hat\varphi$ & 2.33 & 2.38 & 3.11\\
Median $\hat\varepsilon$ & $-0.0014$ & $-0.0006$ & $-0.0005$\\
Share with $\hat\varepsilon>0$ & 11.1\% & 24.4\% & 30.1\%\\
Mean feedback loop gain $\hat Q$ & 0.0091 & 0.0033 & 0.0016\\
\midrule
\multicolumn{4}{l}{Spearman correlation with log depth across the 30 symbols [95\% CI]}\\
\quad mean $\hat C_3$ & \multicolumn{3}{c}{$-0.001$ [$-0.37$, $0.38$]}\\
\quad share $\hat C_3>0$ & \multicolumn{3}{c}{$-0.59$ [$-0.81$, $-0.27$]}\\
\quad $\hat Q$ & \multicolumn{3}{c}{$+0.70$ [$0.41$, $0.87$]}\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item 1{,}424 training symbol-days, ten symbols per tercile. CIs from a symbol-day bootstrap (tercile rows) and a symbol bootstrap (correlations). $\hat Q$ is the loop gain of Chapter~\ref{ch:p1} estimated on one-minute bars; a placebo random walk with each day's volatility gives $\hat Q\approx0$ for every symbol. \end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

Table~\ref{p2:tab:x3} confirms the BTCUSDT result across liquidity: mean $\hat C_3$ is negative in every tercile, and taker flow on average does not deplete the side it hits ($\hat\varepsilon<0$ at the median everywhere). What changes with depth is the frequency of the amplifying regime, not its average. The share of symbol-days with $\hat C_3>0$ rises from 9.4\% in the thick tercile to 16.1\% in the thin tercile, with a Spearman correlation of $-0.59$ against log depth. Depth sensitivity $\hat\varphi$ is larger, and depletion is less negative and more often positive, in thinner books. We had predicted a negative correlation between log depth and mean $\hat C_3$; that prediction fails ($\rho=-0.001$).

The last row is an unexpected finding for Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}. Measured feedback $\hat Q$ is larger in \emph{deeper} books ($\rho=+0.70$): flow responds to price deviations most where liquidity, and plausibly algorithmic participation, is greatest. Feedback and amplification are therefore different objects. Deep books have more feedback and refill fast enough to absorb it; thin books have less feedback and, more often, too little reversion.

\subsection{Overshoot and depletion}

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Local-projection overshoot by tercile, $\max_{k\ge1}\beta_k-\beta_0$ [95\% CI]}\label{p2:tab:x4}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}

 & Thick & Mid & Thin\\
\midrule
(A) total & $-0.045$ [$-0.079$, $0.047$] & $-0.034$ [$-0.054$, $0.024$] & $+0.007$ [$-0.050$, $0.083$]\\
(B) future flow fixed & $-0.181$ [$-0.211$, $-0.152$] & $-0.075$ [$-0.091$, $-0.058$] & $-0.073$ [$-0.094$, $-0.051$]\\

With future flow held fixed there is no overshoot in any tercile (Table~\ref{p2:tab:x4}); the registered prediction of a positive (B) overshoot in thin books fails. The response decays less in thin than in thick books, and holding depth fixed again makes it decay faster, as in Table~\ref{p2:tab:d4}.

To check that replenishment is not an artefact of one-minute aggregation, we sum signed taker flow exactly between consecutive depth snapshots from trade-level data and estimate depletion $\varepsilon_{30}$ at about 30 seconds, with an additional coefficient on sweeps (intervals in the top decile of absolute flow). Table~\ref{p2:tab:x8} reports five symbols on six training days each.

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Depletion at the snapshot scale (about 30 seconds)}\label{p2:tab:x8}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}
\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
\toprule
Symbol & Tercile & $\hat\varepsilon_{30}$ [95\% CI] & Sweep extra [95\% CI] & $\hat\varepsilon$, 1 minute & Days $\hat\varepsilon_{30}>0$\\
\midrule
BTCUSDT & thick & $-0.0045$ [$-0.0058$, $-0.0033$] & $+0.0034$ [$0.0023$, $0.0046$] & $-0.0025$ & 0 of 6\\
XMRUSDT & mid & $-0.0042$ [$-0.0058$, $-0.0029$] & $+0.0037$ [$0.0025$, $0.0051$] & $-0.0011$ & 0 of 6\\
RLCUSDT & thin & $-0.0034$ [$-0.0065$, $-0.0012$] & $+0.0029$ [$0.0011$, $0.0058$] & $-0.0003$ & 1 of 6\\
CELRUSDT & thin & $-0.0015$ [$-0.0032$, $0.0001$] & $+0.0017$ [$0.0004$, $0.0031$] & $-0.0004$ & 2 of 6\\
CVXUSDT & thin & $+0.0010$ [$-0.0033$, $0.0060$] & $-0.0003$ [$-0.0040$, $0.0027$] & $+0.0004$ & 2 of 6\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item Day bootstrap over six training days per symbol. Depth is measured in bands of $\pm1\%$ around the current mid, so a price move also recenters the band; this biases $\hat\varepsilon_{30}$ in an unknown direction. \end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

In these re-centred bands, replenishment is stronger, not weaker, at 30 seconds: $\hat\varepsilon_{30}$ is negative with a CI below zero in three of five symbols and is never significantly positive. Sweeps offset most of it, so a sweep leaves the hit side about as deep as before ($\hat\varepsilon_{30}$ plus the sweep coefficient lies between $-0.0012$ and $+0.0007$). On this measure, the depletion channel that drives Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak}\ref{p2:thm:t6} would be absent even in thin books; the next subsection shows that the measure itself is biased toward replenishment.

\subsection{Depletion on a fixed price grid}\label{p2:sec:fixedgrid}
Binance's depth archive reports depth within bands around the \emph{current} mid. After a buy moves the mid up, the ask band shifts into deeper resting orders and the bid band away from resting bids, which mechanically makes the book look ask-heavy after a buy, the sign we estimate. Bybit's order-book files contain every level, so the band can be held fixed at the previous mid. After seeing Table~\ref{p2:tab:x8} we computed, for the six coins of Table~\ref{p2:tab:bybitob} on four days each, the change in depth imbalance over 30-second intervals regressed on signed taker flow, once with the band fixed at the start-of-interval mid and once re-centred at the end-of-interval mid, Binance's construction.

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Depletion at 30 seconds with a fixed and a re-centred band (Bybit order books, post hoc)}\label{p2:tab:fixedgrid}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
Band & $\hat\varepsilon_{30}$ [95\% CI] & Share $>0$ & Coin-days\\
\midrule
Fixed at previous mid & $+0.0021$ [$+0.0013$, $+0.0032$] & 95\% & 20\\
Re-centred (Binance) & $-0.0004$ [$-0.0007$, $-0.0001$] & 30\% & 20\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item ACH, ALICE, API3, LIT, WOO and ZEN, May 11--12 and September 11--12, 2025; flow normalized by each coin-day's median absolute 30-second flow; mean over coin-days with a bootstrap interval. Post hoc. Kind: real data (Part 13, \texttt{R5}).\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The sign reverses (Table~\ref{p2:tab:fixedgrid}). With the band fixed, buying depletes the ask side in 19 of 20 coin-days; with the band re-centred, the same data reproduce the replenishment of Tables~\ref{p2:tab:d3} and~\ref{p2:tab:x8}. The replenishment finding is therefore an artefact of band recentring, and depletion is present. Two conclusions survive. First, local projections show no peak overshoot once future flow is held fixed (Table~\ref{p2:tab:x4}), so depletion is not large enough to satisfy $\varphi\varepsilon>(1-a)\kappa$ in the data we observe; second, the depth term carries no out-of-sample forecasting content (Tables~\ref{p2:tab:y6} and~\ref{p2:tab:z6}). Both statements rest on Binance's re-centred depth measure, and a fixed-grid measure of $\varphi\varepsilon$ in the same books would require order-level Binance data that are not public.

\subsection{Pre-registered test in thin books}\label{p2:sec:x6}
Before opening 2025--2026 for these symbols we registered the specification (SHA-256 \texttt{5910b6b4d90a\ldots}). It is identical to Section~\ref{p2:sec:depth} except for three changes, all fixed in advance: the primary sample is the thin tercile; the regressions include symbol fixed effects; and the bootstrap resamples dates, with all symbols on a date kept together. The test cell was executed twice in the same session. The specification hash, the code and the data were unchanged between the two runs, and the evaluation is deterministic, so both runs give the numbers below; we report the count because the protocol requires it.

The pre-registered test passes. Holm $p$ is the Clark--West $p$-value adjusted across the six secondary tests. The primary test passes (Table~\ref{p2:tab:x6}). In thin books, $\hat C_3$ estimated from the previous six hours adds $1.2$ points of out-of-sample $R^2$ for the next hour's maximum adverse excursion, beyond realized volatility, flow, volume, depth, imbalance and open interest, and raises the AUC for tail hours by $0.012$. The effect is confined to thin books: in the mid and thick terciles it is zero, and in the pooled sample the Clark--West test rejects but the $\Delta R^2$ interval contains zero. $\hat C_3$ also helps classify hours with a large open-interest drop, but it predicts depth collapse \emph{worse} than the baseline, again pointing away from the depth channel.

\paragraph{Flags.} On training data we set a $\hat C_3$ flag and a volatility flag; 575 of 6{,}521 test hours (8.8\%) are tail hours. The $\hat C_3$ flag fires in 8.9\% of hours with precision 16.5\% and recall 16.7\%, about twice the base rate. The volatility flag, which fires more often (16.0\%), does better (precision 23.4\%, recall 42.4\%), and the union reaches recall 51.1\% at precision 19.5\%. $\hat C_3$ is a useful addition to volatility in thin books, not a replacement for it.

\subsection{Tick size: a caution and a robustness check}\label{p2:sec:tick}

\paragraph{Tick changes.} We located tick changes by inferring each day's price grid and dating changes by binary search over daily files. We found 12 tick reductions, all by a factor of ten, on 11 symbols (GMTUSDT twice) between August 2023 and August 2026; nine fall in the test period. A difference-in-differences design compares 10 days before and after each event with four control symbols of similar liquidity. After a tick cut, $\hat C_3$ rises with a CI above zero in 10 of 12 events, but realized volatility in basis points falls sharply at the same time (by up to 32 bp on ALICEUSDT), and the implied peak ratio does not change in any event (every CI includes zero). The Spearman correlation between the change in refill $\hat r$ and the change in the peak ratio is $-0.04$. We read this as a measurement result, not a test of the theory: at a coarse tick, prices move in discrete steps and the basis-point scale of $\hat C_3$ changes mechanically when the grid changes. The natural experiment cannot test the prediction that faster refill lowers the overshoot.

\paragraph{Does tick size drive Table~\ref{p2:tab:x6}?} Eleven of the 30 symbols, six of them thin, changed tick, and five of those six did so inside the test period. After seeing Table~\ref{p2:tab:x6} we therefore ran the post-hoc checks of Table~\ref{p2:tab:x6r}, counted separately (one evaluation).

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Post-hoc tick-size robustness of the thin-book result}\label{p2:tab:x6r}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.34\textwidth}cccc}
\toprule
Check & Symbols & Test hours & $\Delta R^2$ (pts) [95\% CI] & Clark--West $p$\\
\midrule
R0 baseline (Table~\ref{p2:tab:x6}) & 10 & 6{,}521 & $+1.216$ [$+0.295$, $+8.017$] & $<0.001$\\
R1 + relative tick size and its log & 10 & 6{,}485 & $+1.243$ [$+0.373$, $+7.627$] & $<0.001$\\
R2 drop symbols with any tick change & 4 & 2{,}639 & $+0.511$ [$-0.069$, $+2.061$] & $<0.001$\\
R3 only days with the training tick & 10 & 5{,}642 & $+1.131$ [$+0.256$, $+9.305$] & $<0.001$\\
R1 and R3 together & 10 & 5{,}642 & $+1.222$ [$+0.357$, $+9.410$] & $<0.001$\\
R4 outcome in ticks & 10 & 6{,}485 & $-0.127$ [$-0.319$, $-0.018$] & 0.991\\
R5 $\hat C_3$ percentile within symbol & 10 & 6{,}521 & $+0.158$ [$-0.013$, $+1.322$] & 0.026\\
R6 leave one symbol out (10 fits) & 9 & & $+0.698$ to $+1.760$, all CIs $>0$ & all $<0.001$\\
\bottomrule

\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item Post hoc: decided after Table~\ref{p2:tab:x6}. 500 date-block bootstrap draws (200 in R6). \end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}

The result survives controls for tick size, the removal of every day traded at a tick different from training, and the removal of any single symbol (Table~\ref{p2:tab:x6r}). It weakens when the six thin symbols that ever changed tick are dropped: four remain, and the point estimate halves with an interval that just includes zero. It disappears when the outcome is measured in ticks; at each tick cut this outcome jumps tenfold, a break the training fit cannot anticipate, so we regard R4 as a weak test, but we report it. A scale-free version of the candidate (its percentile within each symbol's training distribution) is weak. Our reading is that $\hat C_3$'s forecasting content is tied to its \emph{level}, which mixes the amplification balance with the basis-point scale of each book. Sections~\ref{p2:sec:y6} and~\ref{p2:sec:y8} test whether that level is more than a measure of scale.

\subsection{Out-of-sample replication on new symbols}\label{p2:sec:y6}
A result found in ten symbols could be specific to them. We therefore drew 24 new symbols, seeded and before any analysis, from the 72 USD-M perpetuals that were not in the original 30 and whose training-day volume was at most 1.5 times that of the most liquid thin symbol. We applied the same validation (2{,}090 of 2{,}160 symbol-days valid) and classified symbols as thin with a cut fixed in advance at the boundary between the original thin and mid terciles (\$0.39M of depth within 1\%). Ten new symbols are thin (ACH, ARPA, CHR, GTC, HOT, IOTX, OGN, SPELL, T and ZEN, all USDT-margined) and 14 are not. A new specification (SHA-256 \texttt{6aa8e2c03d79\ldots}) fixed the primary test identical to Table~\ref{p2:tab:x6} and, as secondary tests, the three terms of $C_3$ separately, a unit-free ratio $A=(\varphi\varepsilon+\kappa^2\eta q)/((1-a)\kappa)-1$ that is positive exactly when $C_3>0$, and the not-thin group. The test set was opened once.
