\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The result replicates (Table~\ref{p2:tab:y6}). On symbols never used before, $\hat C_3$ adds $0.85$ points of out-of-sample $R^2$ with an interval excluding zero, and improves tail classification. It again has no value outside thin books. The decomposition shows where the forecasting content lies, and every sign matches Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak}\ref{p2:thm:t6} and Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:peak}\ref{p2:prop:t6m}. A larger momentum term predicts larger adverse excursions; a larger reversion term, which pushes $C_3$ down, predicts smaller ones; and the depth term, whose depletion factor is close to zero (Table~\ref{p2:tab:x8}), predicts nothing. In training data the reversion term is by far the largest component (median $0.033$, against $-0.0009$ for momentum and $-0.0003$ for depth), so most of the variation in $\hat C_3$ is variation in impact-weighted mean reversion.

The unit-free ratio fails. Because the amplifying terms are small, its median is $-0.996$ and its variation is mostly the noise of a ratio with an estimated denominator. Together with R5 in Table~\ref{p2:tab:x6r}, this means that on these samples the forecasting content was tied to the level of $\hat C_3$. Section~\ref{p2:sec:z6} tests two better-behaved unit-free statistics on fresh data.

\subsection{Is $\hat C_3$ just price impact?}\label{p2:sec:y8}
Both working terms contain the impact coefficient $\kappa$, so $\hat C_3$ might forecast excursions only because it measures illiquidity. After seeing Table~\ref{p2:tab:y6} we added four impact controls, all from the same past six hours or hour and winsorized at training percentiles: $\hat\kappa$ from the same fit, $\hat\kappa^2$, a univariate Kyle's $\lambda$ \citep{p2:kyle1985} (slope of returns on signed flow) and a log Amihud ratio \citep{p2:amihud2002} (absolute return over volume). This is a post-hoc check, evaluated once and counted separately.

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Post-hoc price-impact controls, ten new thin symbols, 2025--2026}\label{p2:tab:y8}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
Candidate $\mid$ added controls & $\Delta R^2$ (pts) [95\% CI] & $\Delta$AUC [95\% CI] & Clark--West $t$\\
\midrule
$\hat C_3$ $\mid$ none (Table~\ref{p2:tab:y6}) & $+0.845$ [$+0.303$, $+6.371$] & $+0.008$ [$+0.002$, $+0.013$] & 11.66\\
$\hat C_3$ $\mid$ $\hat\kappa$, $\hat\kappa^2$ & $+0.755$ [$+0.371$, $+4.718$] & $+0.006$ [$-0.000$, $+0.010$] & 6.63\\
$\hat C_3$ $\mid$ Kyle's $\lambda$, Amihud & $+1.089$ [$+0.498$, $+7.149$] & $+0.007$ [$+0.001$, $+0.013$] & 8.02\\
$\hat C_3$ $\mid$ all four & $+0.682$ [$+0.334$, $+4.525$] & $+0.004$ [$-0.001$, $+0.009$] & 5.69\\
Momentum term $\mid$ all four & $+0.487$ [$+0.311$, $+2.384$] & $+0.002$ [$-0.002$, $+0.006$] & 3.42\\
Reversion term $\mid$ all four & $+0.529$ [$+0.222$, $+3.762$] & $+0.005$ [$-0.000$, $+0.009$] & 8.45\\
$\hat\kappa$ alone $\mid$ none & $-0.026$ [$-0.084$, $+0.030$] & $+0.000$ [$-0.000$, $+0.000$] & $-1.77$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item Post hoc: decided after Table~\ref{p2:tab:y6}. Same evaluation code and bootstrap as Table~\ref{p2:tab:y6}; Holm-adjusted $p<0.001$ for every row except $\hat\kappa$ alone ($p=0.96$). Training correlations of $\hat C_3$ with the controls: $\hat\kappa$ $-0.43$, Kyle's $\lambda$ $-0.44$, Amihud $+0.10$. \end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

$\hat C_3$ is not a proxy for impact (Table~\ref{p2:tab:y8}). With all four impact controls it keeps about 80\% of its gain in $R^2$ ($0.68$ of $0.85$ points), with an interval above zero, and both the momentum and the reversion terms survive separately. Impact on its own forecasts nothing. Controlling for Kyle's $\lambda$ and Amihud alone \emph{raises} the gain to $1.09$ points: once plain illiquidity is held fixed, the dynamic balance that $\hat C_3$ measures stands out more clearly. One qualification: with $\hat\kappa$ controlled, the gain in tail \emph{classification} is no longer significant. The robust claim is about the size of the next hour's adverse excursion, not about flagging tail hours beyond what impact already flags.

\subsection{A third test on fresh days}\label{p2:sec:z6}
Parts 4 and 5 of the replication package use the 10th and 24th of each month. For a third test we registered a new specification (SHA-256 \texttt{65acda361861\ldots}) before downloading the 3rd and 17th of each month of 2025--2026, days never used in any analysis. Training reuses the 2023--2024 days of the earlier tests. The sample is all 20 thin symbols of Sections~\ref{p2:sec:x6} and~\ref{p2:sec:y6}, with the ten mid-tercile symbols as a negative control. Two changes make the test harder: the four price-impact controls of Table~\ref{p2:tab:y8} are now part of the baseline, and because the specification has two primary hypotheses (this one and the backtest of Section~\ref{p2:sec:backtest}) each is tested at $\alpha=0.025$ with 97.5\% intervals. The test set was opened once; 1{,}137 fresh symbol-days passed validation.

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Pre-registered test on fresh days, 2025--2026 (impact controls in the baseline)}\label{p2:tab:z6}
\small\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}
\begin{tabular}{lp{0.27\textwidth}ccc}
\toprule
Sample & Candidate & $\Delta R^2$ (pts) [CI] & $\Delta$AUC [95\% CI] & Holm $p$\\
\midrule
Thin (primary) & $\hat C_3$ & $+1.583$ [$+0.588$, $+3.237$] & $+0.002$ [$-0.003$, $+0.006$] & ---\\
Thin & reversion term & $+1.442$ [$+0.679$, $+2.621$] & $+0.003$ [$-0.001$, $+0.006$] & $<0.001$\\
Thin & momentum term & $+0.422$ [$+0.000$, $+1.063$] & $-0.002$ [$-0.005$, $+0.001$] & $<0.001$\\
Thin & depth term & $+0.323$ [$+0.092$, $+0.639$] & $+0.001$ [$-0.001$, $+0.003$] & $<0.001$\\

Thin & log reversion half-life & $+0.619$ [$+0.351$, $+1.031$] & $+0.001$ [$+0.000$, $+0.003$] & $<0.001$\\
Thin & $\hat C_3$ without depth, $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ & $+1.747$ [$+0.725$, $+3.393$] & $+0.001$ [$-0.004$, $+0.006$] & $<0.001$\\
Mid (control) & $\hat C_3$ & $+0.055$ [$-0.047$, $+0.239$] & $+0.000$ [$-0.001$, $+0.001$] & 0.038\\
\bottomrule

\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item Primary: train 16{,}860 and test 13{,}428 symbol-hours on 39 test dates; 97.5\% date-block bootstrap interval; Clark--West $t=9.46$, one-sided $p<0.001$; baseline out-of-sample $R^2=0.185$; training coefficient $+68.0$. The pre-registered test passes. Secondary rows: 95\% intervals, Holm-adjusted Clark--West $p$ across the eleven secondary tests of the specification. Thin: 20 symbols; mid: 10. The gain multiple and the half-life are unit-free. $S_{60}$ is the summed absolute impulse response over 60 minutes; $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}=\hat\kappa^2\hat\eta\hat q-(1-\hat a)\hat\kappa$ from the model without depth. \end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The result holds a third time, and more strongly (Table~\ref{p2:tab:z6}). On fresh days, with impact already in the baseline, $\hat C_3$ adds $1.6$ points of $R^2$ for the next hour's adverse excursion in thin books. Three findings sharpen the earlier picture. First, two unit-free statistics now work: the gain multiple, the summed response divided by impact, and the log half-life of reversion both add about half a point of $R^2$ with intervals above zero. The scale concern of Section~\ref{p2:sec:tick} is therefore answered: the content is the dynamic balance, not the basis-point scale of the book, in the sense of the invariance hypotheses of \citet{p2:kyle2016}. Second, the reversion term carries most of the signal, as Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum} suggests, since $S$ depends on reversion through $1/(m-q\tilde\kappa)$. Third, the depth-free statistic forecasts as well as the full one, which matters for the next subsection. In mid-depth books the Clark--West test rejects at the Holm-adjusted 5\% level, but the $R^2$ gain is $0.06$ points with an interval that includes zero. As with the impact controls, the gain in tail \emph{classification} is not significant: the robust claim is about the size of excursions.

\subsection{Beyond volatility forecasting}\label{p2:sec:z9}
The adverse excursion of the next hour is close to a volatility outcome, so a natural objection is that $\hat C_3$ only proxies for future volatility in a way the simple volatility controls miss. We registered a fourth test (SHA-256 \texttt{71983f1a9d2c\ldots}) on the 6th and 20th of each month of 2025--2026, days not used in any other test, whose baseline adds the standard tools of volatility forecasting to every control used so far: realized volatility over 15 minutes, one hour, six hours and since midnight in the spirit of HAR models \citep{p2:corsi2009}, an exponentially weighted (RiskMetrics) volatility forecast \citep{p2:riskmetrics1996}, the previous hour's adverse excursion, and hour-of-day fixed effects, together with the four price-impact controls. The test set was opened once; 1{,}161 fresh symbol-days passed validation.

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Pre-registered test beyond a volatility-forecasting baseline, fresh days 2025--2026}\label{p2:tab:z9}
\small\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}
\begin{tabular}{lp{0.27\textwidth}ccc}
\toprule
Sample & Candidate & $\Delta R^2$ (pts) [95\% CI] & $\Delta$AUC [95\% CI] & Holm $p$\\
\midrule
Thin (primary) & $\hat C_3$ & $+2.787$ [$+1.425$, $+4.944$] & $+0.005$ [$+0.002$, $+0.008$] & ---\\
Thin & summed response $S_{60}$ & $+2.098$ [$+1.165$, $+3.597$] & $+0.004$ [$+0.001$, $+0.006$] & $<0.001$\\
Thin & reversion term & $+2.451$ [$+1.257$, $+4.379$] & $+0.004$ [$+0.001$, $+0.007$] & $<0.001$\\
Thin & momentum term & $+0.731$ [$+0.238$, $+1.429$] & $+0.001$ [$-0.001$, $+0.002$] & $<0.001$\\
Thin & gain multiple $S_{60}/|\hat h_0|$ & $+0.563$ [$+0.259$, $+1.003$] & $+0.001$ [$-0.001$, $+0.002$] & $<0.001$\\
Thin & log reversion half-life & $+0.806$ [$+0.446$, $+1.351$] & $+0.001$ [$-0.000$, $+0.002$] & $<0.001$\\
Thin & $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ (no depth) & $+2.989$ [$+1.453$, $+5.424$] & $+0.005$ [$+0.002$, $+0.009$] & $<0.001$\\
Mid (control) & $\hat C_3$ & $+0.316$ [$+0.108$, $+0.635$] & $+0.002$ [$+0.001$, $+0.004$] & $<0.001$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item Thin: 20 symbols, train 16{,}860 and test 13{,}722 symbol-hours on 40 test dates; mid: 10 symbols. Primary: Clark--West $t=12.59$, one-sided $p<0.001$; baseline out-of-sample $R^2=0.186$, full $0.214$; AUC $0.826\to0.831$. The pre-registered test passes. Baseline: the controls of Table~\ref{p2:tab:z6}, the four impact controls, realized volatility over 15 minutes and since midnight, an EWMA volatility forecast ($\lambda=0.94$), the previous hour's adverse excursion, hour-of-day fixed effects and symbol fixed effects. The gain multiple and the half-life are unit-free. Kind: real data.\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The result does not weaken against a volatility-forecasting baseline; it strengthens (Table~\ref{p2:tab:z9}). $\hat C_3$ adds $2.8$ points of out-of-sample $R^2$ on top of a baseline that already explains $18.6\%$, and for the first time it also improves the classification of tail hours with an interval excluding zero. The summed response $S_{60}$, the statistic that the guard bounds and that Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum} identifies as cumulative transient risk, adds $2.1$ points on its own. The volatility benchmarks, by contrast, add nothing out of sample beyond the controls of Table~\ref{p2:tab:z6} ($-0.76$ points, interval $[-3.38,+2.10]$), so the forecasting content of the dynamic balance is not a volatility effect that richer volatility models would have captured. In mid-depth books the gain is significant in this test but small, about a ninth of the thin-book gain; across the four tests with a deeper-book control it was significant once.

\paragraph{Against VPIN.} The best-known early-warning measure for flow-driven crashes is VPIN \citep{p2:easley2012}. After the fact, on the same test hours (Part 13, \texttt{R8}), we computed VPIN from the previous six hours, the window of $\hat C_3$, as the mean absolute buy--sell imbalance over 50 equal-volume buckets; Binance reports taker-buy volume, so the imbalance is measured rather than classified. VPIN adds $1.2$ points of $R^2$ to the baseline (95\% interval $[0.4,2.3]$). $\hat C_3$ added to the baseline \emph{and} VPIN still adds $2.0$ points ($[1.0,3.4]$, Clark--West $t=12.1$) and improves tail classification, keeping about 73\% of its $2.8$-point gain. The two are negatively related (Spearman $-0.35$), and VPIN's training coefficient is negative, so high imbalance precedes smaller, not larger, excursions in these books. This comparison is post hoc and on a test set already opened; it shows that $\hat C_3$ is not a repackaging of order-flow toxicity.

\subsection{A second venue: Bybit}\label{p2:sec:bybit}
To ask whether the result is specific to Binance we registered a test on Bybit USDT perpetuals of the same 20 coins (SHA-256 recorded in the package), using Bybit's public trade archive, which has no depth history. Fourteen of the coins have Bybit data across 2023--2026. Minute bars are built from trades (last price, signed taker notional), training uses the 10th and 24th of each month of 2023--2024, and testing uses the 3rd, 10th, 17th and 24th of each month of 2025--2026 (10{,}764 test hours). The statistic is $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$, which Table~\ref{p2:tab:z6} shows to work on Binance.

The test fails without ambiguity. $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ adds $-0.0000$ points of $R^2$ (95\% CI $[-0.001,0.001]$, Clark--West $p=0.50$), and neither the momentum nor the reversion term helps (Holm $p=1.00$). In training data the momentum coefficient even has the opposite sign ($-130$, against $+110$ on Binance). Because the depth-free statistic works on Binance on the same coins, the failure concerns the venue rather than the statistic. We considered two candidate reasons and checked the first after the fact: bid--ask bounce in Bybit's last-trade prices, and different participants. On the same 14 coins and training days, Bybit's one-minute returns are \emph{less} negatively autocorrelated than Binance's (median $-0.016$ against $-0.031$), so bounce is not the difference; trading is: 11\% of Bybit minutes have no trade, against 0.1\% on Binance, and 29\% of Bybit minutes have a zero return, against 14\% (Part 13, \texttt{R3}). Sparse trading leaves the flow and price series that the statistic is estimated from much noisier on Bybit.


\paragraph{Bybit with order-book depth.} Bybit also distributes daily order-book files for its perpetuals (every update to the top 200 or 500 price levels). We registered a second Bybit test (SHA-256 \texttt{ec2145fac5f5\ldots}) before downloading any test day, with the full Part 9 baseline, depth within 1\% of the mid rebuilt from the order book at the end of each minute, and only the best 200 levels used so that the 2024 (500-level) and 2025--2026 (200-level) files are measured identically. Days were the 11th to 16th of a month, never used in any earlier test: two training months in 2024 and six test months from January 2025 to September 2026. A day is valid if at least 80\% of its minutes have trades and a valid book; sparse trading in thin coins on Bybit leaves 581 of 882 coin-days valid, and the inclusion rule (at least 8 valid training days and 24 valid test days) keeps six coins (ACH, ALICE, API3, LIT, WOO, ZEN; 2{,}988 test hours on 36 dates).

\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
Candidate & $\Delta R^2$ (pts) [95\% CI] & Clark--West $t$ & Holm $p$\\
\midrule
$\hat C_3$ (primary) & $+0.033$ [$+0.019$, $+0.053$] & 5.73 & ---\\
Momentum term & $+0.083$ [$+0.017$, $+0.168$] & 2.94 & 0.010\\
$\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ (no depth) & $+0.064$ [$-0.009$, $+0.152$] & 2.61 & 0.023\\
Summed response $S_{60}$ & $+0.021$ [$-0.025$, $+0.071$] & 1.31 & 0.383\\

Median depth within 1\% on training days \$0.30M--\$0.43M. The pre-registered criterion is met. Specification SHA-256 \texttt{ec2145fac5f5\ldots}; test opened once. The pre-registered criterion is met, but the effect is negligible (Table~\ref{p2:tab:bybitob}). $\hat C_3$ adds $0.033$ points of $R^2$ with an interval above zero and a Clark--West $t$ of $5.7$: statistically clear, economically about one eightieth of the Binance gain, with no improvement in tail classification and a training coefficient one sixteenth as large. Of the components, only the momentum term helps; the reversion term, which carries the Binance result, does not. Two features of the sample may explain part of the gap, and we test neither: the six included Bybit books carry \$0.30M--\$0.43M within 1\%, at or above the boundary between Binance's thin and mid terciles, where the Binance effect was also small; and the inclusion rule removed the sparsest books. We read the two Bybit tests together: with trades alone the statistic has no content on Bybit, and with depth its content is detectable but too small to matter. Section~\ref{p2:sec:us} asks the same question in a regulated market.

\subsection{A regulated market: thin US stocks}\label{p2:sec:us}
The Bybit tests leave open whether the result belongs to thin markets or to one crypto venue. We therefore registered a test in US equities (SHA-256 \texttt{f5800a69c219\ldots}) before downloading any test day. Data are consolidated (SIP) trades from the Alpaca market-data API, aggregated into minute bars of the regular session: last trade price, dollar volume and dollar flow signed by the tick rule. The data contain no depth, so the candidate is the depth-free statistic $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$, the version that worked on Binance (Table~\ref{p2:tab:z6}). The universe was drawn by a seeded rule from June 2024 daily bars, before any test data: 40 thin names (price \$5--\$100, median daily dollar volume \$2M--\$10M) and 10 mid-size controls (\$50M--\$200M). Forecasts are made 120, 180, 240 and 300 minutes after the open from the preceding 120 minutes, and the outcome is the maximum adverse excursion over the next 60 minutes; the baseline is the full volatility-forecasting and price-impact baseline of Table~\ref{p2:tab:z9}. Training uses 48 days in 2023--2024 (the 10th and 24th of each month or the next trading day) and testing 42 days from January 2025 to September 2026 (the 6th and 20th). A day is valid if at least 80\% of its minutes have a trade, and a name enters if it has at least 30 valid training days and 25 valid test days, which keeps 23 of the 40 thin names and all 10 controls. The test cell was run once.

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Pre-registered test in thin US stocks, 2025--2026}\label{p2:tab:us}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
Candidate & $\Delta R^2$ (pts) [95\% CI] & Clark--West $t$ & Holm $p$\\
\midrule
Thin, $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ (primary) & $+0.499$ [$+0.272$, $+0.779$] & 5.05 & ---\\
Thin, summed response $S_{60}$ & $+0.153$ [$+0.046$, $+0.272$] & 3.36 & 0.001\\
Thin, reversion term & $+0.613$ [$+0.322$, $+0.967$] & 5.15 & $<0.001$\\
Thin, momentum term & $-0.011$ [$-0.288$, $+0.261$] & 0.63 & 0.263\\
Mid-size control, $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ & $+0.316$ [$+0.074$, $+0.634$] & 3.12 & 0.002\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item 23 thin names, train 3{,}744 and test 3{,}808 symbol-hours on 42 test dates; baseline out-of-sample $R^2=0.340$; AUC $0.846\to0.847$ (95\% interval of the change $[-0.00002,+0.0026]$); training coefficient on $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ $+21.7$. Mid-size controls: 10 names, 1{,}680 test symbol-hours. Specification SHA-256 \texttt{f5800a69c219\ldots}; test opened once. Kind: real data (Part 14).\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The test passes (Table~\ref{p2:tab:us}). $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ adds $0.50$ points of out-of-sample $R^2$ to the full baseline (95\% interval $[0.27,0.78]$, Clark--West $t=5.05$), and the summed response $S_{60}$, the guard's own statistic, adds $0.15$ points. As on Binance, the content comes from impact-weighted reversion: the reversion term alone adds $0.61$ points, and the momentum term adds nothing. The effect is smaller than in thin Binance books, about a fifth of the $2.8$ points of Table~\ref{p2:tab:z9} on a baseline that already explains $34\%$ of the variance, but about fifteen times the Bybit effect, and tail classification does not improve significantly. Unlike on Binance, the mid-size controls also show a gain ($0.32$ points), against our prediction of little or none: in US stocks the information is not confined to the thinnest names. The controls trade \$50M--\$200M a day, far below the deepest crypto books, and we included no large-capitalization stocks, so the test does not say where the effect ends. Three features of the sample limit the reading. The selection rule did not exclude funds, so the included thin names contain one closed-end fund (CRF) and the controls one bond ETF (BNDX); the validity rule removed the 17 thinnest-trading names; and the asset list contains only names still listed in 2026. Tick-rule signing also misclassifies some trades. Within these limits, the result is not a property of one crypto venue: the reduced-form content of the reversion statistic carries over, at a smaller size, to a regulated equity market.

\subsection{The guard as a position limit}\label{p2:sec:backtest}
The guard of Section~\ref{p2:sec:guard} bounds worst-case loss; a separate question is whether its ingredients help a dealer size positions in practice. We registered a backtest with Table~\ref{p2:tab:z6}. Each hour a rule sets the position $q_t=c_s/R_t$, where $R_t$ is a risk measure from the past six hours and $c_s$ sets the training mean of $q$ to one for each symbol and rule; the hour's loss is $q_t$ times the adverse excursion, and the score is tail loss per unit of average position, ${\rm TLR}={\rm ES}_{95}(L)/\overline q$. The primary comparison is between a volatility limit ($R=$ six-hour realized volatility) and the same limit scaled by the gain multiple to a power $\theta$ chosen on training data ($\theta=0$ is the volatility limit); training data chose $\theta=0.25$.

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Position-sizing backtest, thin books, fresh days 2025--2026}\label{p2:tab:backtest}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
Rule & Risk measure $R_t$ & TLR & Breach rate & $\log{\rm TLR}-\log{\rm TLR}_{\rm vol}$ [CI]\\
\midrule
Volatility limit & $\hat\sigma_{6h}$ & 348.6 & 1.9\% & ---\\
Amplification-adjusted (primary) & $\hat\sigma_{6h}\,(S_{60}/|\hat h_0|)^{0.25}$ & 342.7 & 2.1\% & $-0.017$ [$-0.045$, $+0.011$]\\
Peak-response guard & $U_6\max_k|\hat h_k|$ & 411.4 & 5.3\% & $+0.165$ [$+0.095$, $+0.243$]\\
Summed-response guard & $U_6\,S_{60}$ & 522.2 & 13.2\% & $+0.404$ [$+0.285$, $+0.525$]\\
Static & 1 & 476.8 & 5.3\% & $+0.313$ [$+0.223$, $+0.397$]\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item 20 thin symbols; $U_6$ is the 95th percentile of absolute normalized flow over six hours. Primary row: 97.5\% interval; others 95\%. Breach: loss above the training 95th percentile of the static rule. Bybit: training chose $\theta=0$, and the summed guard is worse than volatility by $+0.484$ [$0.251$, $0.662$]. \end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The primary backtest fails (Table~\ref{p2:tab:backtest}). Scaling the volatility limit by the gain multiple lowers tail loss per unit of position in 91\% of bootstrap draws, but the interval includes zero. Rules built only on the estimated response, without volatility, are clearly worse than the volatility limit and even than a static limit: a six-hour estimate of $S$ is too noisy to size positions on its own. The guard theorems are statements about worst-case loss under a known or certified operator, and this evidence does not contradict them; but it means that, with public data and six-hour windows, an estimated summed response adds at most a little to volatility as a sizing input. A practical guard should combine the two and certify the operator with more data than a six-hour window; Section~\ref{p2:sec:certguard} tests exactly that.

\subsection{A certified guard}\label{p2:sec:certguard}
The guard theorems assume a certified operator, and a six-hour estimate is a poor certificate. We therefore registered a second backtest (SHA-256 \texttt{b9a56af48c93\ldots}) in which the operator of each symbol is certified once a day by pooling the minutes of the previous three days, and applied to every hour of the following day. Evaluation days are the 28th of each month and certification windows the 25th to 27th; none of these days was used in any earlier test. Training is 2024 (12 evaluation days), testing January 2025 to September 2026 (20 evaluation days, 6{,}894 symbol-hours on the 20 thin symbols). The primary rule scales the six-hour volatility limit by the certified gain multiple, $R_t=\hat\sigma_{6h}\,{\rm GM}_{\rm cert}^{\theta}$, with $\theta$ chosen on training data; the score is the tail loss per unit of position of Section~\ref{p2:sec:backtest}.

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Backtest with a certified operator, thin books, fresh days 2025--2026}\label{p2:tab:certguard}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
Rule & $R_t$ & TLR & Breach & $\Delta\log{\rm TLR}$ vs vol [95\% CI]\\
\midrule
Volatility limit & $\hat\sigma_{6h}$ & 296.7 & 2.0\% & ---\\
Certified, amp.-adjusted (primary) & $\hat\sigma_{6h}\,{\rm GM}_{\rm cert}^{0.25}$ & 289.7 & 2.3\% & $-0.024$ [$-0.051$, $-0.0004$]\\
Combined ceiling & stricter of the two & 299.3 & 2.9\% & $+0.009$ [$-0.023$, $+0.039$]\\
Certified summed guard & $U_{\rm cert}S_{\rm cert}$ & 416.5 & 13.8\% & $+0.339$ [$+0.241$, $+0.436$]\\
Six-hour summed guard & $U_6S_{60}$ & 424.8 & 14.9\% & $+0.359$ [$+0.237$, $+0.475$]\\

Combined ceiling: $R=\max(\hat\sigma_{6h}/\bar\sigma,\,U_{\rm cert}S_{\rm cert}/\overline{US})$ with training medians. Breach: loss above the training 95th percentile of the static rule. Certified against six-hour summed guard: $-0.020$ [$-0.165$, $+0.118$]. Secondary comparisons Holm $p=1.00$. Specification SHA-256 \texttt{b9a56af48c93\ldots}; test opened once. Kind: real data (Part 12).\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The primary test passes, narrowly (Table~\ref{p2:tab:certguard}). With a certified operator, scaling the volatility limit by the gain multiple lowers tail loss per unit of position by 2.4\%, with a 95\% interval of $[-5.1\%,-0.04\%]$; training data again chose $\theta=0.25$, as in Section~\ref{p2:sec:backtest}. The gain is small and its interval almost touches zero, so we read it as a modest improvement, not a decisive one: amplification information helps as a correction to a volatility limit once the operator is estimated from days rather than hours. Every secondary comparison fails. A limit built on the certified summed response alone is far worse than volatility, about as bad as the six-hour version, so certification does not rescue the guard as a stand-alone sizing rule; and a combined ceiling that applies the stricter of the two limits does no better than volatility. The guard's theorems concern worst-case loss under a known operator; as a practical limit, its statistic earns its place as a modest adjustment to volatility, not as a replacement. The comparison also differs from the theorem in form: the backtests size positions as $c/R_t$, which levers a position up when the estimated response is small, whereas the guard of Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} is a one-sided cap, $\min(1,B/(I\,U S))$, that only scales admission down. After the fact we applied the certified guard in that form, throttling a position whenever $U_{\rm cert}S_{\rm cert}$ exceeds its training median (27\% of test hours; Part 13, \texttt{R1}). As a cap on a static limit it lowers tail loss per unit of position by 13\% (95\% interval $[-27\%,-3\%]$); as a cap on a volatility limit it adds nothing ($+0.8\%$, $[-1.3\%,+2.7\%]$). Used as the theorem uses it, the guard helps where no volatility information is used and is subsumed by volatility where it is.

\subsection{October 10, 2025}\label{p2:sec:oct10}
The largest liquidation day on record motivated this chapter, so we report what the statistics did on it, descriptively. The event was chosen after the fact and the 10th of each month was a test day in the first two thin-book tests, so this is not a test. For the 20 thin symbols we computed the hourly six-hour estimates on October 3--11, 2025, and expressed each hour of October 10 as a percentile of the same symbol's October 3--9 distribution. The crash hour, the hour on October 10 with the largest adverse excursion in BTCUSDT, began at 21:00 UTC; the median thin-symbol excursion in that hour was about 7{,}500 basis points.

\begin{figure}[t]\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.72\textwidth]{p2figs/fig_oct10.pdf}
\caption{October 10, 2025: median percentile, across thin symbols, of the six-hour summed response $S_{60}$ and of six-hour realized volatility, relative to each symbol's October 3--9 distribution. Dotted line: the crash hour (21:00 UTC). Descriptive; the event was chosen after the fact.}\label{p2:fig:oct10}
\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{p2:fig:oct10} shows two things. First, the summed response was elevated through the whole day: in the morning, before volatility rose, the median thin symbol's $S_{60}$ sat at the 72nd to 87th percentile of its previous week, against the 52nd to 73rd for volatility, and at the start of the crash hour 16 of 18 thin symbols with valid estimates were above their previous-week 90th percentile. Second, volatility reached the top of its range from 16:00 onward as well, so on this day the summed response did not warn of anything volatility missed in the final hours; and across symbols it did not rank which coins fell furthest (Spearman $-0.11$, against $+0.36$ for volatility). We draw no inference from one day. The episode is consistent with the thin-book result, an elevated dynamic risk state that sizes excursions rather than timing them, and it illustrates why the paper's claims are about the size of the next hour's excursion, not about predicting crashes.

\subsection{Every pre-registered test}\label{p2:sec:alltests}
Table~\ref{p2:tab:alltests} lists every pre-registered test in this chapter, with its outcome and the number of times its test set was opened. Of twenty-five pre-registered primaries, fifteen pass, nine fail and one was not tested because the test it depended on failed. The failures are in BTCUSDT futures, in BTCUSDT spot, on Bybit with trades alone, on Coinbase (Part 17), in the six-hour position-sizing backtest, in the two five-minute open-interest liquidation-mechanism primaries (Part 22), in the unbounded-rescaling 18-hour matched-breach capacity test (Part 23), and in the strict joint placebo clause of the persistence mediation test (Part 25, where 15-minute persistence mediates $97.6\%$ of $\hat C_3$'s excursion $\Delta R^2$ while the five-minute jump placebo also exceeds $50\%$). Five forecasting passes are in thin Binance books (including 15-minute persistence in Part 25), one in thin US stocks, two on untouched days (Part 15), three on untouched symbols (Part 16), and two in the capped Rule 15c3-5 pre-trade throttle test (Part 24); the certified-guard backtest passes narrowly; and the Bybit test with depth meets its criterion with a negligible effect. The analyses of Parts 19--21 (Section~\ref{p2:sec:p1921}) were specified in advance but not hash-registered and are not counted here. Figure~\ref{p2:fig:forest} adds the post-hoc checks.

\subsection{Threats to validity}\label{p2:sec:threats}
\paragraph{Multiple testing.} The program registered twenty-five primaries, of which twenty-four were tested, and ran the post-hoc checks of Figure~\ref{p2:fig:forest}. The ten passing forecasting primaries have Clark--West statistics between $5.0$ and $14.8$ (one-sided $p<0.001$ in each), the Part 15 guard primary has a one-sided bootstrap $p$ of $0.0025$, and the two Part 24 capped-throttle primaries have one-sided bootstrap $p\le 0.002$; the nine failures are reported with the same prominence.

\paragraph{Sequential design.} The program was sequential. Each pre-registration fixed one test's specification before its test data were opened, but which test to run next was chosen after the earlier results were known: new symbols after the first pass, impact controls and then a volatility baseline in response to the obvious objections, depth on Bybit after the trade-only test failed, a regulated market after the second venue gave a negligible effect, untouched days and symbols after the information-safety theory, and a certified operator after the six-hour backtest failed, and a new venue last. Pre-registration therefore protects each test from specification search on its own test data; it does not protect the program from choices that adapted across tests. That is why every test is reported, failures included (Figure~\ref{p2:fig:forest}), and why the correction above is taken over all tested primaries.

\paragraph{Dependence among the passes.} The four passing tests share the 2023--2024 training days, and the original ten thin symbols appear in three of them. They are not four independent replications. They vary three things one at a time: the symbols (Table~\ref{p2:tab:y6}, ten new symbols), the test days (Tables~\ref{p2:tab:z6} and~\ref{p2:tab:z9}, days never used before) and the baseline (impact controls, then a full volatility-forecasting model). The Part 16 test (Table~\ref{p2:tab:p16}) shares their training days but none of their symbols. The US test (Table~\ref{p2:tab:us}) shares none of their data: a different market, asset class, data source, universe and set of days.

\paragraph{Look-ahead and estimated regressors.} Every feature uses minutes before the forecast hour; winsorization limits, flow scales, $\theta$ and tail thresholds come from training data; and a scrambling audit leaves every hourly feature unchanged when data from $t$ onward are permuted. $\hat C_3$ and $S_{60}$ are estimated from six hours, but the evaluation compares out-of-sample forecasts rather than testing a coefficient, so estimation error in the regressor can only attenuate the measured gain.

\paragraph{Simultaneity.} The structural fits regress the price change on same-minute flow, so $\hat\kappa$ and the depletion slope mix impact with any within-minute response of flow to price. The guard's identification results (Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:invariance} and Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}) concern the impulse response, not these regression coefficients, and the forecasting evidence uses $\hat C_3$ as an index whose value is judged out of sample, not as a causal parameter. Simultaneity therefore changes what $\hat C_3$ measures, not whether the out-of-sample gains are real. We tried to separate the two after the fact by instrumenting this minute's flow with last minute's flow (Part 13, \texttt{R4}), but at the one-minute scale normalized flow is almost serially uncorrelated (median first-stage correlation $0.005$), so the instrument is weak and the instrumented statistic is noise (it adds $0.01$ points of $R^2$, interval including zero). The causal content of $\hat\kappa$ therefore remains open; a convincing instrument would need order-level data.

\paragraph{Selection and survivorship.} The 30 symbols were fixed from one 2024 training day before any T6 statistic was computed, and the new symbols were drawn by a seeded procedure. Symbols had to be listed through 2026, so coins delisted after a collapse are excluded; if anything this removes the episodes in which thin-book risk was largest.

\paragraph{Economic size.} The largest gain, $2.8$ points of $R^2$ on a baseline of $18.6\%$, is a 15\% increase in the explained variance of the next hour's adverse excursion, and tail classification improves by $0.005$ of AUC. In basis points, on the test hours of Table~\ref{p2:tab:z9} and with quintile breakpoints from training data: within each quintile of the volatility forecast, hours in the top quintile of $\hat C_3$ have a next-hour adverse excursion $25.6$ basis points larger than hours in the bottom quintile (date-block 95\% interval $[18.6,32.8]$), against a mean excursion of $112.5$ basis points, and $61$ basis points larger in the most volatile quintile; the share of hours beyond the training 95th percentile rises from $2.3\%$ in the bottom $\hat C_3$ quintile to $5.1\%$ in the top. Unconditional sorts on $S_{60}$ show no pattern; its content appears only with the controls. As a sizing input the gain is worth about a 2.4\% reduction in tail loss per unit of position once the operator is certified (Table~\ref{p2:tab:certguard}). These are modest gains on top of a strong baseline, not a new predictor that replaces volatility.

\paragraph{In margin terms.} To express the 25.6 basis points in the units a risk manager uses, we fit a lognormal distribution to the excursions in each $\hat C_3$ quintile from its mean and median; at one common threshold the fit reproduces the observed share of hours beyond the training 95th percentile in every quintile to within 0.6 percentage points. Shifting the bottom-quintile distribution up by 25.6 basis points, the effect at a given volatility forecast, roughly doubles the probability that a $20\times$ position (liquidated after a 4\% adverse move, with 1\% maintenance margin) is liquidated within the hour, from 1.3\% to 2.7\% (2.3\% to 3.2\% across the interval for the effect). For a $10\times$ position, liquidated after 9\%, the probability almost triples, from 0.04\% to 0.11\%. On \$1M of notional at $20\times$, the extra expected loss of margin is about \$710 per hour held (\$490 to \$960; \texttt{recompute\_dollar.py}). These figures come from a fitted distribution, not a test, and they treat the hour's excursion from its opening price as the liquidation trigger. They show that a difference that is small in basis points is large in the tail that leverage makes relevant, which is where a pre-trade control operates.

\paragraph{Venue.} The size of the result depends on the venue. It is largest in thin Binance USD-M books; in thin US stocks it passes a pre-registered test at about a fifth of that size, and it also appears in mid-size US stocks (Table~\ref{p2:tab:us}); on Bybit it failed with trade data alone and, with order-book depth, met its pre-registered criterion with an effect about one eightieth of Binance's (Table~\ref{p2:tab:bybitob}).

\subsection{What the evidence says}\label{p2:sec:thin_summary}
Deep books sit on the stable side of the amplification condition. Flow depletes the side it hits on a fixed price grid, although re-centred depth bands make it look like replenishment, and we observe no peak overshoot. In thin Binance books the condition is still negative on average but binds more often, and the six-hour estimates of cumulative transient risk forecast the size of the next hour's adverse excursion out of sample. Four pre-registered tests pass, on the original symbols, on ten new symbols, on fresh days with price-impact controls, and on fresh days beyond a full volatility-forecasting baseline, where the gain is largest ($+2.8$ points of $R^2$) and extends to tail classification. The summed response $S$ of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum}, the guard's own statistic, carries the same information, and unit-free versions work. The content does not run through depth depletion, and in Binance books it is much weaker at mid depth and absent in BTCUSDT. In thin US stocks a fifth pre-registered forecasting test passes, at about a fifth of the Binance size and with a gain in mid-size stocks too; on Bybit the statistic is absent with trades alone and negligible with depth; and as a position-sizing input it improves a volatility limit only modestly once the operator is certified. Thin books are risky because their prices revert too slowly relative to the impact and momentum of the flow that moves them, which is what $S=\tilde\kappa/(m-q\tilde\kappa)$ measures.
\FloatBarrier
\section{Tests of the information-safety results}\label{p2:sec:istests}

The theorems of Sections~\ref{p2:sec:infosafety}--\ref{p2:sec:disclosure} concern declared contracts. This section reports two kinds of tests: simulations in which the true economy is known, so that coverage, attainment and optimality can be checked directly; pre-registered tests on market data that no earlier part of this chapter used, on new days, new symbols and a new venue; and post-hoc checks on data already opened.

\subsection{Simulation tests with a known truth}\label{p2:sec:simtests}

\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}

\toprule

\midrule

T10 & Are the window endpoints proved? & Certified by Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:certnum} for every window; outside the 60-digit endpoints by at most $1.5\times10^{-8}$ relative\\
T11 & What if the tap noise is correlated? & (O1) certificate covers 74--76\% (exact value $75.1\%$); $t$-certificate 94--95\%; Hotelling 100\%; dependent experiments 82--85\%\\
T12 & Should rebates use certified bounds? & No: a 10\% overstatement of $S$ costs $2.4$--$2.8\%$ of first-best welfare, a 50\% overstatement $70$--$80\%$\\
T13 & What if admission changes the dynamics? & Safe sets $[0,0.035]\cup[0.386,1]$ (closed form) and $[0,0.053]\cup[0.178,1]$ (book, certified); no certified level unsafe in 433 sampled economies\\

\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item All tests are deterministic given their seeds and run without data (\texttt{research\_extension/information\_safety/run\_all.py} $\to$ \texttt{results/T*.json}, about six minutes). Further checks: the joint per-period and total-budget worst case equals its linear-programming value in 300 random cases (T05b), and the balance-sheet step keeps the reserve in all 17{,}551 sampled states that satisfy the guard inequality (T12b). Kind: recomputed. \end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

The simulations confirm what the theorems claim and locate what they do not (Table~\ref{p2:tab:simtests}). The certificate is valid where its contract holds, including with delayed feedback and contrarian cohorts, and conservative when the declared memory is long. It fails in two ways that matter for practice: when memory is longer than declared, which is the case Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}(iii) warns about, and when feedback strengthens after certification, in which case a stale certificate covers the new risk only a third of the time. A guard must therefore be re-certified as conditions change; it cannot be set once. The remaining tests check the extensions: the window endpoints are proved rather than computed (T10), certificates that do not assume independent taps keep their level when the taps are correlated while the (O1) certificate does not (T11), non-monotone admission is certified by branch and bound (T13), and sequential probing keeps its error level (T14).

\subsection{Pre-registered test on untouched days (Part 15)}\label{p2:sec:p15}
We registered a test (Part 15) before downloading any day used in it: the 12th to 15th of each month on Binance, days no earlier part used on Binance. It has two primaries, with Holm's correction across them. First, the forecasting model of Table~\ref{p2:tab:z9}, frozen at its 2023--2024 training estimates, is applied to the 15th of each month from January 2025 to September 2026. Second, an uncertainty-aware guard throttles a position by $\min(1,k_s/(U_{\rm cert}S^{\rm up}_{60}))$, where $S^{\rm up}_{60}$ is the 90th percentile of the summed response over 100 block-bootstrap refits of the operator certified from the three preceding days; it is compared with a static limit at matched activity, and secondarily with the point-estimate guard, a depth throttle, a spectral-radius throttle and a volatility cap. The bootstrap bound is a diagnostic for observational data, not the finite-sample certificate of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}.

Guards: 6{,}732 test hours; TLR = ES95 of loss per unit of average position (positions normalized to training mean 1); the last column of panel B reports $\log{\rm TLR}$(robust guard) minus $\log{\rm TLR}$(rule), so negative values favour the robust guard. Primary 2 uses the 97.5\% interval $[-0.173,-0.011]$, one-sided $p=0.0025$. Both primaries pass under Holm's correction. Median $S^{\rm up}_{60}/S_{60}=1.25$. Specification SHA-256 \texttt{69849c96d70f\ldots}; test opened once. Kind: real data (Part 15).\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}

Both primaries pass (Table~\ref{p2:tab:p15}). The forecasting model, frozen at its 2023--2024 estimates and applied to days never used before, finds that $\hat C_3$ adds $4.2$ points of out-of-sample $R^2$ to the volatility and impact baseline (97.5\% interval $[1.6,8.2]$, Clark--West $t=9.9$) and improves tail classification; the summed response $S_{60}$ adds $3.3$ points. The gain is larger than in Table~\ref{p2:tab:z9} partly because the frozen baseline explains less on these days ($5.8\%$ of the variance against $18.6\%$): frozen coefficients transfer imperfectly, and the dynamic statistic picks up part of what the baseline loses. As a one-sided throttle on a static limit, the uncertainty-aware guard lowers tail loss per unit of position by 8\% (log difference $-0.084$, 97.5\% interval $[-0.173,-0.011]$, that is, a reduction of 1\% to 16\%) while retaining 91\% of activity, replicating on untouched days the post-hoc finding of Section~\ref{p2:sec:certguard}. It does much better than throttling on depth (26\% lower tail loss) or on the spectral radius (14\% lower), the two controls a supervisor would reach for first.

Two secondary results limit the claim. The bootstrap upper bound does no better than the point estimate ($+0.3\%$, interval $[-0.5\%,+1.3\%]$): on these data the upper bound is about 1.25 times the point estimate in most hours and ranks hours almost identically, so accounting for estimation uncertainty changes which hours are throttled very little. And, as in Section~\ref{p2:sec:certguard}, a volatility cap does at least as well as the guard, and adding the guard to it does not help significantly ($-1.2\%$, interval $[-3.7\%,+1.3\%]$). Used as the theorem uses it, the guard is a better throttle than depth or spectral rules and a real improvement over a static limit; it is not a substitute for volatility information.

\subsection{Pre-registered test on untouched symbols (Part 16)}\label{p2:sec:p16}
A third registered test (Part 16) uses Binance perpetuals that no earlier part used, thirty drawn by a seeded rule before any analysis and kept if their training depth is below the thin threshold of Part 5. It has three primaries, with Holm's correction across them: the forecasting specification of Table~\ref{p2:tab:z9} on the new thin symbols; the same beyond the baseline \emph{plus VPIN}, which turns the post-hoc comparison of Section~\ref{p2:sec:z9} into a confirmatory one; and the guard of Section~\ref{p2:sec:p15} against a static limit.

All three primaries pass (Table~\ref{p2:tab:p16}), but on a small set. The seeded draw of thirty new perpetuals produced only five with thin books, because the thinnest listed symbols had already been used in Parts 4--15; the rule required no minimum, so the test went ahead with five. On these symbols, re-estimating the Part 9 specification on their own training days, $\hat C_3$ adds $0.73$ points of out-of-sample $R^2$ to the volatility and impact baseline (95\% interval $[0.38,1.43]$, Clark--West $t=5.7$) and improves tail classification. That is about a quarter of the Part 9 gain on the original thin symbols, on a baseline that already explains $31.7\%$ of the variance rather than $18.6\%$.

The second primary makes the post-hoc VPIN comparison of Section~\ref{p2:sec:z9} confirmatory: with VPIN added to the baseline, $\hat C_3$ still adds $0.56$ points ($[0.27,1.16]$). VPIN has content of its own ($+0.28$ points) but leaves most of $\hat C_3$'s gain in place, as in the post-hoc comparison. The summed response $S_{60}$ adds $0.42$ points.

The third primary replicates the guard of Section~\ref{p2:sec:p15} on new symbols. Against a static limit, the uncertainty-aware guard lowers tail loss per unit of position by 23\% (log difference $-0.256$, interval $[-0.497,-0.021]$, a reduction of 2\% to 39\%) while retaining 91\% of activity. The interval is wide: the guard sample is five symbols on about twenty evaluation days, and its one-sided bootstrap $p$ of $0.004$ (8 of 2{,}000 draws) passes Holm's correction within Part 16 but not a Bonferroni correction across all tested primaries of the program (Section~\ref{p2:sec:threats}). The secondary comparisons agree with Part 15. The bootstrap upper bound again does not significantly improve on the point estimate ($-2.8\%$ in log points, interval including zero). Adding the guard to a volatility cap lowers tail loss by a further 7\%, a larger effect than in Part 15 but not significant after correction (Holm $p=0.07$). We do not report the guard against the volatility cap alone, which the registration did not include; the point estimates are close (360 against 367).

\subsection{Pre-registered test on a new venue (Part 17)}\label{p2:sec:p17}
Every venue so far was Binance, Bybit or the US consolidated tape. Part 17 registers a test on Coinbase Exchange spot, a US-regulated venue that no earlier part used, from public trade data: thin USD pairs drawn by a seeded rule from those with median daily dollar volume of \$0.5--5 million in June 2024, the depth-free statistic $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ of Parts 7 and 14, and the Part 9 volatility and impact baseline, on the 6th and 20th of each month from January 2025 to September 2026. Its second primary is a falsification test. Order splitting makes signed flow persistent, and a statistic built from persistent flow could forecast through persistence alone. The placebo recomputes $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ after shifting the flow series by 180 minutes inside its own six-hour window, which keeps the flow's distribution and persistence and breaks its minute-by-minute alignment with prices, the alignment the model says carries the risk. The real statistic must beat the placebo. The two primaries are tested in a fixed sequence, the placebo comparison only if the first passes, because beating a placebo is informative only when the real statistic has content.

The test fails (Table~\ref{p2:tab:p17}). Of the twenty pairs drawn, six traded in at least 60\% of minutes on enough days to enter (ARB, FLR, ICP, QNT, VET and XLM); thin pairs on this venue trade too sparsely for the rest. On these six, $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ adds $-0.09$ points of $R^2$ to the baseline (95\% interval $[-0.41,0.16]$, Clark--West $t=-0.58$), so the first primary fails and, under the registered fixed sequence, the placebo comparison is not tested; descriptively the real statistic and the placebo do equally badly (difference $+0.01$ points, $[-0.33,0.30]$). Nothing else forecasts either: $S_{60}$, VPIN and the statistic in the mid-size control pairs all add nothing. Coinbase joins Bybit with trades alone as a venue where the thin-book result is absent. Both are venues where the statistic must be computed from trades without depth, but so is the US test, which passed; the result is venue-specific, and we do not know which feature of a venue it depends on.

\subsection{Post-hoc checks on opened data (Part 18)}\label{p2:sec:p18}
Two further checks use samples that earlier tests opened, so they are post hoc; their analysis plan was hashed before they were computed. The first applies the same placebo to the Binance samples of Parts 9 and 16. The second adds three controls to the guard comparisons of Parts 15 and 16: a certified peak-response guard (the quantity Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} says is unsafe for repeated trading), a participation cap that limits the position to a share of the last six hours' volume, and a margin-style rule that scales the position by an exchange-style scanning range, the 99th percentile of one-hour moves over the three certification days. A cap on inventory alone is the static limit once activity is matched.

Both checks support the paper's reading, with one exception (Table~\ref{p2:tab:p18}). The placebo carries no information on Binance: on the Part 9 sample it adds $-0.02$ points of $R^2$ while the real statistic adds $2.73$ on the same rows, a difference of $2.74$ points (95\% interval $[1.32,4.82]$), and on the Part 16 sample the difference is $0.79$ points ($[0.42,1.60]$). Real and placebo statistics are uncorrelated (Spearman $-0.004$ and $0.025$). The forecasting content in thin Binance books therefore comes from the minute-by-minute alignment of flow and prices, not from the persistence of order flow. Against the new controls, on the Part 15 sample the uncertainty-aware guard has 3\% lower tail loss per unit of position than the certified peak-response guard and the margin-style rule (both significant after Holm's correction) and far lower than the participation cap; it is not better than the six-hour peak guard ($+3.6\%$, interval including zero). On the five symbols of Part 16 it beats only the participation cap. These are post-hoc comparisons on opened data, so they qualify the guard's ranking rather than test it.

\subsection{Three analyses specified in advance, not deposited (Parts 19--21)}\label{p2:sec:p1921}
The three analyses below (Parts 19--21) were specified in an earlier draft of this chapter, with their hypotheses and criteria, before their data were downloaded, but no specification hash was deposited prior to execution; we therefore label them ``specified in advance, not deposited'' and do not count them among the pre-registered tests of Table~\ref{p2:tab:alltests}. By contrast, Parts 22--25 (Sections~\ref{p2:sec:p22}--\ref{p2:sec:p25}) were hash-registered and committed to the public repository before their 2025--2026 test data were downloaded, and are counted in Table~\ref{p2:tab:alltests}.

\subsection{Leverage interaction and spot comparison (Part 19)}\label{p2:sec:p19}
Primary 1 of Part 19---which predicted a positive slope interaction between $\hat C_3$ and high open-interest-to-depth leverage (${\rm LEV}={\rm OI}/D_{1\%}\ge16.77$, the top training tercile)---failed: the interaction coefficient is $-0.18$ basis points per standard deviation (95\% date-block bootstrap CI $[-3.02,+3.73]$, Table~\ref{p2:tab:p19}). We evaluated two predictions on the 8th of each month, a day no earlier test used for these symbols, across 19 thin perpetuals from Parts 9 and 16 that also trade on Binance spot (training: 12 days in 2024, $4{,}104$ symbol-hours; test: 18 days from January 2025 to June 2026, $6{,}156$ symbol-hours). \emph{Primary 1} tested whether $\hat C_3\times\mathbf{1}\{{\rm LEV}\ge16.77\}$ has a positive slope in perpetuals. \emph{Primary 2} (exploratory, because the spot specification lacks order-book depth controls that Binance does not publish for spot) asked whether on the matched spot pairs, where there are no leveraged liquidations, the out-of-sample $\Delta R^2$ of $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ is at most one quarter of the perpetual gain.

Panel B: pooled regression with symbol fixed effects and date-block bootstrap intervals. Specified in advance, not deposited (\texttt{recompute\_p2\_parts19\_20\_21.py} $\to$ \texttt{R\_P2\_parts19\_20\_21.json}).\end{tablenotes}

Table~\ref{p2:tab:p19} shows why Primary 1 fails and why Primary 2 is only exploratory. In Panel~A, the out-of-sample $\Delta R^2$ of $\hat C_3$ is $+8.172$~points in the top leverage tercile versus $+3.678$~points in the lower two terciles, a difference of $+4.495$~points whose 2{,}000-draw date-block bootstrap 95\% confidence interval ($[-1.094,+11.663]$) crosses zero. \emph{Post hoc}, the mechanical reason why the slope interaction $\hat C_3\times\text{high-LEV}$ is zero ($-0.18$~bp) while the explained variance share is higher in high-leverage hours is that $\hat C_3=\hat\varphi\hat\varepsilon+\hat\kappa^2\hat\eta\hat q-(1-\hat a)\hat\kappa$ already contains $\hat\kappa$, which rises when resting depth $D_{1\%}$ is thin relative to open interest; high-${\rm LEV}$ hours therefore have larger variance in $\hat C_3$ rather than a steeper marginal slope per unit of $\hat C_3$. In the matched spot pairs, $\hat C_3^{\rm flow}$ adds $+0.987$~points of $R^2$ (95\% CI $[-0.093,+2.853]$, significant by the nested-model test of \citealp{p2:clark2007} at $t=4.96$, $p=3.6\times10^{-7}$, though its bootstrap level interval includes zero), giving a spot-to-perpetual point ratio of $0.152$ with a 95\% bootstrap interval of $[-0.015,+0.308]$ that crosses the $0.25$ threshold. Because the spot baseline omits depth controls and the ratio interval spans $0.25$, Part 19 does not establish leverage or liquidations as the mechanism; Section~\ref{p2:sec:p22} tests the liquidation channel directly with a pre-registered design.

\subsection{A natural experiment in fees (Part 20)}\label{p2:sec:p20}
The primary hypothesis of Part 20---that removing zero trading fees lowers dealer reversion speed $\hat m=1-\hat a$ and raises the unit-free gain multiple $\widehat{S/\tilde\kappa}=1/(\hat m-\hat q\hat\kappa)$ as in Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:equil}(i)--(iv)---is \emph{not supported} ($\hat m$ changes by $-0.00120$, $p=0.590$, and $\widehat{S/\tilde\kappa}$ by $-26.88$, $p=0.362$ across the two verified spot zero-fee events; Table~\ref{p2:tab:p20}). What moves in the two verified BTCUSDT spot zero-fee events---introduced on July 8, 2022 (Binance announcement \texttt{10246072590233}, 5th Anniversary Zero-Fee BTC spot trading) and removed on March 22, 2023 (Binance announcement \texttt{be21a7f1940b46fa95A42087b058958a}, Zero-Fee trading ended for BTC spot pairs except BTC/TUSD)---is price impact $\hat\kappa$, which is $0.24319$ lower during zero-fee trading ($-18.3\%$ of its sample mean, $t=-3.86$, $p=0.0002$). Because $\hat\kappa$ was not the primary outcome, this impact reduction is an \emph{exploratory} finding. Moreover, Table~\ref{p2:tab:p20} reports side by side the specification pooling all three originally listed dates (which included a March 20, 2024 USD-M VIP maker-fee adjustment affecting BTCUSDT and ETHUSDT): when that third date is included ($N=1{,}102$, $G=176$ date clusters), the pooled impact coefficient is $+0.00819$ ($t=0.14$, $p=0.8873$).

Pair-days / clusters & $324$ / $59$ & $374$ / $58$ & $698$ / $117$ & $1{,}102$ / $176$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item Difference-in-differences of zero fees relative to control pairs (ETH, SOL, ADA, DOGE, BNB, XRP and LTC against USDT); date-clustered standard errors in parentheses, together with two-sided $p$-values and 80\%-power minimum detectable effects (MDE, $2.8\times\text{SE}$ as a percentage of the sample mean). Spot events verified against Binance announcements \texttt{10246072590233} (July 8, 2022) and \texttt{be21a7f1940b46fa95A42087b058958a} (March 22, 2023); the fourth column adds the March 20, 2024 VIP maker-fee date. Only one pair (BTCUSDT) is treated in the two spot events. Kind: real data (\texttt{recompute\_p2\_parts19\_20\_21.py} $\to$ \texttt{R\_P2\_parts19\_20\_21.json}).\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

Two econometrics limitations govern how Table~\ref{p2:tab:p20} should be read. First, in the two verified spot events only a single pair (BTCUSDT) is treated against seven controls, so clustering standard errors by date does not correct for single-treated-unit cross-sectional dependence \citep{p2:conley2011}. Second, the 80\%-power minimum detectable effects for the two null primary outcomes are wide: $31.9\%$ of the sample mean for $\hat m$ and $52.0\%$ for $\widehat{S/\tilde\kappa}$ in the two spot events ($25.0\%$ and $38.8\%$ across all three dates), so moderate changes in reversion or in the gain multiple cannot be ruled out. With those caveats stated, a reduction in spot impact $\hat\kappa$ during zero-fee trading alongside no detectable change in $\hat m$ or $\widehat{S/\tilde\kappa}$ is \emph{consistent with} Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:equil}(v) (where endogenous depth adjusts $\tilde\kappa$ while leaving $S/\tilde\kappa$ invariant), rather than a confirmation of it.

\subsection{The guard results in dollars (Part 21)}\label{p2:sec:p21}
Tables~\ref{p2:tab:p15} and~\ref{p2:tab:p16} report the guard in tail loss per unit of average position. Because positions are normalized to a training mean of one, that ratio converts directly into dollars: on $\$1$M of average position, a tail-loss ratio of $377.24$ basis points is an expected shortfall of $\$37{,}724$ for the hour. Table~\ref{p2:tab:p21} restates the archived Part 15 and Part 16 results in dollars using no new data.

In dollars, the guard alone ((d) vs.\ (a)) lowers the hour's expected shortfall on $\$1$M of average position by $\$3{,}045$ on the untouched days ($p=0.0025$) and by $\$10{,}518$ on the untouched symbols ($p=0.004$). Against a volatility cap ((c) vs.\ (b)), adding the guard lowers hourly expected shortfall by $\$398$ on the untouched days ($95\%$ CI $[-3.7\%,+1.3\%]$, $p=0.196$) and by $\$2{,}557$ on the untouched symbols ($95\%$ CI $[-18.0\%,+0.5\%]$, unadjusted $p=0.036$, Holm $p=0.07$); neither improvement over the volatility cap is statistically significant after multiple-testing correction. Set beside the official operating budget of the U.S.\ Consolidated Audit Trail ($\$248.8$~million in the November 2024 CAT LLC filing, SEC Release No.\ 34-101901, p.~4, revised to $\$228.3$~million in May 2025; Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}, Table~9), certification of the summed response requires only public price and flow records and none of the participant-level tagging needed to identify individual cohorts.

\subsection{Pre-registered test of the liquidation mechanism (Part 22)}\label{p2:sec:p22}
Because Part 19 was not hash-registered and tested only a static leverage interaction, we pre-registered a direct test of the liquidation mechanism (\texttt{P22\_liquidation\_mechanism.json}, SHA-256 \texttt{2b3794e3812f\ldots}) and committed its hash to the repository before downloading any 22nd-of-month data for 2025--2026 ($3{,}876$ training symbol-hours on the 22nd of each month in 2024; $6{,}766$ test symbol-hours on the 22nd of each month from January 2025 to September 2026 across 19 thin perpetuals; test opened once). When forced liquidations execute in a perpetual futures book without a public liquidation feed, they contract open interest while moving the price adversely; following standard practice we define the hourly forced-liquidation proxy ${\rm LIQ}_{s,t}$ as the sum of five-minute open-interest contractions $(-\Delta{\rm OI}_{s,\tau}/\overline{\rm OI}_s)_+$ accompanied by an adverse five-minute return exceeding one training standard deviation. The registration specified two primaries: (\emph{Primary 1}) $\hat C_3$ forecasts next-hour ${\rm LIQ}_{s,t+1}$ out of sample beyond the Part 9 volatility, impact and lagged-${\rm LIQ}_t$ baseline; and (\emph{Primary 2}) controlling for realised contemporary ${\rm LIQ}_{s,t+1}$ mediates at least 15\% of $\hat C_3$'s out-of-sample $\Delta R^2$ for the next hour's maximum adverse excursion ${\rm MAE}_{s,t+1}$.

\begin{table}[!tbp]\centering\footnotesize
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Pre-registered test of the open-interest liquidation mechanism on untouched 22nd-of-month days (Part 22), 2025--2026}\label{p2:tab:p22}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{1.8pt}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
Specification (19 thin symbols, $6{,}766$ test hours) & $\Delta R^2$ (pts) / Share [95\% CI] & CW $t$ ($p$) & Result\\
\midrule
Excursion ${\rm MAE}_{t+1}\mid\hat C_{3,t}$ (22nd-of-month) & $+6.516$ [$+3.159$, $+10.743$] & $12.21$ ($<10^{-6}$) & ---\\
Primary 1: Liquidation ${\rm LIQ}_{t+1}\mid\hat C_{3,t}$ & $-0.795$ [$-1.529$, $-0.179$] & $-1.12$ ($0.868$) & fail\\
Excursion ${\rm MAE}_{t+1}\mid\hat C_{3,t},{\rm LIQ}_{t+1}$ & $+6.958$ [$+3.405$, $+11.338$] & --- & ---\\
Primary 2: Mediation share $1-\Delta R^2_{\rm LIQ}/\Delta R^2$ & $-6.8\%$ [$-10.2\%$, $-3.1\%$] & --- & fail\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item Trained on 22nd-of-month days in 2024 ($3{,}876$ symbol-hours), evaluated once on untouched 22nd-of-month days from January 2025 to September 2026 ($6{,}766$ symbol-hours); 2{,}000-draw date-block bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals. Specification SHA-256 \texttt{2b3794e3812f\ldots}; test opened once. Kind: real data (\texttt{recompute\_prereg\_oct21\_22nd.py} $\to$ \texttt{R\_P2\_p22\_p23.json}).\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

On these untouched 22nd-of-month test days, $\hat C_3$ strongly forecasts next-hour adverse excursions ($\Delta R^2=+6.516$~percentage points, 95\% CI $[+3.159,+10.743]$, Clark--West $t=12.21$, Table~\ref{p2:tab:p22}), providing a sixth out-of-sample confirmation of the thin-book excursion effect. However, both liquidation-mechanism primaries fail decisively: $\hat C_3$ does not forecast the five-minute open-interest contraction proxy beyond the baseline ($\Delta R^2=-0.795$~points, 95\% CI $[-1.529,-0.179]$, $t=-1.12$), and adding realised next-hour open-interest contractions to the excursion model leaves $\hat C_3$'s predictive gain undiminished ($\Delta R^2=+6.958$~points, mediation share $-6.8\%$, 95\% CI $[-10.2\%,-3.1\%]$). We therefore drop the hypothesis that five-minute open-interest liquidations mediate the effect. The simplest remaining reading is that $\hat C_3$ forecasts adverse excursions because it measures slow book reversion relative to price impact; Part 25 examines it.

\subsection{Pre-registered test of non-resetting multi-hour guard capacity (Part 23)}\label{p2:sec:p23}
To evaluate the guard on a leveraged position that is held across hours without resetting inventory at the top of each hour, we pre-registered a multi-hour margin-breach and notional-capacity test (\texttt{P23\_guard\_capacity.json}, SHA-256 \texttt{d4169377da0b\ldots}) before downloading the 22nd-of-month data for 2025--2026 ($228$ training symbol-days in 2024; $398$ test symbol-days in January 2025--September 2026 across 19 thin perpetuals; test opened once). At 06:00 UTC on each day $d$, a dealer opens a $20\times$ leveraged position ($450$~bp maintenance-margin buffer before forced liquidation) using the three-day certified operator $\hat S^{\rm up}_{s,d}$ estimated from hours 00:00--06:00 across the three preceding evaluation days and holds the position continuously through 24:00 UTC; cumulative mark-to-market loss evolves pathwise across all 18 hours without hourly reset. The primary hypothesis tests whether, when the volatility cap (rule~(b)) and the combined volatility-plus-certified-guard rule (rule~(c)) are scaled to have the exact same out-of-sample forced-liquidation rate as the unit-scaled volatility cap ($39.70\%$), rule~(c) admits a significantly larger mean dollar notional position $\overline{N}$ than rule~(b).

\begin{threeparttable}

\setlength{\tabcolsep}{1.8pt}

\toprule

(c) Vol.\ cap $\times$ cert.\ guard & $42.21\%$ & $166{,}501$ & $0.686$ & $\$1{,}235{,}130$\\
(d) Certified guard alone & $50.00\%$ & $182{,}043$ & $0.838$ & ---\\

Secondary: $\Delta\log{\rm ES}_{95}{\rm(c\ vs\ b)}$ & \multicolumn{4}{c}{$+0.1427$, 95\% CI $[+0.0741,\ +0.1964]$}\\

Specification SHA-256 \texttt{d4169377da0b\ldots}; test opened once. Kind: real data (\texttt{recompute\_prereg\_oct21\_22nd.py} $\to$ \texttt{R\_P2\_p22\_p23.json}).\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

Table~\ref{p2:tab:p23} reports both the unit-scaled outcomes and the matched-breach capacity comparison. At unit training scale, a static $20\times$ position is liquidated on $51.26\%$ of thin-symbol test days (daily $\mathrm{ES}_{95}=\$166{,}642$), while the volatility cap lowers the liquidation rate to $39.70\%$ ($\mathrm{ES}_{95}=\$144{,}360$). When rules~(b) and~(c) are scaled to match the $39.70\%$ out-of-sample liquidation rate, adding the morning-certified guard to the volatility cap raises the mean admitted notional position from $\$902{,}282$ to $\$1{,}235{,}130$---a point gain of $+\$332{,}848$ ($+36.89\%$)---because the guard concentrates notional on symbol-days with faster certified reversion. However, across the $22$ test dates the 95\% date-block bootstrap confidence interval is $[-\$20{,}390,+\$1{,}180{,}808]$, which narrowly crosses zero, and at unit scale rule~(c) has higher tail loss than rule~(b) ($\Delta\log\mathrm{ES}_{95}=+0.1427$). The primary hypothesis therefore fails: in observational data, a three-day morning certificate is too coarse to deliver a statistically significant capacity gain over a trailing six-hour volatility cap across an 18-hour holding window when quiet-morning exposure is rescaled above the \$1M facility limit.

\subsection{Pre-registered test of a capped pre-trade throttle (Part 24)}\label{p2:sec:p24}
Part~24 was registered after the Part~23 primary failed. Why did the unit-rescaled secondary in Part~23 increase daily $\mathrm{ES}_{95}$ ($\Delta\log\mathrm{ES}_{95}=+0.1427$) while the matched-breach capacity interval ($[-\$20{,}390,+\$1{,}180{,}808]$) crossed zero? In Part~23, dividing the multiplicative throttle $q^{\rm raw}_{c,i}=\min(1,k_{{\rm vol},s}/\sigma_{{\rm morn},i})\cdot\min(1,k_{{\rm grd},s}/(U_{{\rm cert},i}\hat S^{\rm up}_{60,i}))\in(0,1]$ by its training mean ($\approx0.686$) levered quiet-morning positions up to $1.46\times$ ($\$1.46\text{M}$, or $29.2\times$ effective leverage), violating the $\$1\text{M}$ ($20\times$) facility cap whenever afternoon shocks arrived; and the 21-date sample provided limited power. A pre-trade credit control of the kind Rule~15c3-5 requires brokers to maintain caps exposure rather than levering it up, so Part~24 registers a capped throttle $q_t\in(0,1]$ that never raises a position above the $\$1\text{M}$ facility limit. To test this capped pre-trade control and evaluate the 42-date matched-breach capacity, we pre-registered Part~24 (\texttt{P24\_guard\_throttle\_and\_capacity.json}, SHA-256 \texttt{698f91196eba\ldots}) and committed its hash before downloading the untouched 2nd-and-9th-of-month data for January 2025--September 2026 ($456$ training symbol-days on the 8th and 22nd of each month in 2024; $796$ test symbol-days across 42 untouched test dates; test opened once).

(b) Volatility cap ($q\le 1$) & $38.69\%$ & $\$163{,}099$ & $\$829{,}463$ & $46.65\%$\\
(c) Vol.\ cap $\times$ cert.\ guard ($q\le 1$) & $31.28\%$ & $\$136{,}670$ & $\$734{,}945$ & $42.56\%$\\

Prim.\ 1: $\Delta{\rm Liq}{\rm(c\ vs\ b)}$ / per \$1M & \multicolumn{4}{c}{$-7.41$~pp [$-11.45$, $-4.15$] / $-4.09$~pp [$-8.47$, $-1.03$] (pass)}\\
Prim.\ 2: $\Delta\text{Daily ES}_{95}{\rm(c\ vs\ b)}$ & \multicolumn{4}{c}{$-\$26{,}429$, 95\% CI $[-\$53{,}146,\ -\$6{,}254]$ (pass)}\\
Sec.: Unbounded $\overline{N}{\rm(c)}-\overline{N}{\rm(b)}$ ($48.37\%$) & \multicolumn{4}{c}{$+\$172{,}416$ ($+17.89\%$), 95\% CI $[-\$219{,}311,\ +\$578{,}090]$}\\

Spec SHA-256 \texttt{698f91196eba\ldots}; test opened once (\texttt{R\_P2\_p24\_p25.json}).\end{tablenotes}
