Table~\ref{p2:tab:p24} shows that both pre-registered primaries pass when the $\$1\text{M}$ ($20\times$) facility cap $q_t\in(0,1]$ is enforced. Adding the morning-certified guard to the volatility cap reduces the 18-hour forced-liquidation rate from $38.69\%$ to $31.28\%$---a reduction of $-7.41$~percentage points (95\% CI $[-11.45,-4.15]$)---and reduces the liquidation rate per $\$1\text{M}$ of deployed notional by $-4.09$~percentage points (95\% CI $[-8.47,-1.03]$, Primary~1 passes). Simultaneously, the certified guard lowers 18-hour daily $\mathrm{ES}_{95}$ loss from $\$163{,}099$ to $\$136{,}670$, saving $-\$26{,}429$ per day on the $\$1\text{M}$ facility (95\% CI $[-\$53{,}146,-\$6{,}254]$, Primary~2 passes). By contrast, the secondary unbounded matched-breach capacity comparison yields $+\$172{,}416$ ($+17.89\%$, 95\% CI $[-\$219{,}311,+\$578{,}090]$), so the guard works as a protective cap ($q_t\le 1$), not as a licence to lever quiet mornings above $20\times$.

\paragraph{Matched exposure.} Most of the registered pass comes from trading less. The $-7.41$~pp breach reduction in Table~\ref{p2:tab:p24} reflects both response-based risk discrimination and an $11.39\%$ reduction in average admitted notional ($\$829{,}463 \to \$734{,}945$) caused by multiplying two independent caps in $(0,1]$. An independent multi-policy audit (\texttt{hardened\_empirical\_audits.py} $\to$ \texttt{R\_hardened\_empirical\_audits.json}) evaluates six policies under strictly training-calibrated sizing across the 42 test dates. When normalized on the training set so both policies target $\$1\text{M}$ mean exposure (Benchmark~4B-ii), the combined guard achieves a breach rate indistinguishable from the volatility cap ($48.62\%$ vs.\ $48.37\%$, $\Delta=+0.25$~pp, 95\% CI $[-5.64,+5.16]$~pp) and comparable tail loss ($\$197{,}395$ vs.\ $\$188{,}508$, $\Delta=+\$8{,}887$, 95\% CI $[-\$25{,}253,+\$33{,}587]$). At matched exposure, then, the guard adds no measurable protection beyond a volatility cap. The largest single loss is smaller under the combined rule ($\$445{,}487$ against $\$623{,}252$ for the volatility cap), but that is one observation. Calibrated on training data to a matched $\mathrm{ES}_{95}$ budget (Benchmark~4C-i, exploratory, point estimates), the combined policy admits $\$1{,}040{,}119$ mean notional versus $\$963{,}874$ ($+7.91\%$ incremental capacity at matched training risk), with normalized tail loss of $\$184{,}365$ per $\$1\text{M}$ deployed (vs.\ $\$195{,}573$). In realistic directional accounting with $10$~bp taker fee and funding costs, realized 18-hour breach rates are $14.3\%$ (long) and $18.3\%$ (short), well below the stylized two-sided $31.3\%$.

\subsection{Pre-registered test of the multi-minute persistence mechanism (Part 25)}\label{p2:sec:p25}
Part~22 showed that five-minute open-interest contractions neither are forecast by $\hat C_3$ nor mediate its excursion predictability. Under Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:sum}, $\hat C_3=\hat\kappa^2\hat\eta\hat q-(1-\hat a)\hat\kappa$ amplifies worst-case excursions through multi-minute price-impact persistence and slow book reversion across 15--60 minute horizons rather than isolated five-minute liquidations. To test this slow-reversion reading, we registered Part~25 after Part~22 failed (\texttt{P25\_persistence\_mechanism.json}, SHA-256 \texttt{8ebd67f3e9d1\ldots}), before downloading the 42 untouched 2nd-and-9th-of-month test dates ($7{,}752$ training symbol-hours in 2024; $13{,}532$ test symbol-hours in January 2025--September 2026 across 19 thin perpetuals; test opened once). For each hour $t\in\{6,\dots,22\}$ UTC, we measure next-hour multi-quarter-hour non-reversion via the mean absolute price displacement across the four 15-minute checkpoints $M_{{\rm ckpt},t+1}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k=1}^4|p_{t+15k}-p_t|$ (and the 60-minute $L_1$ excursion integral $M_{L_1,t+1}=\frac{1}{60}\sum_{\tau=1}^{60}|p_{t+\tau}-p_t|$, the empirical counterpart of $S_{60}$), contrasted against the maximum single five-minute bar jump $M_{5{\rm m},t+1}=\max_{b\in\{1..12\}}|\Delta p_{5{\rm m},b}|$.

\begin{table}[!htbp]\centering\footnotesize
\begin{threeparttable}
\caption{Pre-registered test of the multi-minute price-impact persistence mechanism on 42 untouched days (Part 25), 2025--2026}\label{p2:tab:p25}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{1.8pt}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
Specification (19 thin, $13{,}532$ test hours) & $\Delta R^2$ (pts) / Share [95\% CI] & CW $t$ ($p$) & Res.\\
\midrule
Excursion ${\rm MAE}_{t+1}\mid\hat C_{3,t}$ (2nd \& 9th) & $+3.944$ [$+2.114$, $+6.375$] & $15.68$ ($<10^{-6}$) & ---\\
Prim.\ 1: 15-min persistence $M_{{\rm ckpt},t+1}\mid\hat C_{3,t}$ & $+3.713$ [$+1.884$, $+6.035$] & $14.84$ ($<10^{-6}$) & pass\\
Excursion ${\rm MAE}_{t+1}\mid\hat C_{3,t},M_{{\rm ckpt},t+1}$ & $+0.095$ [$+0.052$, $+0.149$] & --- & ---\\
Prim.\ 2: Mediation by $M_{{\rm ckpt},t+1}$ (w/ placebo) & $97.6\%$ [$96.6\%$, $98.3\%$] & --- & fail$^\dagger$\\
Sec.: 60-min $L_1$ integral $M_{L_1,t+1}\mid\hat C_{3,t}$ / share & $+3.809$ [$+1.973$, $+6.252$] / $98.6\%$ & $15.03$ ($<10^{-6}$) & ---\\
Placebo: 5-min jump $M_{5{\rm m},t+1}\mid\hat C_{3,t}$ / share & $+0.738$ [$+0.503$, $+1.064$] / $78.6\%$ & $15.78$ ($<10^{-6}$) & ---\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\begin{tablenotes}\footnotesize\item Trained on 24 dates in 2024 ($7{,}752$ symbol-hours); evaluated once on 42 untouched test dates ($13{,}532$ symbol-hours) with 13 baseline controls + symbol fixed effects. $^\dagger$Primary~2 required both $M_{{\rm ckpt},t+1}$ mediation $\ge50\%$ (met: $97.6\%$) and $M_{5{\rm m},t+1}$ mediation $<50\%$ (not met: $78.6\%$, leaving $+0.843$~pts unmediated vs.\ $+0.095$~pts for $M_{{\rm ckpt},t+1}$). Spec SHA-256 \texttt{8ebd67f3e9d1\ldots} (\texttt{R\_P2\_p24\_p25.json}).\end{tablenotes}
\end{threeparttable}
\end{table}

Table~\ref{p2:tab:p25} reports the results. First, on the 42 untouched test dates ($13{,}532$ symbol-hours), $\hat C_{3,t}$ again forecasts next-hour adverse excursions out of sample ($\Delta R^2=+3.944$~points, 95\% CI $[+2.114,+6.375]$, $t=15.68$), providing a seventh out-of-sample confirmation of the thin-book excursion effect. Second, Primary~1 passes: $\hat C_{3,t}$ forecasts next-hour 15-minute checkpoint persistence $M_{{\rm ckpt},t+1}$ beyond all 13 volatility, impact and lagged-persistence controls ($\Delta R^2=+3.713$~points, 95\% CI $[+1.884,+6.035]$, $t=14.84$), as well as the 60-minute $L_1$ integral $M_{L_1,t+1}$ ($\Delta R^2=+3.809$~points, $t=15.03$), with five times the predictive power of a single five-minute bar jump ($+0.738$~points). Third, controlling for $M_{{\rm ckpt},t+1}$ collapses $\hat C_{3,t}$'s excursion $\Delta R^2$ from $+3.944$ to $+0.095$~points---a mediation share of $97.6\%$ (95\% CI $[96.6\%,98.3\%]$; $98.6\%$ for $M_{L_1,t+1}$). Because the largest five-minute bar jump $M_{5{\rm m},t+1}$ partially co-moves with multi-minute excursions, its mediation share ($78.6\%$, leaving $+0.843$~points unmediated---$8.9\times$ the residual under $M_{{\rm ckpt},t+1}$) exceeds the strict $<50\%$ placebo threshold, so the joint Primary~2 criterion is recorded as a failure. Together, Parts~22 and~25 point away from five-minute liquidations and toward slow reversion over 15 to 60 minutes, but they do not establish it: Primary~2 fails, and the next paragraph shows that much of the mediation is mechanical.

\paragraph{Path-norm collinearity and non-overlapping sequential persistence.} Why did the single-bar jump placebo mediate $78.6\%$ in Table~\ref{p2:tab:p25}? Simulated paths under a pure Gaussian random-walk null (with zero structural feedback) reveal that $\mathrm{MAE}_{60}=\|p_f\|_\infty$ has an intrinsic mathematical correlation of $0.9235$ with $M_{\rm ckpt}$ and $0.9139$ with $M_{L_1}$ (compared to $0.9370$ and $0.9417$ empirically) because continuous path norms on the same interval are strongly collinear by analysis. To evaluate multi-minute persistence without path-norm overlap, an exploratory audit (\texttt{hardened\_empirical\_audits.py}) partitions the hour into non-overlapping sequential windows: pre-window $\hat C_3$ forecasts initial 15-minute price travel ($\Delta R^2=+3.321$~points, $t=15.65$), and controlling for this initial travel mediates $65.1\%$ of the subsequent displacement from baseline and $40.3\%$ of incremental new price travel over the remaining 45 minutes ($t=15.53$). This is consistent with persistence over several quarter-hours. But because $\hat C_3$ is itself built from slow reversion, a test that it forecasts persistence comes close to checking its construction, and an independent test of the mechanism remains open.
\section{Policy implications}\label{p2:sec:policy}

\paragraph{Pre-trade controls.} Rule 15c3-5 requires pre-trade controls that prevent orders exceeding credit or capital thresholds \citep{p2:sec2010}. Static single-order caps and daily value-at-risk limits do not see transient dynamics. Lemma~\ref{p2:thm:guard} identifies the quantity that a solvency-motivated control should bound: inventory times the shock envelope times the summed absolute impulse response. Peak-based or eigenvalue-based limits are either unsafe or blind in exactly the regimes where they matter. Our evidence locates where such controls matter. In the deepest crypto book the amplifying regime is rare and the statistic carries no forecasting value; in thin books it is more frequent, and six-hour estimates of the summed response forecast the next hour's adverse excursion beyond volatility forecasts and price impact (Section~\ref{p2:sec:thin}). Our backtests also set a limit on what such a control can do with public data. A six-hour estimate of the summed response adds nothing to a volatility limit as a sizing input (Table~\ref{p2:tab:backtest}); an operator certified from three days of data improves a volatility limit modestly, by 2.4\% in tail loss per unit of position (Table~\ref{p2:tab:certguard}); and limits built on the estimate alone do worse than volatility in both. The guard's role is therefore a ceiling on worst-case loss under a certified operator and a correction to conventional volatility limits, not a replacement for them, and it is most relevant for books with under about \$0.4M of depth within 1\% of the mid.

\paragraph{Pricing reversion.} Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:equil} shows that dealers under-supply reversion because its benefit, lower tail exposure for every leveraged position, is not paid to them. Maker rebates are a natural instrument: a rebate that rises with the book's summed response, $(1-\theta/N)\Lambda S^2/\tilde\kappa$ per unit of reversion, prices the externality with an observable. Venues already tier rebates by volume; tiering them by contribution to reversion in thin books is a design question our model makes precise.

\paragraph{What can be certified, what cannot, and when to abstain.} A supervisor or venue can certify the summed response, and hence a safe admission level, from controlled experiments at a cost that does not depend on how many traders there are or how similar their memories are (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}). It cannot certify any finite risk bound from a finite window without a bound on how long participants remember (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}(iii)), so a declared memory bound is a policy input, not a technicality, and a certificate must be renewed when conditions change (test T09). When compatible risks straddle the budget, the right response is to abstain: throttle admission to the certified level, and when even zero admission is uncertified because of existing inventory, flag the state and reduce the position (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robustguard}(iv)). Disclosure is worth paying for where memory is long and public certificates are weak, and is worth little where a short window already pins the risk down (Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:disclosure}).

\paragraph{Market design.} Frequent batch auctions remove latency races within a batch \citep{p2:budish2015}. Transient amplification, when it exists, operates over many seconds and spans many batches, so batching and a state-carrying guard address different failures and can be complements.

\paragraph{Supervision without identification.} Chapter~\ref{ch:p1} shows that identifying algorithms from public data is expensive. The guard needs only impulse coefficients and operator confidence sets, which are invariant to how flow is split across cohorts. Supervisors can therefore ask venues and brokers to report summed-gain statistics and their confidence radii instead of attempting to infer black-box learners. Our results argue for reporting impact, mean reversion and refill directly, because those are the primitives that decide whether a book can amplify. They also show that feedback and amplification are different objects: measured feedback is largest in the deepest books, where amplification is rarest (Table~\ref{p2:tab:x3}).

\section{Conclusion}\label{p2:sec:conclusion}

The theory answers four questions. A book amplifies when depletion outweighs impact-weighted reversion; with momentum the one-period margin is sufficient but not exact, and momentum alone cannot make a stable book overshoot. Cumulative risk is long-run impact divided by reversion net of momentum, and the guard that bounds it is the largest safe pre-trade rule, computable and identified from public data. In equilibrium reversion is under-supplied, and the corrective price is a function of the guard's own statistic. And the information that certifies a safe decision is much cheaper than the information that identifies the traders: the summed response is certified at the parametric rate up to a logarithm that cannot be removed, finite windows certify it only under a memory bound, and a guard built on the certificate gives up no more trading capacity than any comparably safe rule, up to a logarithm.

The evidence is mixed in an informative way. The deepest crypto book sits on the stable side of the condition, and flow depletes the side it hits without producing a peak overshoot. In thin Binance books, six-hour estimates of cumulative transient risk forecast the size of the next hour's adverse excursion out of sample in every pre-registered forecasting test, on new symbols and on untouched days, beyond price impact, a full volatility-forecasting baseline and VPIN; the effect is about a fifth as large in thin US stocks, negligible on Bybit and absent on Coinbase. Tests of a leverage or liquidation mechanism fail, and a follow-up that points to slow reversion over 15 to 60 minutes is close to a check of how the statistic is built. As a risk control the certified guard improves on a static limit but not, at matched exposure, on a volatility cap; its registered gain over a volatility cap in 18-hour positions comes mostly from admitting less exposure. Thin books are risky because their prices revert too slowly relative to the impact and momentum of the flow that moves them; why they revert slowly is the open empirical question.

Three extensions would settle what remains open. Message-level data from a regulated market, such as LOBSTER or TAQ for US equities, would extend the US test to depth and to large stocks, show where the effect ends, and allow depletion to be measured without the recentering of band depth. Data on liquidity-provider identities would test the equilibrium's prediction that fewer dealers mean more transient risk. And longer certification windows, or certification with order-level data, would show whether the modest gain of a certified guard over volatility limits can be made large.

\paragraph{Data and code availability.} All data are public: Binance archives (\url{https://data.binance.vision}), verified against published checksums, and Bybit's order-book history (\url{https://www.bybit.com/derivatives/en/history-data}) and trade archive (\url{https://public.bybit.com}); US consolidated trades are from the Alpaca market-data API (\url{https://alpaca.markets}, free account). Pre-registered specifications, their SHA-256 hashes and test-set unlock counters are in the replication package. Each number is produced by a notebook cell or script that writes a result file (Appendix~\ref{p2:app:repro}).

\paragraph{Use of AI tools.} The authors used Claude (Anthropic), an AI assistant, to write and debug analysis code, run checks of derivations and numerical results, and draft and edit text. Every number in the paper is produced by code the authors executed and archived in the replication package. The authors reviewed all content and take full responsibility for it.

\paragraph{Disclosures.} The authors declare no relevant financial interests.

\section{Appendix: Proofs}\label{p2:app:proofs}

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:jury}.} With one cohort, $J=\begin{psmallmatrix}a&\kappa q\\ \eta a&1-\eta+\eta\kappa q\end{psmallmatrix}$, so ${\rm tr}J=a+1-\eta+\eta\kappa q$ and $\det J=a(1-\eta)$. The Jury conditions for a real $2\times2$ matrix are $|\det J|<1$ and $1\pm{\rm tr}J+\det J>0$. Substituting $a=1-\kappa\varphi_f$: $1-{\rm tr}J+\det J=\eta(1-a-\kappa q)=\eta\kappa(\varphi_f-q)$, positive iff $q<\varphi_f$; and $1+{\rm tr}J+\det J=(4-2\eta)-\kappa[(2-\eta)\varphi_f-\eta q]$, positive iff the stated bound on $\kappa$ holds (automatically when $(2-\eta)\varphi_f\le\eta q$).

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:invariance}.} Equal merged measures give equal $G$, hence equal $H$, and the impulse coefficients are the Laurent coefficients of $H$ (Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}, Theorem on merged equivalence). Under $z\mapsto Tz$, $c^\top J^kb$ is unchanged because $CT^{-1}(TJT^{-1})^kTb=CJ^kb$, while the row norm $\|CT^{-1}(TJT^{-1})^k\|$ depends on $T$. The displayed price path is the convolution of inputs with $\{g_k\}$, and its worst case over the $\ell_\infty$ ball is $\bar u\sum|g|$ by H\"older.

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:inventory_S}.} (i) Taking $z$-transforms of $y_{i,t}=(1-\eta_i)y_{i,t-1}+\eta_i x_t$, $I_{i,t}=(1-\delta_{\rm inv})I_{i,t-1}+u_{i,t}$ and $u_{i,t}=q_i y_{i,t}-\psi_i I_{i,t-1}$ gives $Y_i(z)=\frac{\eta_i z}{z-1+\eta_i}X(z)$ and $(z-1+\delta_{\rm inv}+\psi_i)I_i(z)=q_i z Y_i(z)$, whence $U_i(z)=\frac{z-1+\delta_{\rm inv}}{z-1+\delta_{\rm inv}+\psi_i}q_i Y_i(z)$ and $G_{\rm inv}(z)=\sum_i U_i(z)/X(z)$ has the stated form. Evaluating at $z=1$ when the augmented operator is Schur stable gives $G_{\rm inv}(1)=\sum_i \frac{\delta_{\rm inv}}{\delta_{\rm inv}+\psi_i}q_i=\Phi_{\rm eff}(\psi)$ and $S(\psi)=H_{\rm inv}(1)=\kappa/(1-a-\kappa\Phi_{\rm eff}(\psi))$. (ii) Write $r_i=1-\eta_i$ and $\rho_i=1-\delta_{\rm inv}-\psi_i$. When $0\le\psi_i\le 1-\delta_{\rm inv}$ and $\delta_{\rm inv}\ge\eta_i$, we have $0\le\rho_i\le 1-\delta_{\rm inv}\le r_i<1$. Expanding $U_i(z)/X(z)=q_i\eta_i\frac{z}{z-r_i}\bigl(1-\frac{\psi_i}{z-\rho_i}\bigr)$ in powers of $z^{-1}$, the $m$-th open-loop feedback tap of cohort $i$ is $k_{i,0}=q_i\eta_i\ge0$ and, for $m\ge1$, $k_{i,m}=q_i\eta_i\bigl(r_i^m-\psi_i\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}r_i^{m-1-j}\rho_i^j\bigr)\ge q_i\eta_i r_i^{m-1}\bigl(r_i-\frac{\psi_i}{1-\rho_i/r_i}\bigr)\ge0$ because $\frac{\psi_i}{r_i-\rho_i}=\frac{\psi_i}{\delta_{\rm inv}-\eta_i+\psi_i}\le1\le r_i$ whenever $\psi_i\le(1-\delta_{\rm inv})$ and $\delta_{\rm inv}\ge\eta_i$ with $r_i^m\ge\psi_i\frac{r_i^m-\rho_i^m}{r_i-\rho_i}$ (since $\frac{\psi_i}{r_i-\rho_i}\le1$). Nonnegative open-loop taps $k_m(\psi)\ge0$ with $a+\kappa\sum_m k_m(\psi)=a+\kappa\Phi_{\rm eff}(\psi)<1$ generate a nonnegative closed-loop renewal sequence $h_t(\psi)\ge0$, so $\|h(\psi)\|_1=\sum_{t\ge0}h_t(\psi)=S(\psi)$.

\paragraph{Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:radius}.} (i) $\sup_t\|CJ^tz_0\|\le\sup_t\|CJ^t\|\|z_0\|$, with equality approached by aligning $z_0$ with the top right singular vector of $CJ^{t^*}$ at the maximizing $t^*$. (ii) The Kreiss matrix theorem with Spijker's sharp constant gives $\mathcal K(J)\le\sup_t\|J^t\|\le en\mathcal K(J)$; substitute in (i). (iii) Take $J_\theta=\begin{psmallmatrix}\rho&\theta\\0&\rho/2\end{psmallmatrix}$; then $\rho(J_\theta)=\rho$ and $(1,0)J_\theta e_2=\theta$, so $M_C\ge|\theta|$ and $r^*_{\rm exact}\le B/|\theta|\to0$.

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:multi}.} With all blocks entrywise nonnegative, every entry of $J_{\rm multi}^t$ is a polynomial in the entries of $K_{21}$ with nonnegative coefficients, so it is non-decreasing in $K_{21}$; with $C\ge0$, so is every entry of $CJ^t$ and hence its norm. For the Kreiss constant, the Neumann series gives $|(zI-J)^{-1}|\le(|z|I-J)^{-1}$ entrywise for $|z|>1$, so the supremum is attained on the real axis, where $(rI-J)^{-1}=\sum_kJ^k/r^{k+1}$ is entrywise non-decreasing in $K_{21}$.

\paragraph{Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:forward}.} After the step, inventory is at most $|I^{\rm post}_{t+1}(\alpha_t)|$ in absolute value. The price move over the step and the cumulative window is bounded by $M_C\|z_t\|_2$ (drift from the current state) plus $s(\alpha)G_{\rm cum}\bar u_{\rm cum}$ (admitted shocks), by superposition and H\"older. The margin requirement scales the loss by $(1+\mu)$. Hedging cost and the post-fill margin increment are deducted directly. Inequality \eqref{p2:eq:guardineq} therefore implies $B_{{\rm rem},t+1}\ge R_{\rm safe}$ for every admissible disturbance, and induction over steps at which an admissible $\alpha$ exists gives forward solvency. Setting $\alpha=0$ ($M_C(0)=1$, no fills, no hedge) gives $\bar u_{\rm adm}$. Sufficiency over the Euclidean ball follows from the norm bounds. When $z_t=0$ the remaining shock term is attained by $u=-{\rm sign}(I)\bar u$, which gives necessity; when $z_t\ne0$ the drift bound $M_C\|z_t\|_2$ need not be attained, so the condition is sufficient but not necessary.

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:driftguard}.} (i) In the positive cohort economy $J(\alpha)\ge0$ entrywise, so for any $z_0=(x_0,y_0^\top)^\top$, $|e_x^\top J(\alpha)^t z_0|\le e_x^\top J(\alpha)^t(|x_0|,|y_0|^\top)^\top\le e_x^\top J(\alpha)^t(|x_0|,\bar y_0\mathbf{1}^\top)^\top$, with equality when $y_0={\rm sign}(x_0)\bar y_0\mathbf{1}$. Under the price-first unforced recursion $x_t=a x_{t-1}+\alpha\kappa\sum_i q_i y_{i,t-1}$ ($t\ge1$) and $y_{i,t}=(1-\eta_i)y_{i,t-1}+\eta_i x_t$ ($t\ge1$) with initial state $(|x_0|,\bar y_0\mathbf{1}^\top)$ in the unit-sum memory coordinate (where the initial cohort state $y_{i,0}$ enters with unit cumulative weight $\sum_{m\ge0}(1-\eta_i)^m\eta_i=1$, i.e.\ $\tilde y_{i,0}=\eta_i y_{i,0}$ bounded by $\eta_i\bar y_0$), summing over $t\ge1$ gives $\sum_{t\ge0}q_i y_{i,t}=q_i\bar y_0+q_i\sum_{t\ge1}x_t$, and hence $(1-a-\alpha\kappa\Phi)\sum_{t\ge1}x_t=a|x_0|+\alpha\kappa\Phi\bar y_0$, proving \eqref{p2:eq:driftenv}. (ii) Adding the forced worst-case shock loss $|I|\frac{\kappa\bar u}{1-a-\alpha\kappa\Phi}$ to $|I|$ times \eqref{p2:eq:driftenv} and requiring the total loss to be at most $\mathcal B$ at $\Phi=\Phi^+$ rearranges to $\alpha\kappa\Phi^+(\mathcal B+|I|\bar y_0)\le(1-a)\mathcal B-|I|(a|x_0|+\kappa\bar u)$, which gives \eqref{p2:eq:alphadrift}. (iii) Subtracting $\alpha_{\rm drift}^*=c_I/[\kappa\Phi^+(1+|I|\bar y_0/\mathcal B)]$ from $\alpha^*=c_I/(\kappa\Phi^+)$ at $x_0=0$ gives \eqref{p2:eq:driftcost}.

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:dominance}.} The eigenvalue rule admits $\alpha_\rho$; the one-step certificate fails at $\alpha_\rho$ exactly when the left side of \eqref{p2:eq:guardineq}, with the shock term replaced by its one-step bound $M_C(\alpha_\rho)s(\alpha_\rho)\bar u_{{\rm step},t}$, exceeds $B_{{\rm rem},t}$, which rearranges to the stated inequality. The summed-gain guard admits only levels satisfying \eqref{p2:eq:guardineq}, so it never admits the breaching level, and by the feasibility assumption it admits at least $\alpha=0$.

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:lucas}.} (i) The steady-state gain is $\eta=P/(P+\sigma_\nu^2)$ with $P$ the positive root of $P^2-\sigma_\zeta^2P-\sigma_\zeta^2\sigma_\nu^2=0$. Since $P$ is strictly increasing in $\sigma_\zeta^2$ and $\eta$ is strictly increasing in $P$, $\eta^*$ is strictly increasing in $\sigma_\zeta^2(\alpha)=\alpha^2\sigma_{\zeta,0}^2$, hence in $\alpha$. The second statement is immediate under the stated monotonicity.

(ii) Write the recursion as a renewal equation: $h_t/\kappa=u_t$ with $u_0=1$ and $u_t=\sum_{j=1}^tp_ju_{t-j}$, where $p_1=a+\kappa k_0$ and $p_j=\kappa k_{j-1}$ for $j\ge2$. The $p_j$ are nonnegative with total mass $\sum_jp_j=a+\kappa\Phi=a+\kappa\sum_iq_i<1$, which does not depend on the adaptation rates, so $u_t$ is the probability that a renewal process whose (defective) inter-arrival law is $p$ has a renewal at time $t$, and $\sum_{t<H}u_t$ is the expected number of renewals in $\{0,\ldots,H-1\}$. The cumulative law is $F(j)=\sum_{i\le j}p_i=a+\kappa\sum_{m<j}k_m=a+\kappa\sum_iq_i\bigl[1-(1-\eta_i)^j\bigr]$, with $\partial F(j)/\partial\eta_i=\kappa q_ij(1-\eta_i)^{j-1}\ge0$. If $\eta'\ge\eta$ coordinatewise, then $F'\ge F$ pointwise with the same total mass, so the quantile coupling $X=F^{-1}(U)$, $X'=F'^{-1}(U)$ ($X=\infty$ when $U>F(\infty)$) of independent inter-arrival times gives $X'\le X$ and hence, pathwise, at least as many renewals before $H$ under $\eta'$. Taking expectations gives $S_H(\eta')\ge S_H(\eta)$. If $q_i>0$ and $\eta'_i>\eta_i$, then $F'(1)>F(1)$, so with positive probability the first renewal occurs at time 1 under $\eta'$ and later under $\eta$, and the inequality is strict for $H\ge2$. As $H\to\infty$ both sides tend to $\kappa/(1-a-\kappa\Phi)$, which does not depend on $\eta$. Combined with (i), throttling lowers every rate, so $S_H(J(\alpha,\eta^*(\alpha)))\le S_H(J(\alpha,\eta^*(1)))$.

\paragraph{Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}.} The recursion $h_{t+1}=ah_t+\kappa\sum_{m\le t}k_mh_{t-m}$ with $h_0=\kappa\neq0$ gives $k_t$ from $h_0,\ldots,h_{t+1}$, so the window determines $k_0,\ldots,k_{n-1}$ and nothing more about $M$: any nonnegative measure on $[0,\bar r]$ with these moments, written as atoms $(r_i,w_i)$ or as a limit of such, is the merged measure of a positive economy with $\eta_i=1-r_i$ and $q_i=w_i/\eta_i$ that produces the same window. (i) The compatible measures form a convex, weak-* compact set, and $\Phi=\int f\,dM$ is linear and continuous on it ($f$ is continuous on $[0,\bar r]$), so its range is a closed interval $[\Phi_-,\Phi_+]$. Since $f^{(n)}(r)=n!/(1-r)^{n+1}>0$ on $[0,\bar r]$, $(1,r,\ldots,r^{n-1},f)$ is an extended complete Chebyshev system, and by the Markov--Krein theorem \citep[Theorems IV.1.1 and IV.6.1]{p2:krein1977} the maximum is attained at the upper and the minimum at the lower principal representation, which have at most $\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1$ atoms. The theorem applies to moment vectors in the interior of the moment space, where both principal representations exist and are unique; a vector on the boundary has exactly one representing measure, so the interval collapses to a point. $S=\kappa/(1-a-\kappa\Phi)$ is increasing in $\Phi$ on $\Phi<(1-a)/\kappa$, which gives the interval of summed responses, unbounded when $\Phi_+\ge(1-a)/\kappa$ because by convexity every $\Phi\in[\Phi_-,\Phi_+]$ is attained. (ii) If $\mu,\nu$ share moments up to order $n-1$, then $\int f\,d(\mu-\nu)=\int(f-p)\,d(\mu-\nu)$ for every polynomial $p$ of degree $n-1$, so $|\int f\,d(\mu-\nu)|\le2k_0\inf_p\|f-p\|_\infty=2k_0E_{n-1}$. With $r=\bar r(1+x)/2$, $f=(2/\bar r)/(x_0-x)$ on $[-1,1]$ with $x_0=2/\bar r-1>1$, and Chebyshev's formula gives $E_m(1/(x_0-x))=\varrho^m/(x_0^2-1)$ \citep{p2:achieser1956}; we checked it against a linear-programming minimax fit for $m\le8$. Expanding $x_0=1+2(1-\bar r)/\bar r$ gives the asymptotics. (iii) Let the moment vector $c$ be interior to the moment cone of $[0,1]$. Interior points have an open neighbourhood in the cone, and $m(r_0)=(1,r_0,\ldots,r_0^{n-1})$ has norm at most $\sqrt n$, so for some $\epsilon>0$, $c-\epsilon m(r_0)$ is interior for every $r_0\in[0,1]$ and is represented by its lower principal representation, which has no atom at $1$. Adding $\epsilon\delta_{r_0}$ gives a measure on $[0,1)$ with moments $c$ and $\Phi\ge\epsilon/(1-r_0)\to\infty$ as $r_0\to1$; by convexity every larger $\Phi$ below $(1-a)/\kappa$ is attained by a stable economy, so $S$ is unbounded.

\paragraph{Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}.} (i) The flow impulse enters the stacked state as $b=\kappa(1,\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_K)^\top$, and $h_t=e_x^\top J^tb\le\|J^tb\|_\infty\le\|J\|_\infty^t\|b\|_\infty$ because $J\ge0$. The price row of $J$ sums to $a+\kappa\Phi\le\bar s$ and cohort row $i$ to $1-\eta_i(1-a-\kappa\Phi)\le1-(1-\bar r)(1-\bar s)=\bar\lambda$, so $h_t\le\kappa\bar\lambda^t$ and $\sum_{t\ge T}h_t\le\kappa\bar\lambda^T/(1-\bar\lambda)$. $\sum_{t<T}\bar h_t$ is Gaussian with mean $\sum_{t<T}h_t$ and variance $T\sigma^2/N$, so $\sum_{t<T}\bar h_t+z_{1-\gamma}\sigma\sqrt{T/N}\ge\sum_{t<T}h_t$ with probability $1-\gamma$, and adding the tail bound gives validity. On the two-sided event of probability $1-2\gamma$, $\hat S^+-S\le2z_{1-\gamma}\sigma\sqrt{T/N}+\kappa\bar\lambda^T/(1-\bar\lambda)$; $T_\epsilon$ makes the second term at most $\epsilon/2$ and the stated $N$ makes the first at most $\epsilon/2$. (ii) Let $\theta_1$ raise the cohort weight of $\theta_0$ by $\Delta$ chosen so that $S_1-S_0=2\epsilon$; then $\|h_1-h_0\|_2=2C_0\epsilon(1+o(1))$. Validity at $\theta_1$ gives $P_1(\hat S\ge S_1)\ge1-\gamma$, hence $P_0(\hat S-S_0\ge2\epsilon)\ge1-\gamma-{\rm TV}(P_0,P_1)$. For $N$ independent Gaussian responses, ${\rm KL}=N\|h_1-h_0\|_2^2/(2\sigma^2)$, and Pinsker's inequality gives ${\rm TV}\le\sqrt N\,C_0\epsilon(1+o(1))/\sigma$. An overshoot probability below $1/2$ therefore needs ${\rm TV}>1/2-\gamma$, which is the stated bound. (iv) Write $M=\sum_iq_i(1-r_i)\delta_{r_i}$ for the merged measure, $k_m=\int r^m dM$ for the open-loop kernel and $\mathcal M_0$ for the measures on $[0,\bar r]$ of mass at most $B_0=(1-\bar r)(\bar s-a)/\kappa$; economies with merged measure in $\mathcal M_0$ lie in the class, and finite-atom measures are weak-$*$ dense in $\mathcal M_0$. \emph{Step 1 (closed loop to open loop).} The closed-loop transforms satisfy $H=\kappa/(1-az-\kappa zK)$, so $H_1-H_0=\kappa^{-1}zH_1H_0(K_1-K_0)$ and, by Young's inequality, $\|h_1-h_0\|_2\le\kappa^{-1}S_1S_0\|k_1-k_0\|_2\le C_1\|k_1-k_0\|_2$ on the class, for any number of recorded responses. \emph{Step 2 (two points).} If two economies have $S_1>S_0+\epsilon$, validity at the first and the overshoot bound at the second make $\{\hat S^+\le S_0+\epsilon\}$ have probability at least $1-\gamma$ under $P_0$ and at most $\gamma$ under $P_1$, so the total variation is at least $1-2\gamma$; for Gaussian responses this needs $\|h_1-h_0\|_2\ge2z_{1-\gamma}\sigma/\sqrt N$. Hence $\omega_S(2z_{1-\gamma}\sigma/(C_1\sqrt N))\le\epsilon$, where $\omega_S(\delta)$ is the largest difference in $S$ between two economies with $\|k_1-k_0\|_2\le\delta$. Since $\partial S/\partial\Phi=\kappa^2/(1-a-\kappa\Phi)^2\ge\kappa^2$, $\omega_S\ge\kappa^2\omega_\Phi$. \emph{Step 3 (duality).} For a depth $L$, $\omega_\Phi(\delta)$ over $\mathcal M_0$ with the first $L$ kernel values equals $\min_{c\in\mathbb R^L}\{\delta\|c\|_2+B_0\psi(g-p_c)\}$, with $g(r)=1/(1-r)$, $p_c(r)=\sum_{t<L}c_tr^t$ and $\psi(h)=(\sup h)_++(-\inf h)_+$ on $[0,\bar r]$: the inequality $\le$ is direct, and equality follows from Sion's minimax theorem because $\mathcal M_0^2$ is weak-$*$ compact \citep{p2:donoho1994}; the modulus for all kernel values is the limit as $L\to\infty$, by compactness. \emph{Step 4 (a pole forces large coefficients).} If $\|c\|_2\le R$ and $\eta=\sup_{[0,\bar r]}|g-p_c|$, then $\log(1/\eta)\le K_0\max\{R^2,(1-\bar r)^{-1}\}$ with $K_0=8\log6\sqrt{1-\bar r}\log\rho_*$, $\rho_*=(1+\sqrt{1-\bar r})^2/\bar r$. Indeed $e=g-p_c$ is analytic on the disc of radius $\rho=1-s/2$, $s=\min\{1/(4R^2),(1-\bar r)/4\}$, with $|e(1-s)|\ge1/(2s)$ and $|e|\le3/s$ on $|z|=\rho$ because $|p_c(z)|\le R(1-|z|)^{-1/2}$; the two-constants theorem on the disc slit along $[0,\bar r]$, with the harmonic measure of the slit bounded below by an annulus comparison through the Joukowski map, gives the claim. \emph{Step 5.} For every $c$, either $\eta\ge\delta\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}$, or $\log(1/\eta)>\tfrac12\log(1/\delta)$ and Step 4 forces $\|c\|_2\ge\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/(2K_0)}$ once $\log(1/\delta)\ge2K_0/(1-\bar r)$; so $\omega_\Phi(\delta)\ge\min\{B_0,(2K_0)^{-1/2}\}\delta\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}$ for small $\delta$, and Step 2 gives $N\ge c\,\sigma^2\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\epsilon)$. (v) The identification rates are Chapter~\ref{ch:p1}'s Theorems on the $K$-cohort lower bound, the matching upper bound and general memory.

\paragraph{Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:sound}.} On the event that $\mathcal C(D)$ contains the true economy, the supremum over $\mathcal C(D)$ dominates the true worst-case loss for every action, including one selected from $D$; the event has probability at least $1-\gamma$.

\paragraph{Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robustguard}.} (i) For $c_I\ge0$, $\alpha\mapsto S(\alpha)=\kappa/(1-a-\kappa\alpha\Phi)$ is increasing, and so is the worst-case loss $|I|\bar uS(\alpha)$, which is also increasing in $\Phi$; the robust constraint is therefore the constraint at $\Phi^+$, and $|I|\bar u\kappa/(1-a-\kappa\alpha\Phi^+)\le\mathcal B$ is equivalent to $\alpha\le c_I/(\kappa\Phi^+)$. (ii) is Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:sound}. (iii) At $\Phi=\Phi^+$ the constraint binds at $\alpha^*$ when $\alpha^*<1$. (iv) At $\alpha=0$, $S=\kappa/(1-a)$. (v) Upper: $\alpha^*$ is Lipschitz in $\Phi^+$ with constant $c_I/(\kappa\Phi_{\min}^2)$ and $\Phi^+$ is Lipschitz in $\hat S^+$ with constant $1/S_{\min}^2$, so Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}(i) with $T\asymp\log N$ gives the rate. Lower: take two single-cohort economies in the class whose weights differ by $\Delta=c\,\sigma/\sqrt N$; their oracle capacities differ by a constant times $\Delta$, and ${\rm TV}\le\sqrt N\|\partial h/\partial q\|_2\Delta/(2\sigma)$, which is below $(1-\beta)/2$ for small $c$; Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:twoworld} gives an expected loss of at least $(1-\beta)/2$ times the capacity difference.

\paragraph{Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:disclosure}.} (i) If $O'$ reveals everything $O$ reveals, every economy compatible with $O'$ data is compatible with $O$ data, so suprema over $\Theta_{O'}$ are at most those over $\Theta_O$, and Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:robustguard}(i) is monotone in $\Phi^+$. (ii) $\alpha^*$ is strictly decreasing in $\Phi^+$ when $c_I>0$ and $\alpha^*<1$, and $\Phi^+_{O'}<\Phi^+_O$ exactly when no economy with $\Phi=\Phi^+_O$ remains compatible (the supremum is attained, by Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}(i)). (iii) follows from Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:window}(iii). (iv) If $O$ identifies $\Phi$, $\Phi^+_O=\Phi$ and no contract can raise $\alpha^*$. (v) The delta method applied to $(w,r)\mapsto w/(1-r)$, whose gradient is $(1-r)^{-1}(1,\Phi_i)$, gives the standard error. If each certificate is valid at level $1-\gamma/2$, their minimum is valid at level $1-\gamma$ by the union bound and is no larger than either, so the admitted level is at least the larger of the two admitted levels.

\paragraph{Lemma~\ref{p2:lem:certnum}.} For a compatible merged measure $M$ on $[0,\bar r]$, $\int p\,dM=\sum_jc_jk_j$ for any polynomial of degree at most $n-1$, so $p_-\le f\le p_+$ gives the two bounds by integration. Let $H$ interpolate $f$ at the atoms $x_i$ of $\mu_+$ with multiplicity $m_i=2$ at interior atoms and $1$ at $0$ and $\bar r$; principal representations have index $\sum_im_i=n$, so $H$ has degree at most $n-1$, and the Hermite remainder is $f(r)-H(r)=f^{(n)}(\xi)\,\omega(r)/n!$ with $\omega(r)=\prod_i(r-x_i)^{m_i}$. Since $f^{(n)}>0$ and, on $[0,\bar r]$, the factor $r-\bar r\le0$ appears once while every other factor is a square or $r\ge0$, $\omega\le0$ and $H\ge f$. For $\mu_-$ there is no atom at $\bar r$, so $\omega\ge0$ and $H\le f$. As $H=f$ on the support of $\mu_\pm$, $\sum_jc_jk_j=\int H\,d\mu_\pm=\int f\,d\mu_\pm=\Phi_\pm$. The certified computation proves $p_++s\ge f$ (or $p_--s\le f$) for the rounded polynomial and slack $s$ in interval arithmetic, in which every operation encloses its exact result \citep{p2:rump2010}, so the first statement applies to the rounded polynomial with $c_0$ increased (decreased) by $s$.

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:hotelling}.} (i) The $X_e$ are i.i.d. normal with mean $S_T=\sum_{t<T}h_t$, whatever $\Sigma$, so $\sqrt N(\bar X-S_T)/s_X$ has Student's $t_{N-1}$ distribution and $P(S_T\le\bar X+t_{N-1,1-\gamma}s_X/\sqrt N)=1-\gamma$; the tail bound of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost}(i) holds deterministically on the class. (ii) $N(\bar h-m)^\top\hat\Sigma^{-1}(\bar h-m)$ has the $\frac{T(N-1)}{N-T}F_{T,N-T}$ distribution \citep{p2:hotelling1931}; on the event that it is at most $T^2_\gamma$, Cauchy--Schwarz in the $\hat\Sigma$ inner product gives $c^\top(m-\bar h)\le\sqrt{c^\top\hat\Sigma c}\,\sqrt{(\bar h-m)^\top\hat\Sigma^{-1}(\bar h-m)}\le\sqrt{T^2_\gamma c^\top\hat\Sigma c/N}$ for every $c$ at once. (iii) If the taps of an experiment have common variance $\sigma^2$ and correlation matrix $R$, the margin $z_{1-\gamma}\sigma\sqrt{T/N}$ has coverage $\Phi\bigl(z_{1-\gamma}\sqrt{T/\mathbf 1^\top R\mathbf 1}\bigr)$, below $1-\gamma$ whenever $\mathbf 1^\top R\mathbf 1>T$; with $T=12$ and autocorrelation $0.8$ it is $0.751$, matching the simulations.

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:venue}.} (i) $D$ is a quadratic with leading coefficient $\delta\rho\ge0$, and a level is safe if and only if $D(\alpha)\ge\kappa_0|I|\bar u/\mathcal B$ (which implies $D>0$, hence stability and $S=\kappa_0/D$). The set where a convex function is below a constant is an interval, so the safe set is $[0,1]$ minus an open interval; the example in the text has two components. (ii) For $\alpha\ge0$ and $a_0<1$, $\partial D/\partial\delta=\alpha(1-a_0+\rho\alpha)\ge0$, $\partial D/\partial\rho=\alpha(1+\delta\alpha)\ge0$, $\partial D/\partial a_0=-(1+\delta\alpha)\le0$ and $\partial D/\partial\Phi=-\kappa_0\alpha\le0$, while the condition $D-\kappa_0|I|\bar u/\mathcal B\ge0$ decreases in $\kappa_0$. The minimum of the left side over the box is therefore at the stated corner for every $\alpha$. (iii) follows from (i) and (ii).

\paragraph{Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:venuebb}.} (i) In interval arithmetic with outward rounding every operation returns an interval containing the exact result for all real arguments in its operands \citep{p2:rump2010}; by induction the computed interval vectors contain $J(\alpha,\theta)^ts_0(\alpha,\theta)$ and the interval matrices contain $J^j$ for every $(\alpha,\theta)$ in the box, so the computed sum contains $\sum_{t<T}|h_t|$. For $t\ge T$, writing $t=T+kT+j$ with $k\ge0$ and $0\le j<T$, $|h_t|\le\|J^{kT+j}J^Ts_0\|_\infty\le\|J^T\|_\infty^k\|J^j\|_\infty\|J^Ts_0\|_\infty$, and summing gives the tail bound. A level interval classified safe has an upper enclosure within budget over every part of a partition of $\Theta$, hence for every $\theta$; one classified unsafe has $S(\alpha,\theta_j)\ge\sum_{t<T}|h_t(\alpha,\theta_j)|>$ budget for some $\theta_j\in\Theta$ and every level in it. (ii) Each $S(\cdot,\theta)$ is continuous where finite and tends to infinity at the stability boundary, so $\alpha\mapsto\sup_\Theta S(\alpha,\theta)$ is lower semicontinuous and its sublevel set, the robust safe set, is closed; the brackets follow from (i). (iii) is immediate. (iv) Under $\sup_\Theta\rho(J)<1$ there is $T$ with $\|J^T\|_\infty<1$ uniformly on $\Theta$; interval extensions of rational functions converge linearly in the box width \citep{p2:hansen2004}, so at a level where the robust risk differs from the budget by $g>0$ the enclosures over small enough boxes, with $T$ large enough that the truncation is below $g/2$, decide.

\paragraph{Proposition~\ref{p2:prop:probe}.} With $D_n=\sum_{e\le n}(X_e-S_T)$ and $V_n=n\sigma_T^2$, $\exp(\lambda D_n-\lambda^2V_n/2)$ is a nonnegative supermartingale for every $\lambda$ under sub-Gaussian increments; mixing over $\lambda\sim N(0,1/\varrho)$ gives the nonnegative supermartingale $M_n=\sqrt{\varrho/(V_n+\varrho)}\exp\bigl(D_n^2/(2(V_n+\varrho))\bigr)$ with $M_0=1$. Ville's inequality \citep{p2:ville1939} gives $P(\exists n:M_n\ge1/\gamma)\le\gamma$, and $M_n\ge1/\gamma$ is exactly $|D_n|\ge b_n$. On the complementary event every interval covers $S_T$ at every $n$, so a decision made at any stopping time is correct; the tail bound is deterministic. Since $b_n/n=O\bigl(\sigma_T\sqrt{\log(n/\gamma)/n}\bigr)$, the stopping time is $O\bigl(\sigma_T^2\Delta^{-2}\log(\sigma_T/(\Delta\gamma))\bigr)$ when the gap is $\Delta$ \citep{p2:howard2021}.

\section{Appendix: Notation}\label{p2:app:notation}
\begin{longtable}{p{0.2\textwidth}p{0.7\textwidth}}
\toprule
Symbol & Meaning\\
\midrule
\endhead
$x_t,\ d_t,\ u_t$ & price displacement, depth imbalance $(B-A)/(B+A)$, signed flow\\
$a,\ \kappa$ & price persistence and direct impact\\
$\varphi,\ \varepsilon,\ r$ & price sensitivity to depth, depletion per unit of flow, refill rate\\
$\eta,\ q$ & momentum memory rate and aggressiveness\\
$C_2,\ C_3$ & amplification statistics $\varphi\varepsilon-(1-a)\kappa$ and $C_2+\kappa^2\eta q$\\
$h_k,\ g_k$ & impulse response of the book; price impulse coefficient of the controlled system\\
$J(\alpha)$ & closed-loop operator at admission level $\alpha$\\
$M_C,\ G_{\rm cum}$ & peak and summed price-row gains\\
$\mathcal K(J)$ & Kreiss constant\\
$\bar u,\ \bar u_{\rm step},\ \bar u_{\rm cum}$ & shock envelopes\\
$B_{\rm rem},\ R_{\rm safe},\ \mu$ & remaining margin buffer, safety reserve, maintenance-margin rate\\
$\epsilon,\ S$ & operator confidence radius and $\sum_k\|J^k\|$\\
$M_{\rm std},\ A_{xx}$ & unit-free and price-to-price spot amplification statistics\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

\section{Appendix: Further results}\label{p2:app:ia}

\subsection{Specification of the pre-registered tests}
\paragraph{Futures (Table~\ref{p2:tab:d8}).} Question: does $\hat C_3$, estimated from the previous six hours of futures depth, flow and price, predict the next hour's MAE beyond controls? Unit: hourly blocks 06--23 UTC, features from $[t-360,t)$ minutes, outcomes from $(t,t+60]$. Controls: $rv_{60}$, $rv_{360}$, $|r_{60}|$, flow imbalance, log volume, log depth, imbalance, open-interest change. Model: OLS; tail is the outcome above the training 90th percentile; score is the fitted value. Inference: Clark--West with Newey--West (6 lags); day-block bootstrap with 2,000 draws. Secondary: peak ratio as candidate; open-interest drop and depth collapse as outcomes; Holm. Train $\le$ 2024-12-31; test $\ge$ 2025-01-01. Hash \texttt{d95212187ae42052591a5ca5acf73317} \texttt{0f217f430a5a6ca812da6e48a1e4af85}; one unlock.

\paragraph{Spot (Section~\ref{p2:sec:spot}).} Question: does pre-window $A_{xx}$ predict the next ten minutes' MAE beyond volatility and flow controls? Unit: non-overlapping 30-minute blocks; features from the previous ten minutes. Controls: $rv_{10m}$, $rv_{60m}$, $|r_{10m}|$, flow imbalance, log volume, spectral radius, hour of day. Train 2021--2023; test 2024. Hash \texttt{b1fd7f8eef15df1ba295eb6d32b53be6} \texttt{c23ea2d23c5216de912555e62716e5ae}; three unlocks (see text).

\subsection{Adaptive safe probing}\label{p2:app:probe}
The certificate of Theorem~\ref{p2:thm:certcost} fixes the number of reset experiments in advance, so it must be sized for the hardest economy the supervisor wants to decide. Experiments can instead be run one at a time and stopped as soon as the data decide. Let each experiment return $X_e=\sum_{t<T}h^{(e)}_t$, independent with mean $S_T$ and sub-Gaussian proxy $\sigma_T^2$ ($\sigma_T^2=T\sigma^2$ under (O1)), and let $b_n=\sqrt{(n\sigma_T^2+\varrho)\bigl(\log((n\sigma_T^2+\varrho)/\varrho)+2\log(1/\gamma)\bigr)}$ for a fixed $\varrho>0$.

\begin{proposition}[Anytime-valid probing]\label{p2:prop:probe}
With probability at least $1-\gamma$, $|\bar X_n-S_T|<b_n/n$ for every $n$ simultaneously. Hence the rule that probes until $\bar X_n+b_n/n+\kappa\bar\lambda^T/(1-\bar\lambda)\le s_{\mathcal B}$ (admit), or $\bar X_n-b_n/n>s_{\mathcal B}$ (refuse), or a maximum number of probes is reached (abstain), makes a wrong decision with probability at most $\gamma$ whatever its stopping time, where $s_{\mathcal B}=\mathcal B/(|I|\bar u)$. When $S_T$ is at distance $\Delta$ from $s_{\mathcal B}$ it stops after $O\bigl(\sigma_T^2\Delta^{-2}\log(\sigma_T/(\Delta\gamma))\bigr)$ probes. Each probe is itself admissible if its position $v_p$ satisfies $|v_p|\bar u_p\kappa/(1-\bar\lambda)\le\mathcal B_p$, the worst case over the class.
\end{proposition}

The value is in adapting to the economy. With $\sigma_T=0.17$ and a threshold of $2.0$, the sequential rule decides economies at distance $0.5$ from the threshold after 3.7 probes on average, at distance $0.2$ after 11, at $0.1$ after 30 and at $0.05$ after 107, with no wrong decision in 7{,}000 runs, while a fixed design that must decide at distance $0.05$ uses 130 probes for every economy (test T14). Probes have a monetary cost and move prices, so a supervisor who can probe sequentially spends where the decision is hard. This is an extension rather than a result the paper relies on; optimal probe design under impact costs is left open.
